Computation and Language 100
☆ Direct Pass@$k$ Optimization for Thought Pruning: Balancing Quality and Diversity in Test-Time Reasoning
Mahdi Salmani, Adel Javanmard, Devansh Gupta, Meisam Razaviyayn
Test-time scaling has emerged as a key inference strategy for reasoning models, where a model generates a large number of candidate solutions in the hope of finding at least one correct solution. Running all candidate solutions to completion can be computationally demanding, so recent work prunes the candidate pool after generating only a short prefix, committing further computation only to the surviving candidates. Existing pruning strategies divide broadly into two categories: Quality-based methods assign a score to each partial solution and discard those predicted to ultimately lead to an incorrect final solution, while diversity-based methods select partial solutions that follow different reasoning trajectories and are therefore likely to cover different final solutions. The tension between these two objectives is an instance of exploration versus exploitation, yet little to no prior work on pruning exposes this trade-off directly, let alone optimizes it. In this paper, we introduce a pruning procedure that addresses this trade-off directly by optimizing pass@$k$ over the selected solution pool. In particular, it chooses the pool to maximize the probability that at least one of its partial solutions, when continued to completion, yields a correct answer. Because this objective simultaneously rewards high-quality partial solutions and penalizes redundancy among them, quality and diversity are naturally balanced by the criterion itself rather than through separate ad-hoc design choices. On eight open-weight reasoning models, our method outperforms existing quality-based and diversity-based pruning strategies. The gains are particularly pronounced when the pruning procedure keeps a moderate number of partial solutions for continued generation, highlighting the value of directly balancing quality and diversity during pruning.
☆ Cost-Aware Abstention for LLM-Based IT Ticket Classification ICTAI 2026
Sachin Gupta
Wrong automatic routing of an IT support ticket can cost more than human triage. We evaluate cost-aware abstention as an offline, cost-conditional readiness assessment: select a routing policy on calibration data under explicit costs, then evaluate that frozen policy once on held-out test data. On a public 47,837-ticket, eight-class corpus, we compare four trained non-LLM classifiers with four large language models under zero- and few-shot prompting. Under the illustrative cost model (c_ok, c_triage, c_wrong) = (0, 1, 5), TF-IDF with logistic regression and a threshold of 0.60 selected on calibration reduces test cost from 0.763 to 0.472, a 38.1% relative reduction (paired ratio-bootstrap 95% CI [35.7%, 40.4%]), while lowering wrong routes from 15.3% to 3.5%. Frozen calibration-selected policies also beat both always-route and always-defer on held-out test data in 14 of 16 settings in a post-hoc cost-parameter analysis. The strongest prompted configuration reaches 0.477 accuracy on a stratified 1,000-ticket test subset, versus 0.879 for fine-tuned DistilBERT on the same tickets. Isotonic lowers expected calibration error without reliably improving discrimination: GPT-4o-mini few-shot has one confidence value before and after recalibration and correctness AUROC 0.500. We therefore report correctness AUROC and tie-aware risk-coverage area alongside ECE. At the analytically derived break-even probability 0.80, seven prompted configurations defer all tickets; Haiku few-shot routes only 19/1,000. An LLM-only agreement control and margin/entropy rejection baselines further separate calibration from discrimination. The findings are scoped to this dataset, prompt design, model endpoints, and illustrative costs; the work is an evaluation framework, not a new abstention algorithm or universal deployment certification.

☆ Low-Bit N-Gram Embeddings for Language Models
Stephen Chung, Wenyu Du
Many recent open-weight language models use n-gram embedding tables to improve performance. These tables store learned representations of short token sequences, such as pairs or triples of consecutive tokens, which the model retrieves during processing. However, they can require substantial memory: DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, for example, allocates 196 billion parameters to its Engram n-gram memory. We therefore investigate whether this memory cost can be reduced while preserving the performance improvement that the tables provide. We propose storing n-gram embeddings at low precision and systematically investigate different quantization methods and bit allocations across tables. Across nanochat-, Llama-, and GPT-2-family models trained from scratch, four-bit tables save approximately 75% of table memory while preserving more than 99% of the validation-loss improvement from adding the tables. Using fewer bits leads to a larger drop in performance, although we find that a short fine-tuning phase recovers a significant part of this loss. In addition, reinvesting the saved memory in larger tables can improve performance beyond that of the original full-precision tables while still using less memory. These table memory savings also extend to large existing models: on DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash and Qwen3.8-Flash-Next, low-bit tables save 47-55% of table memory, with perplexity increases of less than 1.2% and largely preserved downstream task performance. These findings establish low-bit precision as a practical way to reduce the memory requirements of n-gram embeddings and use the saved capacity to improve model performance.

☆ Can an Agent Debug a Web Corpus? Agentic Auditing of Slovak FineWeb2 EMNLP 2026
Marek Šuppa, Ivan Vykopal, Jaroslav Kopčan
Multilingual pipelines such as FineWeb2 apply one recipe across a thousand languages, leaving the failure modes of individual languages unanticipated. We use an LLM once, as a corpus debugger rather than a classifier: a tool-using agent explores the corpus through a read-only, budgeted API and compiles each finding into an executable test, which a native speaker then validates. On the 26.5M-document Slovak portion of FineWeb2, three runs costing \$2.21-\$2.84 each found seven findings over four phenomena. The largest is a machine-generated content farm of 1,408,437 documents (5.32%) that passed the pipeline's filters, 86.6% of it passing even stricter thresholds. Blinded labels support 6 of the 7 findings (defect rates .53-1.00 against .08 in a random control) and do not support one. The compiled suite scans the full corpus in 0.7 s of wall-clock time without a GPU, against \$12k-\$90k per LLM classification pass. The best static sampling baseline validates more selectors than any agent run, at under half the cost. The agent contributes exact prevalence, a precision estimate checked against sampled documents, an account of why the pipeline missed each slice, and a reusable test. We release the framework, Slovak test suite, validation data and agent trajectories.

☆ Bilingual Language Models Capture Cross-Linguistic Influence in Second-Language Reading
Abderrahmane Issam, Irene E. Winther, Stefan L. Frank
While monolingual language model (LM) surprisal is widely established as a predictor of reading times, it cannot capture the non-native (L2) reading dynamics that are shaped by the reader's first language (L1). In this work, we investigate whether the cross-lingual transfer within bilingual models aligns with human L1-driven biases during L2 reading. We evaluate bilingual models on eye-tracking data of readers from 12 diverse L1 backgrounds reading English texts. To isolate L1-specific transfer, we compare bilingual models trained on an L1 that matches the readers' L1 background to both a monolingual English baseline and bilingual models trained on other L1s. We demonstrate that matching the readers' L1 improves the prediction of L2 reading times. This predictive advantage is concentrated in late-stage eye-tracking measures, modulated by L1-L2 script overlap, and peaks among intermediate-proficiency readers. These findings provide evidence that bilingual LMs capture aspects of cross-linguistic influence, and that such influence is present in L2 reading behavior.

☆ How Ground Truth Shapes Retrieval Scores: Evidence-Aware Evaluation of a Retrieval-Augmented Generation System for Breast Lesions
Miguel Mochizuki Silva, Samuel Ismael, Mateus C. Dias, Thaís Gaudêncio, Yuri Malheiros, Barufaldi
Purpose: To describe an evidence-aware retrieval evaluation protocol for a clinical retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) system over breast lesion management literature, addressing the dependence of retrieval scores on annotation coverage. Methods: An 18-document corpus and an 18-query development benchmark, with one Yes/No or WH-question per document, were used. Retrieval combined dense embeddings, BM25, and cross-encoder reranking. Chunk sizes of 128 to 1024 tokens, overlaps of 0 to 20%, and four retriever configurations were evaluated. Relevance was defined by evidence spans mapped to all containing chunks, treating overlapping chunks with equivalent evidence as relevant. Retrieval was evaluated using R-Precision, Recall@K, MRR, and nDCG@K. Separately, generation was assessed on a 512-token index at K=1 to 5 using DeepEval claim-level metrics, with deepseek-v4-pro as the LLM judge, and BLEU/ROUGE metrics. Results: The selected hybrid retriever, using 384-token chunks and 20% overlap, retrieved accepted evidence in the top three results for 17 of 18 queries. Recall@3, R-Precision, MRR@3, and nDCG@3 each reached 0.94. Ground-truth curation increased evidence spans from 25 to 37 and accepted chunk targets from 36 to 51 without changing retrieval rankings. Conclusion: Cross-encoder reranking achieved the highest retrieval scores. Expanded relevance annotations increased recognized evidence without changing rankings, highlighting annotation sensitivity. The small development set and retrieval-informed curation require independent validation, including radiologist assessment of evidence sufficiency.

☆ Would an LLM Match into a Residency? Uncertainty, Reasoning and Image Use of Open Language Models on the Spanish MIR Examination
Miguel Fernandez-de-Retana, Unai Zulaika, Ruben Sanchez-Corcuera, Aritz Bilbao-Jayo, Aitor Almeida
Accuracy on medical examinations is common evidence offered for the clinical use of language models, yet a health system that intends to run an open model on its own hardware needs to know considerably more: whether the model recognizes when it is likely to be wrong, whether medical fine-tuning improves this ability, whether its correct answers rest on sound clinical reasoning, or whether it actually uses the images it is given. We examine these questions for four open models of 8 to 27 billion parameters on the Spanish MIR residency examination, scoring 1,552 questions from eight editions under the exam's own rules. Answering every question, the three larger models would rank among roughly the first 600 to 3,000 candidates, with 41 to 43 specialties still open to them. Abstention, however, never pays, because even their least confident answers are right more often than the 25% break-even. Medical fine-tuning reduced overconfidence at the cost of accuracy, without improving how well confidence separates right from wrong answers. Moreover, 13-38% of correct answers rejected a distractor for a clinically unsound reason that confidence did not reflect, and removing or swapping the provided contextual images left accuracy unchanged.

☆ Diffusion Language Models for Perplexity-Aware Black-Box Attacks
Ali Al-Lawati, Elsayed Eshra, Dongwon Lee, Suhang Wang
\emph{Continuous-space} attacks find prompts that trigger malicious behaviors, such as overthinking, in black-box LLMs by sampling or optimizing over a low-dimensional Gaussian latent space and decoding it to text through a \emph{random mapping} and nearest-token lookup. Because the random mapping is not learned from language, its prompts can be highly unnatural, and as such easily detectable through input guardrails, and the risk estimates they yield are not grounded in natural prompts. In this paper, we demonstrate that the same continuous latent-space framework can be coupled directly to a learned generative distribution over language, a continuous diffusion language model (dLLM), which removes the artificial random mapping while preserving the standard-Gaussian latent formulation and the downstream sampling or optimization procedure. We evaluate this approach under two settings: sampling-based estimation using \emph{RareTrap} and optimization-based search using \emph{ThinkTrap}. We find that character-level perplexity drops from $10^3$--$10^7$ to 3--5 in RareTrap and from 20--28 to 4.3--5.4 in ThinkTrap. We also find that model risk estimates can differ by an order of magnitude across the two mappings.
☆ An Empirical Study of Recent Large Language Models for Knowledge Graph to Text Generation on Standard Benchmarks
Priyanka, Renaud Delbru, Souhail Bakkali, Mickaël Coustaty, Antoine Doucet
Knowledge graph-to-text (KG-to-text) generation verbalizes a set of knowledge graph triples into fluent natural language. While fine-tuned encoder-decoder models have long been the standard approach, recent LLMs are becoming increasingly competitive. We benchmark recent LLMs against fine-tuned encoder-decoder models, using reference-based metrics and graph-grounded reference-free LLM-based evaluation validated against human annotations. We observed that recent LLMs substantially narrow, and in several settings close, the gap with fine-tuned models on reference-based metrics, while performing better on graph-grounded metrics. LLM-based evaluation scores show limited correlation with reference-based metrics, suggesting that the two approaches capture different aspects of generation quality. Graph-grounded evaluation reveals that prompted models produce fewer contradictions and triple omissions than fine-tuned models, and these errors increase more rapidly with graph size for fine-tuned models, highlighting differences between the two approaches that reference-based metrics may not fully capture.

☆ The Geometry of Metaphor: Probing and Steering Metaphor Representations in Large Language Models
Shihui Li, Mette Andersen, Martha Lewis
Metaphor is a crucial aspect of human cognition. As such, we would like to understand how it is represented in large language models (LLMs) and how it might be controlled. We use mechanistic interpretability techniques to identify causal subspaces in a range of LLMs covering different architectures and sizes. We show that we are able to steer output on classification tasks across different parts of speech. We are also able to steer output on generation tasks, indicating that these techniques can be used to control the metaphoricity of LLM output.

☆ Towards User Simulators That Stay On Task And Still Sound Natural
Mustafa Omer Gul, Philippe Laban, Tanya Goyal, Jennifer Neville
Users increasingly rely on AI systems for extended, multi-turn tasks, yet evidence indicates that LLM-based system performance degrades over prolonged interaction. To evaluate and improve AI assistants in such settings, we need user simulators that faithfully follow complex user intents while exhibiting realistic conversational behavior. Existing approaches manifest a trade-off: prompted assistants can follow complex user intents but lack behavioral fidelity, while specialized user simulators better approximate conversational behavior but struggle to remain faithful to complex intents. In this paper, we propose a reinforcement learning (RL) recipe that jointly rewards task intent adherence and behavioral naturalness. A crucial contribution of the method is operationalizing naturalness with a lightweight discriminator trained in an alternating, adversarial loop with the user simulator. We release the resulting model, UserLM-2.0, which produces valid simulated conversations (i.e., meeting both task-faithfulness and naturalness criteria) 46.2% of the time, over three times the rate of the strongest prior user simulator (14.1%).

☆ Measuring the Verifier's Floor: How Much Apparent Rare-Word Text-to-Speech Error Is ASR Error? ICASSP 2027
Kirill Borodin, Vasilii Kudryavtsev, Nikita Vasiliev, Grach Mkrtchian
Text-to-speech (TTS) is usually evaluated by transcribing its output with an automatic speech recognition (ASR) system, so the word error rate also counts recognizer errors. We measure these errors on human recordings of the test sentences. On LibriSpeech and FLEURS, the evaluation recognizer misses 33-67% of very rare words even in human speech, and all three recognizers miss 22-42%. Checking every word with two more recognizer families lowers the apparent TTS error on words that at least one recognizer hears two- to three-fold (very rare LibriSpeech words: from 10-13% to 3-5%), yet very rare words remain the hardest. Ignoring words the recognizer misses in human speech is biased: the other recognizers find TTS errors far more often on these words (4-6% vs. 0.2-0.3%) and overturn about half of the errors this mask keeps. As half of the remaining adjudicated errors may be spelling variants, a listening test is needed.
comment: Submitted to IEEE ICASSP 2027
☆ From Structured Records to Patient Narratives: Preserving Clinical Alignment in LLM-Generated Health Summaries
Bayram Ayadi, Florian Geissler, Johanna Schmidhuber
As wearable and digital health systems increasingly generate longitudinal physiological records, communicating these observations in patient-accessible language remains a significant challenge. Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a promising mechanism for translating structured clinical information into readable narratives, but this process may introduce semantic drift, where clinically precise meaning is distorted during simplification. We investigate evidence-grounded health summarization for menstrual health monitoring using a constrained generation framework that combines structured evidence conditioning with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) using a prompt-based constrained generation framework. We evaluate semantic alignment using Natural Language Inference (NLI), atomic factuality metrics, and readability measures. Across three LLMs and 4,500 generated summaries, explicit grounding constraints consistently improve clinical alignment, particularly by improving uncertainty preservation and, once faithfulness is evaluated against retrieved evidence, generally preserving or improving faithfulness, while effects on omission remain model-dependent. We also find that improved clinical alignment comes at the cost of increased reading difficulty and medical terminology. These findings highlight the need to jointly evaluate faithfulness, uncertainty preservation, and readability when developing patient-facing medical LLM systems.

☆ How Different, or Different How? Stance Granularity in Viewpoint Selection for Dialog Systems that Engage Users with Alternative Views
Morita Tarvirdians, Reeve Lorena, Catharine Oertel
Systems that aim to help a user engage with viewpoints other than their own have to decide how different those viewpoints should be. Viewpoints are mainly represented on a scalar scale, too coarse to distinguish viewpoints that disagree with the user about different things. We introduce a selection policy in which a bandit sets how far viewpoints may sit from the user, and the policy selects the most novel viewpoint within that bound. We vary only its stance encoder, comparing a scalar score with Perspectivized Stance Vectors, which break a position into separate stances on concepts. We integrate it inside a dialog system and run a between-subjects study (N=63). Concept-level stance produces more varied exposure sequences and broader coverage of the topic's positions, but does not change how deeply users justify viewpoints or how accurately they reconstruct them. Finally, we analyze user--viewpoint relations at the concept level, showing that users reconstruct a viewpoint least accurately on concepts that one side took no position on, rather than on concepts where the two disagreed. Our findings open up opportunities for dialog systems that support engagement with alternative viewpoints by acting on which concepts a user and a viewpoint disagree on, rather than on distance alone.
☆ Multi-Token Prediction and Alternative Tokenization Improve Learning Latent Multi-Hop Graph Search at Scale
Arvid Frydenlund, FuTe Wong, Katherine Lambert, Frank Rudzicz
Given an unseen graph, a start node, $s$, and an end node, $t$, can a language model generate the shortest path from $s$ to $t$? Prior work has found that models fail to learn this task as the graphs scale. We show that this failure has two causes: the supervision signal and the tokenization. First, we theoretically establish and empirically demonstrate the effectiveness of multi-token prediction (MTP), and show that the benefits of MTP are topologically agnostic. Second, we introduce alternative tokenizations and show that the two causes are orthogonal. Using these methods, we break through the scaling wall observed by prior work, consistently and stably learning to solve the task at scales far beyond previous limits.

☆ How Well Do Decision Models Transfer to Vietnamese? An Empirical Study of Text Classification
Phat Huu Ha, Nguyen Xuan Phuc, Nong Nhut Quy, Trinh Tran Tran, Nguyen Minh Triet, Dang Van Thin
Recent advances in specialized decision models have expanded the possibilities for zero-shot classification, yet their effectiveness on Vietnamese remains underexplored. This study evaluates four specialized decision models across 13 classification settings spanning six task groups, examining zero-shot performance, sensitivity to label-description language, and robustness to Vietnamese linguistic variations. The evaluation considers English, Vietnamese, and bilingual label descriptions, alongside common linguistic perturbations involving teencode, typographical errors, and diacritic removal. Under English label descriptions, JEV achieves the strongest overall performance, ranking first in 11 classification settings. The effects of label-description language vary across models and datasets, with bilingual descriptions providing no consistent improvement. Robustness experiments reveal that diacritic removal causes the most substantial performance degradation, reducing mean macro-$F_1$ by 9.51--15.47 points across model configurations, considerably more than teencode or typographical errors. These findings highlight the importance of label-description choices and Vietnamese orthographic variation when evaluating specialized decision models for zero-shot text classification.
☆ How Depth Growth and Looping Shape In-Context Learning in Language Models
Ferdinand Kapl, Kaitlin Maile, Stefan Bauer, Johannes von Oswald
Looped and depth-grown language models reuse computations across depth and have attracted interest for their reasoning capabilities. Building on prior suggestive evidence of improved in-context-learning (ICL), we investigate how such reuse favors ICL, including how it shapes the acquisition, retention, and mechanisms of this ability. Our analysis spans pretrained language models up to 3B parameters and controlled tasks. The experiments provide evidence for a shared inductive bias toward learning from context: grown and looped models rely more on information in context and, compared with a full-depth dense reference, acquire ICL earlier in controlled tasks and retain it more strongly when learning fixed associations in the weights is also possible. Tracing attention heads through training reveals that depth growth propagates early induction heads across layers. Ablations show that grown and looped models rely more strongly on induction-ranked heads for both few-shot performance and later-token prediction. Together, these findings provide evidence for a shared inductive bias toward learning from context and suggest that (soft) parameter reuse shapes how this ability develops and which attention mechanisms support it.
☆ SyRA-CS: Synthetic-to-Real Adaptation for Code-Switched ASR with Meta-Learning
Fadel Thior, Ahmer Tabassum, Haania Siddiqui, Hanan Aldarmaki, Thamar Solorio
Code-switched (CS) automatic speech recognition (ASR) remains difficult because real CS speech is scarce and synthetic speech does not fully match it. We investigate whether synthetic CS speech is more useful for learning an adaptation-friendly initialization than for ordinary continual fine-tuning. We propose SyRA-CS, a pair-specific synthetic-to-real adaptation framework based on Reptile meta-learning. For each language pair and pretrained model, we meta-train on the corresponding synthetic CS-FLEURS subset and then adapt using one hour of labeled real target speech. We evaluate four architectural settings: Encoder-CTC, FastConformer-RNN-T, Attention Encoder-Decoder (AED) and Speech-LLM. Experiments cover on Arabic-, French-, German-, and Hindi-English, against synthetic-only transfer, real-only fine-tuning, and continual fine-tuning. SyRA-CS improves over continual fine-tuning on WER in all 16 model-pair comparisons and on CER in 15 of 16, with relative macro WER reductions of 17.7% for XLS-R and 5.3-5.8% for the other three models. The gains hold across the tested models but vary in size across architectures, language pairs and metrics.
☆ SQuAT: Spherical Lattice Vector Quantization-Aware Training
Sebastian Loeschcke, Ivan Koryakovskiy, Tycho F. A. van der Ouderaa, Riccardo Del Chiaro, Mart van Baalen, Paul N. Whatmough, Markus Nagel
Vector quantization (VQ), in particular lattice-based methods, offer strong rate-distortion trade-offs for low-bit large language models (LLMs), yet their behavior under quantization-aware training (VQ-QAT) remains poorly understood. We scale lattice VQ-QAT to billion-parameter LLMs and multi-billion-token training regimes, and introduce SQuAT, a shape-gain VQ-QAT approach trained with a separate straight-through estimator (STE) for the shape and gain. SQuAT decomposes weights into direction and magnitude: normalized directions are quantized using an $E_8$-derived spherical codebook, while gains are quantized independently with low-bit scalar quantizers. We show that, unlike a standard STE applied to the full quantizer, which ignores the shape-gain structure, these separate STEs induce tangent-space updates for directions, yielding an implicit form of spherical (Riemannian) optimization. To enable scalability, we develop a distributed exact nearest-neighbor assignment scheme, in which workers quantize disjoint weight blocks and communicate only discrete indices, reducing VQ-QAT training time by 12.8$\times$ and allowing us to scale it to 10B tokens. Across instruction-tuned LLaMA-3.2-1B/3B, Qwen3-4B, and base Qwen3-1.7B, SQuAT consistently outperforms standard VQ-QAT STE under identical quantizers and initialization, and achieves SOTA VQ-QAT accuracy.

☆ Same Entity, Different Script: Diagnosing Cross-Script Entity Matching Failures in Language Models
Athira Gopal, Patil Prathamesh Pravin, Ankush Kadu, Arpit Jain, Ashwanth Krishnan
Multilingual language models can give different identity judgments when the same people's names are written in different scripts. We study this effect using 601 audited identity pairs with attested Devanagari and Latin forms, holding the question and relation fixed. Qwen2.5-7B's same-person accuracy falls from 35.1% in Latin to 8.5% in Devanagari under Marathi prompts, while different-person accuracy remains above 98%. Evaluation across five models reveals distinct response biases, and reduced tokenization fragmentation does not reliably close the gap. Replicating the design on 374 audited Malayalam pairs built from the same entities reproduces the effect in a second script and language family: same-person accuracy falls to 5.3%, about half the matched Devanagari rate, while the margin still ranks matching above non-matching names at 0.925 AUROC. Latin-trained probes recover label information from Devanagari states, exceeding a string-feature baseline under similarity-matched negatives. Scalar calibration recovers 87% of the raw model-probe balanced-accuracy gap, identifying decision criterion placement as a major contributor. Activation patching localises causal sensitivity to the second entity in Qwen's middle layers; one shared sensitivity direction transfers about half the full-state margin correction. A fact-independent intervention at this site raises held-out Marathi same-person accuracy from 6.5% to 33.5%, with 98.5% different-person accuracy. Together, these results distinguish script-dependent discrimination from criterion placement and identify a causal site for improving identity judgments.
☆ HERD: Harness-Evolving Recursive Distillation for Continual Learning
Xun Sun, Thanh Thi Nguyen, Thuy-Trang Vu
Continual learning requires a model to keep acquiring new skills and knowledge without compromising its existing capabilities. Parametric methods suffer from catastrophic forgetting and loss of plasticity. Non-parametric methods, such as prompts, skills, and harnesses, leverage in-context learning but are limited by context length and lossy plain-text compression. To effectively combine their complementary strengths, we propose HERD (Harness-Evolving Recursive Distillation), a hybrid continual learning framework that learns efficiently in the harness and internalizes experience into cumulative capabilities via distillation. On the harness side, HERD continually refines its skills to capture reusable patterns, while retaining high-residual examples that the skills fail to capture in a bounded memory. On the parameter side, HERD performs drift-constrained distillation with dual anchors, substantially reducing forgetting over long recursive runs. On the 25-stage task stream of TRACE+17, under fixed memory and compute budgets, HERD matches the strongest parametric baseline in new-task acquisition while exhibiting the least forgetting and the strongest retention of general capabilities.

☆ Parameter-Efficient Adaptation of Large Language Models to a Low-Resource Agglutinative Language: A Corpus, Datasets, and a Reproducible Benchmark for the Bashkir Language
Mullosharaf K. Arabov, Svetlana S. Khaybullina
Bashkir is a low-resource agglutinative language written in Cyrillic and remains poorly represented in modern LLMs. We present an open resource suite and a systematic benchmark of adaptation methods: the Bashkir Web Corpus (71,567 documents, 46.9M tokens, 16 sources) and three labelled datasets for binary, multiclass and multilabel text classification; the benchmark covers the first two. Experiments use eight architectures (82M-8B: DistilGPT2, GPT-2 base/medium, Phi-3-mini, Mistral-7B, YandexGPT-5-Lite-8B, mBERT, XLM-R) and three adaptation groups (full fine-tuning, QLoRA, in-context learning), plus continual pretraining (CPT). (1) QLoRA on 3.8-8B models outperforms encoder-only fine-tuning on binary classification: YandexGPT-5-Lite-8B with CPT reaches macro-F1 0.748 vs 0.653 (XLM-R) and 0.650 (mBERT); pairwise differences are significant after Holm correction (p<0.001). (2) YandexGPT-5-Lite-8B significantly outperforms Mistral-7B and Phi-3-mini on multiclass classification, BPB and native-speaker ratings (mean 3.28 vs 2.52 and 2.22; n=100; alpha=0.69-0.77; p<0.05); on binary classification its QLoRA advantage is not significant (p>=0.084). (3) CPT effects depend on model and task: perplexity improves for Phi-3-mini (+0.58) and YandexGPT-5-Lite-8B (+0.33) but worsens for Mistral-7B (-1.15, p<0.001); on binary classification CPT helps Mistral-7B most (+4.0 macro-F1). (4) Tokeniser fertility on Bashkir varies 2.67x (2.92-7.80) and tracks adaptation quality, while perplexity across tokenisers is not comparable. Cyrillic tokenisers with Russian pretraining are preferable; LLM judges reproduce native ratings for grammar and coherence (rho=0.33-0.46) but not language correctness (rho=0.16, p=0.11); native evaluation remains necessary. All artefacts are open-licensed.

☆ A Task-Centric Survey of Long-Document Visual Question Answering: Taxonomy, Evolution, and Future Directions
Yufei Tong, Guanjie Cheng, Tianle Wu, Leilei Zhang, Hang Zheng, Peihan Wu, Jianwei Yin, Shuiguang Deng
Visual document understanding is essential for acquiring knowledge from visually rich documents and is attracting growing research interest. Existing surveys emphasize architectures or technical approaches, with limited attention to how task bottlenecks shape technical choices and research priorities. We present a task-centric survey that decomposes long-document visual question answering (VQA) into four components: document parsing, evidence organization and retrieval, generator organization and learning, and evaluation. For each, we develop a fine-grained taxonomy and trace motivations, technical responses, and cross-component connections. We focus on explicit retrieval while covering direct reading. Our temporal analysis traces expanding priorities from multimodal content understanding to sufficient, high-fidelity evidence acquisition and sustained evidence management. We outline future directions in evidence sufficiency, verifiable and correctable understanding, and budget-aware coordination, with an outlook on continual document understanding.

☆ AfterDeleteBench: Auditing Post-Deletion Information Persistence in LLM Agents
Ehsan Saleh, Roozbeh Nahavandi, Sama Salarian, Raef Bassily, Andrew Perrault
Many LLM agents maintain external persistent memory and derive additional state from past interactions, such as summaries. Deleting an original memory record therefore need not remove information carried by its descendants. We introduce AfterDeleteBench, a controlled behavioral audit for this failure mode in fixed-weight LLM agents. For each synthetic secret, we compare an exposed-then-deleted agent with an otherwise matched agent that was never exposed to the secret, allowing post-deletion recovery to be attributed to the original exposure. In our primary setting, a single query recovers the deleted secret from Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct in roughly two thirds of cases, while matched never-exposed controls have zero recovery. Crucially, recovery does not require a surviving verbatim copy: when the complete secret string is absent and only separately stored components remain under a public composition rule, exact reconstruction succeeds in 62-99% of cases across Qwen, Mistral, and Phi-3. We also show that the response interface is part of the measurement system: target-bearing replies can be scored as failures when the requested format and parser are misaligned. Finally, we separate reconstruction from write-back and fresh-session recall. Provenance-aware closure eliminates observed recovery in the tested positive-baseline settings, whereas an exact-literal write filter blocks reinsertion but cannot erase information already present. Together, these results show that source deletion should be evaluated behaviorally at the level of the agent and its memory system, not only as a storage operation.

☆ CrisiLangs: A Multilingual Resource for Crisis Warning Generation
Giacomo Gonella, Stefano Menini, Marco Guerini
Communicating with the public during an emergency entails issuing warning messages promptly. In Europe, it often requires producing them in more than one language. Recent work has shown that language models can be trained to generate warning messages as a crisis unfolds, but the available data covers only English. ☆ SurveyGraph: Generating Academic Surveys via Methods-Entity Knowledge Graphs EMNLP 2026
Yifan Yuan, Yancheng Zhu, Zuoyu Zhang, Xiaogang Peng
Most automated survey generation systems retrieve papers as flat documents, limiting their ability to capture research structure. We present SurveyGraph, a framework built around a Methods-Entity Knowledge Graph (MEKG) encoding method-level follow-on, competition, and shared-resource relations. The graph supports core-paper discovery, retrieval, hybrid outlining, and evidence-gated narrative synthesis. ☆ When LLMs Forget What They Believe: Characterizing State Integrity Collapse in Long-Horizon Dialogues EMNLP 2026
Yancheng Zhu, Yifan Yuan
Large language models can repeat an instruction while failing to keep obeying it. We study this recall-compliance dissociation in long-horizon dialogue and define State Integrity Collapse: a model preserves a stated constraint in memory but stops applying it to later turns. We introduce StateTrack-Bench, a controlled suite of conversations covering numerical, format, role/persona, factual-memory, and logical-rule constraints. Across seven provider-pinned model snapshots and five dialogue-depth probes, strict joint retention ranges from 62.4% to 100.0%, with degradation concentrated in symbolic constraints and in one especially brittle model. A forked recall audit shows that 88.6% of observed failures are behavioral-drift failures rather than ordinary forgetting; excluding the brittle outlier preserves this diagnosis (84.4% of failures). Extended evaluations at depths up to 50 turns confirm that the dissociation persists beyond the core benchmark, while Structured State Anchoring recovers up to 24.4 points on immutable constraints. The results show that reliability cannot be inferred from single-turn instruction following alone; evaluation must track whether remembered constraints remain operational as dialogue state grows. Code and artifacts are available at: https://github.com/xzzvsxd/StateTrack.

☆ Cola2: Co-Adaptive Hierarchical Language Modeling with Continuous Semantic Compression and Balanced Discrete Generation
Hongcan Guo, Yian Zhao, Feng Wang, Shen Nie, Guoqiang Wei, Yan Zeng, Hengshuang Zhao
Hierarchical language generation separates continuous semantic modeling from discrete text realization, but its effectiveness depends on what the latent space compresses and how the discrete generator uses it. We propose Cola2, a framework that jointly studies continuous semantic compression, the balance between reconstruction and generation, and continuous-discrete co-adaptation. Cola2 dynamically segments text into semantic spans, controls the compression rate through auxiliary-loss-free negative feedback, and combines reconstruction and generation supervision to learn compact, predictive latent representations. A block-causal masked diffusion decoder is trained with clean, noisy, and dropped latent conditions, while classifier-free guidance adjusts the balance between semantic reconstruction and autonomous generation. Token-prediction gradients update the encoder through the latents, whereas Flow Matching prior learning is decoupled from representation learning by stop-gradient. During inference, generated text is re-encoded under a fixed span layout to keep the latent history consistent with the actual text context. Experiments on five downstream tasks with about 210 billion training tokens validate the core designs and show competitive, unsaturated scaling. With 346.03 billion source tokens, an unconverged checkpoint reaches a 60.10 average across eight tasks and surpasses Llama-3-8B on HellaSwag and StoryCloze despite its reported pretraining budget being over 43 times larger. These results demonstrate the potential of continuous-latent hierarchical language generation.

☆ The Value of Memory in Language Agents
Zehao Lin, Erenkai Zhu, Helan Hu, Xixuan Hao, Chunyu Li, Kai Chen
Memory enables language agents to carry useful distinctions from past experience into future decisions. Its value depends on which distinctions survive, whether a bounded agent can use them, and what can be recovered or revised as demands evolve. We develop a decision-theoretic framework that follows this value from raw history through persistent state, operational use, and costly recovery and revision. Original and retained value are expressed in units of avoided task loss; risk-cost frontiers describe attainable recovery and preservation across uses. Unknown demand yields value profiles, while interactive feedback changes the distribution of future work. A cost-aware simulation condition and five minimal counterexamples expose the roles of information timing, retrieval access, component interaction, feedback and demand uncertainty. In particular, a pointer with zero mutual information about the answer can still reduce the cost of exact recovery. Diagnostics on LoCoMo, a two-project update task and longitudinal cases exhibit failures of preservation, use and measurement. At fixed retrieved evidence, a saved ledger changes subsequent answers; at fixed agent outputs, an evaluator revision changes scores. The framework connects these distinctions to operational risk bounds and testable principles for allocating memory, preserving recovery options and revising evidence dependencies.

Shmuel Amar, Benjamin Lieber, Ori Shapira, Debanjana Kar, Sebastian Maximilian Dennerlein, Yufang Hou, Ido Dagan
While LLMs have transformed text generation, standard chat-and-canvas interfaces often reduce human authoring to quite passive supervision, causing review fatigue, over-reliance on AI, and the sidelining of the human role in long-form, source-grounded writing. Prior systems typically address isolated writing phases, leaving comprehensive end-to-end human-LLM collaboration largely unexplored. In this paper, we introduce CoWriter, a proactive agentic scheme that adapts expert human (co-)authoring methodologies from cognitive science to flexibly steer the entire writing workflow while actively encouraging the user to engage. Overall, our results demonstrate that proactively engaging users through established human practices is beneficial for effective LLM-supported co-authoring, providing useful methodologies, techniques and insights for future research on collaborative agents.
☆ Memory-Aware Reward Modeling: Learning Human Preferences for LLM Memory Use
Yijiang River Dong, Zexue He, Cheng Yu, Tiancheng Hu, Michael J Ryan, Jan Batzner, Zheng Hui, Shaolong Wu, Orestis Papakyriakopoulos, Alex Pentland, Nigel Collier, Jiaxin Pei
Memory enables language model assistants to maintain continuity across sessions and generate personalized responses, but knowing more about a user does not necessarily produce responses they prefer. Existing evaluations assess memory capabilities without directly establishing whether memory use benefits the user. To study this gap, we collect approximately 1,000 pairwise judgments from 87 participants using their own ChatGPT queries and personal memories. We find that memory-conditioned responses are not consistently preferred over responses generated without memory and in some cases, users strongly prefer the latter. Automatic evaluators also show limited agreement with users and favor memory-conditioned responses substantially more often than users do. Motivated by these gaps, we introduce a memory-aware reward model that learns from user judgments, achieving 72.8\% preference prediction accuracy and outperforming the existing baselines. Our findings motivate evaluation and optimization objectives that align memory use with the preferences of the people whose information is being used.
☆ A Systematic Comparison of Search Space Complexity and Efficiency for Time-Synchronous End-to-End ASR Models
Simon Berger, Larissa Kleppel, Ralf Schlüter
Search is a central component of Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) systems and has been deeply investigated for classical ASR modeling based on Hidden Markov Models (HMMs). However, in work on current sequence-to-sequence ASR architectures it is often simplified to a small fixed-size beam search without external Language Model (LM). In this work, we present a comparative analysis of search space complexity and efficiency for Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) and neural transducer models under different pruning strategies and LM configurations. We show that these end-to-end (E2E) models work with a small search space most of the time but exhibit large temporary peaks in hypothesis count during periods of uncertainty. Score-based pruning dynamically adapts the search to model uncertainty, leading to a significantly lower Real-Time Factor (RTF). Our results highlight the limitations of search with low fixed-size beam pruning and provide practical guidelines for efficient search in E2E ASR.

☆ IndoNutriQA: An Indonesian Clinical Nutrition Question Answering Benchmark EACL
Tirana Noor Fatyanosa, Ummu Ditya Erliana, Mahendra Data, Muhammad Nurwegiono, Eleazar Tadeo Eman, Alham Fikri Aji

IndoNutriQA is released to foster research in Indonesian clinical NLP and nutritional decision support.

☆ Investigating Multi-Cultural Value Selection Biases in LLMs
Siddhesh Pawar, Arnav Arora, Dustin Wright, Dirk Hovy, Isabelle Augenstein
Large language models (LLMs) serve users whose cultural identities are multi-layered, yet cultural evaluations commonly reduce identity to one label. In this work, we study LLMs under simultaneous identity cues, with a country persona in the system prompt and a second identity signaled in the user turn, either by naming a country (explicit cue) or through the query language (implicit cue). We test World Values Survey (WVS) multiple-choice probing and open-ended sociopolitical generation. In multiple-choice probing, language cues shift answers about three times more than explicit cues but away from the associated country's distribution, and explicit cues do not consistently move answers toward the named country. Across 1.6 million open-ended responses, naming a country closes 30-44% of the gap to the model's own responses for that country, and explicit cues produce more differentiated value changes than language cues. Our results suggest that country and language cues should be evaluated as distinct signals rather than interchangeable proxies for culture.

☆ When More Metadata Misleads: Probing Field-Level Grounding in LLM-Generated Museum Descriptions EMNLP 2026
Clemencia Siro, Laura Hollink
Metadata-conditioned generation is often framed as a data-to-text task in which additional input fields should improve grounding. Museum description generation challenges this assumption because catalogue records are heterogeneous, partially visual, and unevenly documented across geographic regions. We study how metadata composition shapes the generation of artefact descriptions through a two-phase framework. Phase 1 analyses natural metadata variation in a large museum collection, where field count, field type, image availability, and Place co-vary. Phase 2 introduces controlled field probes that hold object identity fixed while varying metadata quantity, field type, and image access. Our results show that metadata quantity alone does not explain generation behaviour. Field type determines whether models ground, omit, or infer information, while images can both support visual grounding and increase unsupported interpretation. More broadly, our findings show that metadata-conditioned data-to-text evaluation should assess field- and claim-level support, rather than relying only on aggregate input coverage or reference similarity.

☆ Prompting Strategy Effects Depend on the Judge: A Multilingual Study of LLM-as-a-Judge Robustness
Asal Mehrabi, Mohammad Aliannejadi, Clemencia Siro
LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation is increasingly applied to multilingual tasks, yet prompting strategies such as Chain-of-Thought and Role Conditioning are recommended based solely on English benchmarks. Whether their effectiveness transfers across judge models and languages remains unknown: prompt-sensitivity studies are largely English-centric, while existing multilingual judge studies fix the prompt template. In this work, we address this gap by measuring this interaction through a benchmark of 9 prompting strategies, 4 open-weight judges, 27 languages, and 4 tasks across 13 datasets, yielding over 1M pointwise scores on gold-reference outputs, validated against human judgments. We find that prompting strategies are not transferable: the choice of the judge model dominates the choice of prompting strategy, strategy effects reverse direction across judges, and stability degrades in low-resource languages, where these effects are concealed by aggregate reporting. These findings show that prompting effects in multilingual evaluation are conditioned on the judge, task, and language. Consequently, prompting strategies should be validated for the intended evaluation setting, and cross-study comparisons should account for both judge and prompt configuration.

☆ Beyond Watching and Searching: Benchmarking Agents for Video-centered Deep Research Reports
Jiahao Meng, Qize Yang, Yuxuan Wang, Yunfei Chu, Wensi Dai, Jin Xu, Yunhai Tong
Deep research agents synthesize open-web evidence into long reports, but current benchmarks do not evaluate research centered on a source video. We introduce Video-Centered Deep Research Report Generation. Given a video and user request, an agent identifies research questions, retrieves external multimodal evidence, and produces an interactive report linked to relevant video moments. ☆ Prompt Sensitivity in Tool-Augmented Model Comparisons
Mateusz Modrzejewski, Tomasz Radzikowski
Language models are compared on question-answering benchmarks that pose each question in one wording, and a reworded question can change which of two models scores higher. We ask whether giving the models tools makes such comparisons more stable. An accuracy gain cannot answer this, because tools change several steps between the question and the scored answer at once. We propose a matched protocol that separates those steps: the answer readout, reasoning, the decision to call, the tool's answer, and the report supplied without a call. We apply it to six open models in five domains under five wordings. We find that tools raise accuracy where a program answers the question and widen the wording sensitivity of many pairwise comparisons. The widening follows how often a model calls, and call frequency follows the wording for some models and not others. It also depends on the interface and the instructions: a native tool-calling interface and a second tool description leave no detected widening in molecules, and sensitivity persists where tools return intermediate results. These findings show that deterministic reports do not guarantee stable comparisons. We recommend reporting call frequency and comparison stability under several wordings beside accuracy.

☆ Mixing Matters: Evidence Position Bias Across Sequence Mixers in Long-Context Question Answering NeurIPS 2026
Aman Behera, Namit Solanki, Mehul Anand
Long-context models often use answer-bearing evidence less effectively when it appears away from a prompt boundary. We compare this position bias across Transformer, state-space, and hybrid Mamba architectures by moving one gold document through a fixed set of nine distractors. The evaluated Transformers show a beginning advantage that the evaluated state-space models lack, while both families favor evidence near the end. A matched pure-versus-hybrid Mamba comparison points in the same direction but remains statistically uncertain. The family difference appears among models that can answer the task. Transformer primacy reappears on synthetic retrieval, where state-space ceiling prevents a family comparison. A corpus swap within one state-space architecture changes overall accuracy without a detectable change in curve shape. Prompt changes can create or nearly remove a beginning advantage. Position remains decodable across architectures even when response curves differ, and attention concentration near initial tokens covaries with beginning sensitivity in the tested Transformer family. These exploratory results show that effective context use depends on the evaluated architecture family, capability, task, and prompt, without isolating the sequence mixer as the sole cause.

☆ Delta N-gram: Forward-Only Continual Writing into a Language Model's Native N-gram Memory
Hao Wu
Our previous analysis found that a model's native hashed N-gram table is where continual updates disturb the base least, but its writes needed backpropagation and changed the pretrained table. We present Delta N-gram, a forward-only write path into that table. The pretrained table T^0 is never modified. Evidence is written into a sparse patch Δon the rows it addresses: a 4.1M-parameter writer emits a target and a gate per (position, hash head) event, and a block-averaged delta rule keeps each row a convex combination of its old value and the gated targets. The writer is trained offline on raw text with the pretraining objective; at use time writing takes forward passes only, and clearing Δrestores the original model. A 0.6B prototype trained to answer questions keeps 16/16 facts after a second write; online gradient writes keep 2--4/16. We study two writers: a plain writer that encodes evidence with the T^0-only model, and a delta writer that reads its current memory while encoding. On Qwen3.8-Flash-Next, the plain writer's memory is document-specific: on 64 held-out news events it lowers continuation NLL by 0.068 nats/token against empty memory and by 0.024 against another document's memory, and 0.063 and 0.013 after 16 intervening articles. The delta writer's memory is more document-specific and keeps more of it under distraction (70% vs 54%). Over 35,000 writes on our previous seven-task chain both writers stay stable and the delta writer drifts less (layer-2 math drift 176% vs 188%); later accuracy changes by -0.26/-0.52pp (-7.29pp for gradient updates) and small-k_i drift is 17.9/17.5% (39.2%). Acquisition is negative, and general-input drift reaches 32.8% (20.75%): format rows read by every example grow to 4--12 times their pretrained norm. Zeroing eight such rows removes over 98% of layer-2 drift on math inputs, together with their NLL gain.

☆ Through a PRISM, Clearly: Step-Sequence Reasoning for Mistake Recognition in Procedural Videos
Olga Loginova, Oleksandr Bezrukov, Frank Keller
Procedural mistake recognition requires identifying deviations from valid multi-step activities. We study post-completion recognition from videos with benchmark-provided step boundaries. We introduce PRISM (Procedural mistake Recognition via Interpretable Semantic Modeling), which grounds video segments in language and maps their descriptions to procedural states using a lightweight hidden Markov model trained on correct executions. Reference alignment and semantic checks identify errors in step content and order. To support evaluation across heterogeneous benchmarks, we map dataset-specific annotations to four edit-based error types: insertion, deletion, substitution, and transposition. Across three benchmarks, we compare gold and VLM-generated descriptions in raw, semantic-role, and action-object forms. Decoder controls show consistent gains from the trained local decoder over nearest-candidate matching, with additional, dataset-dependent benefits from sequence decoding. Semantic role labeling improves Any-F1 in all six matched grounded comparisons, although the gains vary across datasets and do not translate into uniform improvements under separate configuration selection. These results establish language grounding as an effective interface for explicit procedural mistake reasoning and characterize the contributions of representation and decoding.
☆ A Grammar of French Civil Cassation: A Ground-Level Dataset and Tool
Avrile Floro, Tamara Dhorasoo, Nils Holzenberger, Thomas Le Goff, Tiphaine Viard, Maria Boritchev
This paper presents a dataset of 121,536 ground-reasons pairs across 86,464 rulings of the five civil chambers of the French Court of Cassation (2016-2025). An appeal on points of law cannot itself be appealed and often decides a case for good. Understanding how the Court rules is thus crucial and can help lawyers who draft those appeals. Inside a ruling, every ground of appeal (a distinct legal complaint against the appealed decision) has its own answer from the Court. This answer is called the reasons and it belongs to one of a few doctrinal families. Using a deterministic pipeline, we pair each ground with its reasons and attach the pair to its family, according to the established French taxonomy. We obtain a descriptive grammar of civil cassation that shows which grounds succeed, which legal texts they refer to, and how both evolve over the decade. We release the dataset, the code and an interactive consultation tool.

☆ Learning What to Ask for Tomorrow: Active Preference Acquisition for Long-Term Personalized Web Agents
Yiqi Wang, Zizhi Zeng, Jiaqi Zhang, Yiqun Duan, Mingkai Zheng, Taotao Cai
Web agents increasingly serve the same user across repeated online tasks such as booking travel, shopping, and scheduling, so their long-term usefulness depends on how well they know the user's preferences. Passive memory and personalization methods cannot obtain preferences the user never reveals, and clarification methods that value a question by its benefit to the current task overlook the benefit of reusing the answer in later tasks. We study Active Preference Acquisition (APA): which missing preferences are worth the cost of asking? We define the Prospective Personalization Value (PPV) of a missing preference as its expected cumulative utility gain over future tasks minus the effort and disclosure-risk cost of asking, and select questions under a per-interaction budget. We characterize when cumulative reuse justifies acquisition despite insufficient single-task benefit, and evaluate this regime with an initial-profile diagnostic. APA computes the counterfactual gain of each candidate preference on public task prototypes with the agent's own task solver and aggregates it by the expected reuse of each task family. We evaluate it on ActiveWebPref, a controlled longitudinal benchmark with hidden but queryable user profiles and exact task utilities. At a horizon of 20 tasks, 34.4% of budget-feasible valuable missing preferences are amortization-dependent; APA acquires 80.3% of them, whereas current-task value of information acquires 5.1%. Under per-user asking-cost caps matched to APA's realized expenditure, APA achieves higher net utility than four heuristic selection baselines. In our cost setting, asking about every unknown preference yields lower net utility than not asking, while APA recovers 84.2% of its utility gain with 27.6% of its questions. The value of acquiring a user preference should account for its reuse across future tasks, not only its benefit to the task at hand.
☆ RIFT: KV Cache Compression for Draft Models with Complementary Compressibility Training
Dor Tsur, Maor Ashkenazi, Maximilian Golub, Peter Hicks, Yaniv Galron, Talor Abramovich, Yoav Miron, Tiyasa Mitra, Bita Darvish Rouhani, Ran Zilberstein
Speculative decoding is a popular decode acceleration method. As such, it is particularly relevant to long-output generation, which requires an increasingly large KV cache. KV cache compression offers a way to constrain costs in long-context drafting. However, compression methods that focus on prefill information can lose relevance as the output length grows. We term this phenomenon decode staleness and show that it degrades draft model accuracy under selection-based KV cache compression. We introduce RIFT, a family of selection-based KV cache compression strategies that balance prefill information and recent generated tokens. We show that refreshing the KV cache selection during token generation significantly improves the drafter's quality at the cost of additional decode latency. To alleviate the refresh costs, we propose an adaptive KV refresh strategy that reduces the number of score calculations while retaining the drafter's quality. We further demonstrate how a compressibility-driven drafter training phase, based on sparse attention masks, can improve the drafter's quality under a compressed KV cache. Combined with compressibility training, RIFT enables Qwen3.5-4B, drafting for Qwen3.5-122B, to retain over 98% of its full-cache acceptance length on 32K-token inputs, 4K-token outputs, and an 8K-token KV cache budget.
☆ What Pairwise Evaluation Misses: Reward Scores across Prompts
Mateusz Modrzejewski, Tomasz Radzikowski
Reward models score responses for preference optimization, filtering and ranking, and pooling scores across prompts needs them to be comparable. Yet pairwise evaluation compares two responses to the same prompt, so any score component shared by a prompt's responses cancels and goes untested. We study this component by comparing between-prompt variation in model scores with the same variation in human ratings of the same responses. We propose a human-referenced audit, built from a between-prompt variance share, a sibling-rating regression and prompt substitutions, and apply it to eighteen released scorers and six matched training conditions. We find that scores cluster by prompt more than the ratings do: on HelpSteer2, six reward models have between-prompt shares of 0.34 to 0.64 against a human share near zero. Replacing the original prompt with a fixed instruction keeps substantial clustering but weakens agreement with prompt-level human ratings, so clustering alone does not show agreement with humans. Part of that variation is useful: subtracting prompt means lowers the helpfulness of globally selected responses, and scores stay sensitive to a wrong prompt even when training shuffles prompt means. We propose that reward evaluation check between-prompt variation, sensitivity to a wrong prompt and usefulness for selection separately.

☆ SCOPE: Compiling Natural-Language Policies into Neurosymbolic Predicate Programs
Arnon Mazza, Elad Levi
Policy enforcement for LLM agents has shifted from single-turn content moderation to compound, application-specific policies evaluated over multi-turn transcripts and tool-call trajectories. Fine-tuned guardrail models are efficient but degrade on compound policies, and while LLM-as-a-judge approaches achieve higher accuracy, they incur high inference cost and tend to produce unstable predictions. Neurosymbolic methods offer a natural alternative, decomposing the policy into narrow neural decisions composed under explicit logic. However, existing symbolic layers lack the required expressiveness and are optimized against fixed data rather than the policy itself. To address this, we introduce SCOPE, which compiles a policy into an executable program of neural predicates executed by small models, deterministic extractors, and code that composes their outputs. The program is optimized jointly with its training data, using adversarial generation of counterexamples that target where the program deviates from the policy. We evaluate SCOPE on a benchmark of real-world policy tasks against prompt optimization, task decomposition, and workflow generation methods, achieving the highest average accuracy across tasks and models. Programs optimized with the weakest executor transfer to other models without further optimization. This allows them to be deployed in production on inexpensive models, where they reach accuracy comparable to frontier reasoning models. Unlike these monolithic models, SCOPE produces substantially more stable predictions, and each verdict is interpretable through the predicate outputs that produced it. Additionally, we validate the key components of our method through an ablation study. SCOPE thus enables reliable, interpretable, and efficient policy enforcement at production scale.
☆ Harm Is Behavioural, Moderation Is Lexical: How Language and Culture Decide Whom Automated Safety Systems Protect and Whom They Silence
Gabriel Lars Sabadin, Dr Nicola Harding, Sarah Kingston
Content warning: this paper quotes slurs and hateful statements from published test suites; the racial slur among them is masked. Automated systems decide at scale which messages are harmful, and they decide unevenly. Classifiers label African American English as offensive up to twice as often as other English, the accounts of prominent drag queens can score as more toxic than those of white nationalists, and safety behaviour that holds in English collapses in low-resource languages. We argue that over-flagging and under-protection share one cause: deciding harm from vocabulary and surface form rather than from what a message does. We organise the evidence into seven failure modes and argue, as testable hypotheses, that harmful behaviours (a request for secrecy, a demand for payment, an attempt to isolate someone) travel across languages better than the words that express them. We propose an evaluation protocol that makes the gap between the best- and worst-served language communities a headline result, and show that the naive gap is inflated under equal service and that its usual bootstrap interval is unreliable. Applied to five open systems in eleven languages, from a keyword lexicon to two current multilingual guard models, the protocol finds that none reaches native-speaker accuracy at separating harmful function from harmless form (51 of 55 system-language pairs fall short); that greater capability raises discrimination in nine languages while leaving Arabic and romanised Hindi near chance, so the gap between the best- and worst-served languages grows with capability; that one system both over-flags and under-protects the same language most; and that a language a system reads worst can look the most fairly treated when only false positives are reported.

☆ Chunk Recomputation under Real Document Revisions: Measuring Update Cost and Retrieval Quality of Fixed, Recursive, and Content-Defined Chunking
Yuning Bian, Lele Xu, Yifan Yuan, Xinyu Yin, Chengyan Zhan
Chunking strategies for retrieval-augmented generation are almost always evaluated on a static snapshot, yet the documents underlying a knowledge base continue to change. Because chunks are embedded independently, a boundary that moves after an edit forces unchanged text to be re-embedded. We measure this update cost on 120 pairs of adjacent Chinese and English Wikipedia revisions for three stateless chunkers (non-overlapping fixed-size, recursive paragraph-first, and content-defined chunking) under a strict byte-identical reuse rule and a frozen dense encoder. On the 116-article common-success pool, with frozen, length-calibrated parameters, fixed-size chunking recomputes 57-68% of its full new-version embedding-input workload, recursive chunking 38-56%, and content-defined chunking 43-54% (it failed on 4 of 62 Chinese articles), while at these parameters mean evidence recall under a fixed 2048-token retrieval budget differs by less than two percentage points within either language. A cost-only parameter grid shows that the ranking of the two content-aware chunkers changes with chunk-length settings, whereas non-overlapping fixed-size chunking has the highest recomputation cost in every length-matched condition. Controlled edits and a verbatim-presence diagnostic support boundary drift as one contributor to recomputation of text already present in the previous version. Update cost is therefore a substantial, parameter-sensitive dimension that static retrieval evaluations do not expose and that should be reported alongside retrieval quality.

☆ Mostly Reading: What Open-Book Scores Measure in Document-Level LoRA Adapters ACL
Martino M. L. Pulici, Cuong Xuan Chu, Evgeny Kharlamov, Volker Tresp
Single-document LoRA adapters have been proposed as units of knowledge to be merged and retrieved; yet open-book scoring, with the document in the prompt, can credit a document's own adapter (its specialist) for reading that document as readily as for holding it. Training one adapter for each of 150 Wikipedia articles on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, we find that open-book scores mostly measure a reading behaviour every adapter shares: a random adapter trained on another document recovers 82 % of the specialist's open-book advantage over the base model but only 2 % of its advantage on a cloze probe that asks for the same document's facts without providing the source text, a share we cannot distinguish from zero. Content does reach the weights, since the specialists beat the base model in cloze recall by 0.094, and the geometry of their updates follows the documents they read; yet, in a test of 30 queries, adapters chosen by weight-space proximity or text embedding are not distinguishable from random ones on either probe, although that test cannot exclude an advantage of about four fifths of what a query's own adapter gains over a random one. At 16 documents or more, correctly scaled merges recover 87 % to 89 % of the specialists' open-book advantage but only 12 % to 16 % of their cloze advantage, with intervals that run from below zero to at most 32 %, against 94 % for joint training.We also report four silent errors, each of which produced a clean, wrong result.

☆ Padding to k: A Minimum Count in the Schema Makes Open-Weight Models Pad Their Extraction Lists
Abhishek Barali, Aayush Paudel
Extraction pipelines ask language models for JSON that follows a schema, and the schema can set a minimum number of list entries. We ask what models do when the document holds fewer items than that minimum. Under a preregistered plan, we test eight open-weight models on 558 real documents from nine annotated datasets, with six more models on hosted services as a reference. Three findings stand out. First, models treat the count as a target: a minimum shown in the schema, but not enforced, raises the share of lists longer than the true count from 34.5% to 55.4%. Second, the padding is hard to catch, because most added entries are real text taken from the document; about half of them pass a check that each entry occurs in the source. Third, common fixes help only partly: a line saying fewer items are fine removes the effect on average but misses more true items, a count in prose ("up to k") also raises over-counting, an "N/A" option under an enforced minimum still leaves half the lists padded, and re-asking with the validation error makes padding far more common. We release the code, answer lists and run records at https://github.com/AbhishekBarali/padding-to-k

☆ ColaBench: Evaluating Memory-Guided Knowledge Acquisition in Personal Agents
Wenjie Zhang, Zhichao Shi, Xuhui Jiang, Xiaojun Wu, Cehao Yang, Chengjin Xu, Xiaoning Song
Personal agents need to draw on long-term interaction history to retrieve and apply external knowledge suited to a user's current request. Even an answer grounded in relevant documents can be unsuitable when it overlooks the user's circumstances. This creates two connected challenges: using historical conditions to identify the knowledge needed for a new request, and applying that knowledge to the user's situation. Existing benchmarks advance memory recall, personalized retrieval, and memory-guided action, while the joint evaluation of these two challenges remains underexplored. We introduce ColaBench, a synthetic benchmark in health and daily living, with 349 tasks across 10 Profiles and a knowledge base of 13,033 documents. Its Evidence Graph connects personal conditions to their disclosures across public interaction sessions, records prior document exposure, and links answer requirements to personal and newly acquired document evidence. We also introduce MemKB, which uses state summaries to inform queries and rule application, and memory-document links to retrieve knowledge associated with the user's history. MemKB achieves 86.25% Accuracy with an average of 4.07 interaction rounds per task. Task-level analysis finds larger gains on tasks integrating information across multiple historical Facts; error analysis further identifies unsupported personal assumptions and rule misapplication as obstacles to using retrieved evidence. ColaBench provides traceable tasks and an evaluation framework for studying how memory guides knowledge acquisition and rule application.

☆ Interpretive Variation and Discourse Structure in Detecting Enthymemes in Controversial Political Discourse
Martial Pastor, Nelleke Oostdijk
Enthymemes are arguments that leave a premise or conclusion unstated, and they are a common persuasion device on social media. Because readers must fill in the missing part themselves, they often disagree on whether a text contains one. This paper asks how to detect and evaluate enthymemes when such disagreement is part of the task. On tweets about vaccination and immigration, each labeled by five annotators, F1 stalls around 0.67 for every fine-tuned model we train, regardless of data size, added features or label type. We therefore ask how these models should be evaluated instead, and propose metrics that compare predictions with the full distribution of annotator votes, together with a model that predicts this distribution directly and captures disagreement better at no cost in F1. We then ask what role discourse structure plays. Models learn to rely on explicit argumentative structure: they reliably recognize tweets without an enthymeme but rarely commit on tweets that contain one. Both the models and a discourse parser miss arguments whose links are left implicit and must be inferred by the reader. Finally, we discuss what these blind spots mean for content moderation systems trained on majority labels.
☆ A Reality Check of Inference-Time Context Augmentation Methods
Haeun Yu, Geert Heyman, Pepa Atanasova, Isabelle Augenstein
Large language models (LLMs) often fail to utilise the context given in the input, resulting in hallucinations or a fallback to parametric knowledge. Context augmentation methods aim to improve model performance by modifying the input, from inserting filler tokens that add computational time to adding relevant content such as elaboration of a context during inference. However, these methods have been evaluated in isolation and only by their performance gains, leaving it unclear how they compare and whether they reliably improve performance. In this paper, we present an evaluation framework spanning five methods that differ in how informative the inserted content is and apply them at the sentence level across seven models spanning multiple sizes on three datasets. We find that accuracy gains are not always genuine improvements but undirected perturbations: the methods correct some predictions while breaking others, particularly when the inserted text allows only for more computational time. Our analysis reveals that methods that bring no additional content primarily change model predictions near the decision boundary. Our findings highlight the need to evaluate the reliability of the accuracy gains before adopting them in practice.

☆ Joint or Per-Option? Compact Encoders for Closed-Set Decisions from 17 Million to 1 Billion Parameters
Tardelli Ronan Coelho Stekel
Many language-model pipelines end in a closed-set decision, such as selecting a tool. Prompting a large language model for it is slow and order-sensitive. We ask how small an encoder for such decisions can be and whether it should read the options jointly or separately. In a three-seed study at 32 million parameters, per-option scoring beats joint scoring on a held-out sample of a 37-benchmark decision suite by 5.8 points of chance-corrected skill at matched optimizer steps and by 2.8 at approximately matched training compute; two external benchmarks agree, though not significantly on their unseen tasks. Crossing reader with initialisation shows that the advantage requires a reranker initialisation, whose query-candidate pair input matches per-option reading; from the raw encoder the two readers are indistinguishable. Per-option scoring needs 5 to 13 times more encoder passes: at most twice the median latency, but more than four times the mean. Of six released joint encoders (17 million to 1 billion parameters), the 68- and 150-million-parameter models exceed a 435-million-parameter DeBERTa-v3 zero-shot classifier by 4.9 and 5.9 accuracy points on 209 overlap-audited System1-Arena tasks and are at least on par with it on the 145 tasks unseen in training. The smallest answers a median request in 14 milliseconds on 16 processor threads, against 728 for the classifier. Reordering options changes 12 to 23% of the joint models' answers (per-option scoring: none). Up to 68 million parameters, they lead their size range on a public leaderboard's private, mainly in-distribution tests.

☆ Once a Tree, Always a Tree? Cross-lingual Transfer of Hierarchical Generalization in Language Models EMNLP 2026
Shin Yamaguchi, Masaya Taniguchi, Tatsuki Kuribayashi, Taro Watanabe, Yoshinobu Kano
Language models (LMs) often exhibit successful cross-lingual transfer through continued (pre)training, still their underlying process remains unclear. One possibility is that LMs can leverage the evidence toward language-universal principles, namely, hierarchical structure, from a source language (L1) to a new language (L2), while many other non-linguistic explanations, e.g., improved initialization of position embeddings, can be potentially possible. In this study, to quantify the cross-linguistic transferability of linguistic evidence in cross-lingual LM training, we introduce a new experimental design, namely, a cross-lingual poverty-of-stimulus (CPOS) setting that ablates how LMs' hierarchical generalization in L2 can be led by structural evidence provided in L1. We contrast counterfactual L1 and L2 learning scenarios where the L2 corpus is ambiguous between linear and hierarchical grammar rules, while the amount of positive evidence for the hierarchical rule is controlled on the L1 side. Our experimental results show that LMs can partially, yet not perfectly, transfer L1 structural evidence to L2, yielding only a small but detectable hierarchical bias, while the strength of transfer depends on language pairs. We further ablate the conditions for successful transfer, motivated by recent reports on the substantial impact of vocabulary overlap. We indeed find that vocabulary overlap is crucial even for the cross-lingual transfer of hierarchical syntactic generalization. This suggests that this also explains the language-pair-dependent tendencies in the main experiments and suggests that the transfer of linguistic evidence in continued new-language learning is only conditionally achievable.

We present IQuest-Q1, a sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) agentic foundation model with 320B total parameters (15B activated). IQuest-Q1 is developed through a three-stage process: (1) stable and efficient pre-training, combining Stable SplitNorm, Muon, and muP with a two-stage curriculum whose second stage increases the coding-data mixture, preserving a wide stability margin between safe and optimal learning rates; (2) fine-grained mid-training, expanding the data mixture across domains and formats and progressively extending the context; and (3) multi-scaffold with multi-version post-training, combining SFT, domain-specialized RL, and MOPD across various scaffolds to integrate complementary training signals and strengthen agentic coding capabilities.
