☆ RetiJev: Fast and Source-Grounded Ophthalmic Decision Models
Bo Liu, Lianyu Wang, Meng Wang, Ching-Yu Cheng, Huazhu Fu
Ophthalmic clinical decision support requires fast, accurate, and privacy-preserving inference, yet autoregressive large language models (LLMs) incur substantial latency and computational overhead for decisions among predefined actions. We therefore investigate a decision-oriented alternative to generative reasoning. Specifically, we first introduce RetiJev-Data, a source-grounded ophthalmology corpus comprising 78,158 clinical-decision and diagnosis questions derived from articles in PubMed Central and a MeSH vocabulary covering 531 eye diseases, and 50,074 guideline-grading questions spanning 11 grading systems, along with a 24,662-question evaluation benchmark. ☆ Tracing Hallucinations in LLM Reasoning for UML Class Diagram Generation: An Empirical Taxonomy and Mitigation Study
Mojdeh Rahmanian, Foutse Khomh, Ashkan Sami, Yanchao Yu
Large Language Models (LLMs) can generate UML class diagrams from natural language requirements, but hallucinations may compromise the correctness and completeness of the resulting diagrams. Existing evaluations mostly assess the final diagram, without examining the intermediate traces of reasoning-capable LLMs, which can reveal where these hallucinations arise. We develop an empirical taxonomy of eleven hallucination sub-types organised into three dimensions: Prompt Faithfulness (deviations between requirements and reasoning), Code Faithfulness (discrepancies between reasoning and generated PlantUML code), and Reasoning Quality (problems within the reasoning). The taxonomy was derived from 60 manually annotated DeepSeek-R1 traces, applied to 120 further DeepSeek-R1 traces through a dual-LLM annotation pipeline, and examined on 20 DeepSeek-V4-Pro traces. Code Faithfulness was the most frequent dimension for DeepSeek-R1 in both datasets, whereas Prompt Faithfulness dominated for DeepSeek-V4-Pro, suggesting model-dependent failure patterns. In the manually annotated traces, reasoning-trace length showed no significant monotonic association with hallucination frequency, whereas requirement complexity was negatively associated with it, particularly for Prompt Faithfulness errors. In a mitigation experiment on 13 Golden UML ModelSet cases with three Qwen3-based models, adding Prompt Faithfulness and Reasoning Quality constraints to the generation prompt raised the pooled mean F1 score by 4.0 percentage points, whereas a post-hoc Code Faithfulness review provided no consistent further benefit. Relationship modelling remained the lowest-scoring element throughout. These results suggest that localising hallucinations within the generation process can inform targeted, prompt-based mitigation. Whether these findings generalise beyond the DeepSeek and Qwen3 model families remains to be tested.
☆ Verbalizing Activation Differences with Natural Language Autoencoders
Andrea Blasi Núñez, Gianluca Barmina, Federico Torrielli, Thiusius Rajeeth Savarimuthu, Peter Schneider-Kamp, Lukas Galke Poech
Activation verbalizers translate the internal representations of large language models into human-readable text. The recently proposed natural language autoencoders (NLAs) learn this by reconstructing activations through a natural language bottleneck. However, it remains unclear how well these methods generalize beyond the setting they were trained for. Here, we study this question for NLAs along four closely related questions: (i) Do NLA verbalizations exhibit compositional structure with respect to their input? (ii) Do NLAs generalize to finetuned versions of the model they were trained for? (iii) Can NLAs recover finetuning objectives in model diffing? (iv) Can NLAs be specialized for model diffing? Across four models from three families, our experiments provide evidence supporting a largely positive answer to all four questions. Specifically, verbalizations of composed analogy activations mostly name the target while rarely mentioning the subtracted word, and an NLA can be reused on lightweight finetunes of its original model without retraining, with reconstruction quality degrading noticeably only for the largest model. Verbalizing activation differences between Taboo model organisms and their original models recovers the concealed secret word for most organisms, and reinforcement learning on activation differences further improves this recovery on held-out organisms. We also find that NLAs can recover the finetuning objective without the finetuned model generating any token, since the most informative token positions precede the user prompt. NLAs do not yet match state-of-the-art model diffing methods on the emergent misalignment detection task, but outperform a blackbox baseline. Our findings suggest that NLAs can meaningfully verbalize differences in activation space, rendering NLAs a promising direction for future work.

☆ ReflectPO: Reflection-Guided Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon LLM Agents
Rumei Li, Xunliang Cai
With external tools, large language models (LLMs) can tackle complex tasks through sustained environmental interaction. LLM-based agents are therefore increasingly used to tackle complex long-horizon tasks in real-world environments. However, these tasks involve sequential, interdependent decisions and changes in environmental states. Early execution errors affect subsequent interaction histories and environmental states, and their effects accumulate along the decision chain. To address this challenge, we propose Reflection-Guided Policy Optimization (ReflectPO), a reinforcement learning framework for autonomous error recovery in long-horizon tasks. ReflectPO comprises two core components. The first is Reflection-Guided Sampling. We use branch differences and repeated visits in interaction trajectories to construct hints for error recovery. The second is Tree-Structured Credit Assignment. We organize sampled trajectories into trees and assign action-level credit using parent--child reward differences and recovery gains. Experiments over two long-horizon tool-use benchmarks show that ReflectPO outperforms multiple existing baselines in most domains. It also achieves the best overall performance on average. Further analyses show that ReflectPO effectively improves agents' ability to recognize their own deviations, recover from erroneous states, and continue making progress toward task completion.
♻ ☆ Uncensored Is Not Unbiased: Abliteration Removes Refusals but Not Script-Dependent Bias About Taiwan
Ju-Chun Ko
"Abliterated" ("uncensored") builds of open-weight language models remove the activation direction that mediates refusal, and are popular partly on the assumption that a model that no longer refuses is free of political censorship. We test this on two matched pairs of 2-bit models: NVIDIA's Nemotron 3.5 Bolt 74B-A9B and Qwen3.8-Flash-Next, each against a community abliteration. Each model judged 22 statement pairs (on the Republic of China (Taiwan), 1989 Tiananmen, control countries) in Traditional Chinese, Simplified Chinese (a character-level conversion) and English, and answered 27 open questions per script. Script mattered far more than abliteration: the original Bolt scored +0.58 on Index S (-1: People's Republic of China (PRC) position; +1: ROC position) in Traditional Chinese, +0.57 in English and +0.05 in Simplified Chinese, and affirmed "Taiwan is part of the PRC" with probability 0.91 in Simplified but 0.06 in Traditional characters; abliteration changed its scores by at most 0.06, mostly by raising "yes" on both sides. In Qwen, abliteration removed every refusal on 1989 and raised scores by 0.21 to 0.34, yet the abliterated Qwen still affirmed that Taiwan is part of the PRC in all three scripts. Naming the state the Republic of China (Taiwan) raised every model's score by 0.10 to 0.33 but left the script gap. Control pairs showed no script gap; both edits changed only their declared tensors. In 740,000 web documents, Simplified text on Taiwan used PRC-framing terms 3.7 times as often as Traditional text. Steering the script gap away failed; a stance direction from the ROC wording raised scores on new statements by 0.04 to 0.06; negated, it lowered them more. A four-sentence system prompt raised the abliterated Qwen to +0.93 to +0.97. Keyword scoring rules failed the models closest to the ROC position mostly for attributing or denying the PRC claim. Uncensored is not unbiased.

♻ ☆ TabAgent: Distilling LLM Agent Components into Cheap Classifiers
Ido Levy, Segev Shlomov, Avi Yaeli, Nir Mashkif, Asaf Adi, Yinon Goldshtein, Eilam Shapira, Leshem Choshen
Agentic systems, AI architectures that autonomously execute multi-step workflows to achieve complex goals, are often built using repeated large language model (LLM) calls for closed-set decision tasks such as routing, shortlisting, gating, and verification. While convenient, this design makes deployments slow and expensive due to cumulative latency and token usage. We propose TabAgent, a framework for replacing generative decision components in closed-set selection tasks with a compact textual-tabular classifier trained on execution traces. TabAgent (i) extracts structured schema, state, and dependency features from trajectories (TabSchema), (ii) augments coverage with schema-aligned synthetic supervision (TabSynth), and (iii) scores candidates with a lightweight classifier (TabHead). On the long-horizon AppWorld benchmark, TabAgent maintains task-level success while eliminating shortlist-time LLM calls, reducing latency by approximately 95% and inference cost by 85-91%. Beyond tool shortlisting, TabAgent generalizes to other agentic decision heads, establishing a paradigm for learned discriminative replacements of generative bottlenecks in production agent architectures.

♻ ☆ Sharp Degree Bounds for Putinar Certificates in Terms of the Positivity Margin via Analytic Sums of Squares
Yuxiang Zhao, Ting Gan
We establish sharp degree bounds for Putinar sum-of-squares (SOS) certificates in terms of the positivity margin $δ$. For any fixed Archimedean quadratic module with prescribed generators and any fixed degree bound on the objective, normalized polynomial objectives admit a uniform certificate-degree upper bound of $O(δ^{-1/2}\log^2(2/δ))$. The bound is sharp: we construct a matching lower-bound example in three variables. We further show that the number of inequality generators affects the optimal degree bound. For quadratic modules with at most two inequality generators, the bound improves to $O(δ^{-1/2}\log(2/δ))$, which is also sharp. This improvement is obtained by directly converting two-generator Stengle refutations into certificates for the original quadratic module. We also establish sharp degree bounds for trigonometric SOS certificates on tori. In particular, for the full torus without additional constraints, the optimal worst-case order improves to $δ^{-1/4}$, whereas additional equality constraints can lead the order to $δ^{-1/2}\log(2/δ)$. These results also yield sharp worst-case convergence rates for the Lasserre hierarchy.
♻ ☆ Beyond Naturalness: Probing Automated Text-To-Speech Evaluators on Linguistically Grounded Dimensions EMNLP 2026
Oluwanifemi Bamgbose, Jash Shah, Simon Rosen, Lindsay Devon Brin, Hoang H Nguyen, Anke Koelzer, Rachel Hansen, Tara Bogavelli, Fanny Riols
Automated Text-to-Speech (TTS) evaluation methods (Mean Opinion Score (MOS) predictors and Audio Large Language Model (Audio-LLM) judges) are expected to reflect human perception, yet it is unclear how well they capture the distinct aspects of speech that listeners actually perceive. We deconstruct "naturalness" into a linguistically grounded annotation schema spanning 10 distinct perceptual dimensions, and use it to construct the first dimension-level meta-evaluation benchmark for TTS, comprising 860 utterances annotated by trained linguist raters. Results from benchmarking four MOS predictors and four Audio-LLM judges reveal that MOS predictors are more sensitive to acoustic signal-level properties at the prosodic and paralinguistic levels, while Audio-LLM judges show selective, prompt-dependent detection that favors word-level errors and does not generalize across all dimensions. Neither class reliably captures a breadth of linguistically structured, human-perceptible speech errors. Our dataset, annotation schema, and evaluation code are publicly released to support more targeted and interpretable TTS evaluation.

♻ ☆ Evaluating Rational Contracting in Natural Language
Bhavyesh Sajja, Max Kleiman-Weiner, Roger Zimmermann, Tan Zhi-Xuan
The emergence of language-based AI agents promises to transform the scope of machine economic activity. Instead of just proposing bids or following hard-coded protocols, such agents can be used to negotiate and execute agreements in natural language. However, most evaluations of these abilities have focused on one-off exchanges or simple economic games, leaving open the rich space of time-extended, contingent, and incomplete contracts expressible in language; they also focus on raw profit, without measuring the qualities required for trustworthy contracting. We address this by formulating a rational framework for how agents should negotiate and perform natural language contracts in uncertain multi-step environments. Using this framework, we develop metrics and baselines for quantifying rational and cooperative play. We instantiate our framework in ContractSim, an evaluation suite where two players negotiate and execute a multi-turn supplier contract under environmental and inter-player uncertainty. We find that LLM-based agents agree on efficient contracts when environmental uncertainty is low, but under high uncertainty, often fail to negotiate satisfiable, efficient, or mutually beneficial contracts, underperforming even heuristic baselines. When executing contracts, LLM agents are also frequently uncooperative, violating contracts for extra profit even when they are easy to satisfy. These findings highlight room for improvement in the design of language agents that can negotiate and execute contracts both rationally and cooperatively.

♻ ☆ MLEvolve: A Self-Evolving Framework for Automated Machine Learning Algorithm Discovery
Shangheng Du, Xiangchao Yan, Jinxin Shi, Zongsheng Cao, Shiyang Feng, Zichen Liang, Boyuan Sun, Xu Guo, Tianshuo Peng, Yifan Zhou, Jie Hou, Xin Li, Jie Zhou, Liang He, Bo Zhang, Lei Bai
Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly applied to long-horizon tasks such as scientific discovery and machine learning engineering (MLE), where sustained self-evolution becomes a key capability. However, existing MLE agents suffer from isolated exploration, inefficient experience reuse, and lack of hierarchical control, which together hinder long-horizon optimization. We present MLEvolve, an LLM-based self-evolving multi-agent framework for end-to-end machine learning algorithm discovery. By extending tree search to Progressive MCGS, MLEvolve enables cross-branch information flow through graph-based reference edges and gradually shifts the search from broad exploration to focused exploitation with an entropy-inspired progressive schedule. To allow the agent to evolve with accumulated experience, we introduce Retrospective Memory, which combines a cold-start domain knowledge base with a dynamic global memory for task-specific experience retrieval and reuse. For stable long-horizon iteration, we further decouple strategic planning from code generation with adaptive coding modes. Evaluation on MLE-Bench shows that MLEvolve achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple dimensions including average medal rate and valid submission rate under a 12-hour budget (half the standard runtime). Moreover, MLEvolve also outperforms specialized algorithm discovery methods including AlphaEvolve on mathematical algorithm optimization tasks, demonstrating strong cross-domain generalization. Code is available at https://github.com/InternScience/MLEvolve.

♻ ☆ How Well Does Mathematical Reasoning Transfer Across Languages in English-Centric LLMs? EMNLP 2026
Wen Yang, Junhong Wu, Chong Li, Chengqing Zong, Jiajun Zhang
Recent advances in Reinforcement Post-Training (RPT) have substantially improved the mathematical reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs), but it remains unclear how well such gains transfer across languages. In this work, we study cross-lingual mathematical reasoning transfer in English-centric LLMs under controlled multilingual evaluation. We first evaluate a range of English-centric reasoning models on multilingual mathematical reasoning benchmarks and find that transfer is highly variable across initial model choice, target language, and training paradigm. Through controlled comparative experiments, we further find that stronger English-centric initialization does not necessarily lead to stronger relative transfer efficiency, even when it yields higher multilingual reasoning accuracy. Finally, we show that the largest improvement comes from moving beyond English-only supervision: adding a single parallel language yields a substantial gain, while further gains from additional languages are smaller but consistent. Overall, our results highlight the need to evaluate multilingual mathematical reasoning directly, using both absolute performance and relative transfer-efficiency metrics.

♻ ☆ How Far Can the Gemma 4 Speech Encoder Go Without Changing Its Weights? Saudi and Gulf Arabic Speech Recognition with a Frozen and an Adapter-Augmented Encoder
Amr Abdelkhalik
We study the 305M-parameter audio encoder shared by Gemma 4 and EmbeddingGemma 2 (bit-identical to that of Gemma 4 E2B except for the final audio-embedding projection) for Saudi and Gulf Arabic speech recognition, without changing any of its pretrained weights; the adapters of our adapted system are additive and kept separate. A frozen system trains only a 22.6M-parameter CTC reader over a learned mix of four intermediate encoder layers and the encoder output; an adapted system adds 3.1M low-rank adapter parameters, a 41.5M-parameter hybrid CTC/attention reader and joint decoding with a sub-word language model. Against Cohere Transcribe Arabic (2.07B parameters), re-run on identical audio, the frozen system trails in word error rate (WER) but is not significantly different in character error rate (CER) on five of six human-labelled conditions, while running 3.4 times faster on CPU with greedy decoding. The adapted system is not significantly different in WER on a public Saudi customer-service call (19.20% against 19.27%) and on clean Emirati speech, is significantly better on telephone-coded Emirati speech, has a significantly lower CER on all six conditions of these sets, and beats a 0.6B recogniser fine-tuned on SADA in WER on all six; it still trails Cohere in WER on long-form Saudi speech. On a blind, human-corrected public call-centre sample it ranks 4 of 15 systems, behind three commercial APIs (significantly only one) and ahead of the other self-hostable systems by point estimate. An optimised joint decoder runs it at 139 times real time on one GPU. We also find, on our data, that references made by correcting machine drafts favour the drafting system. Code and public-set results are released.

♻ ☆ When Should LLMs Reason? Adaptive Inference Routing via Entropy Dynamics
Wei Xia, Haoqing Wang, Zhi-Hong Deng, Yehui Tang
Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning can improve LLM performance, but its benefits vary across tasks, models, and individual queries. On some tasks, CoT provides marginal or negative accuracy gains while substantially increasing token consumption. We investigate whether early decoding dynamics can help identify when explicit reasoning is useful. Our analysis shows that tasks with larger CoT gains tend to exhibit decreasing early-stage entropy, while low-gain tasks more often show oscillatory or increasing trajectories. These patterns motivate an empirical regime interpretation of decoding behavior. We propose \textbf{EDRM} (Entropy Dynamics-based Reasoning Manifold), a lightweight routing framework whose default heuristic requires no router training. EDRM summarizes early entropy trajectories with a compact, interpretable representation and selects among Direct, Standard, and CoT decoding. Across 15 benchmarks and 7 LLMs, its global and instance-level variants offer \textbf{16--55\%} token savings at comparable accuracy. The Full instance-level configuration combines routing with a short Direct fallback branch; our heuristic-router ablation reports up to \textbf{4.9 percentage points} of additional accuracy from oracle answer selection over routing without fallback. These results support selective reasoning based on early decoding signals and demonstrate the potential of entropy-based control for efficient LLM inference.
♻ ☆ Latent Bridges for Multi-Table Question Answering
Simone Varriale, Tamara Cucumides, Floris Geerts, Paolo Papotti
We introduce GRAB, a constructor-encoder-bridge pipeline for table question answering. Our method lifts relational data into an heterogeneous graph, encodes it via message passing, and transfers the signals to an LLM through a small set of query-conditioned latent tokens. This provides the LLM with a compact, task-relevant structural representation together with the flattened text. Crucially, the LLM remains strictly frozen to preserve its general reasoning capabilities; we train only the lightweight graph encoder and latent bridge (91M parameters), allowing the entire pipeline to be trained efficiently. Our pipeline significantly improves performance on relational Question Answering, with the largest gains in demanding multi-table settings, offering an efficient, principled way to connect relational deep learning with LLMs.

♻ ☆ ShIOEnv: A Command Evaluation Environment for Grammar-Constrained Synthesis and Execution Behavior Modeling
Jarrod Ragsdale, Rajendra Boppana
We introduce ShIOEnv, a Gymnasium-compatible Bash environment for synthesizing execution-annotated shell input traces. Datasets containing such traces are useful for training models to perform execution-free shell simulation, which in turn is useful for deploying honeypots for cyber deception. Existing datasets, such as NL2CMD and other Bash command corpora, do not include complete execution artifacts (stdout, stderr, exit status, and changes to the system's state) associated with a command's execution. ShIOEnv synthesizes shell inputs, executes them in a controlled system context, and records stdout, stderr, exit status, and structured filesystem and environment patches. Unconstrained (random) generation of shell inputs (command and arguments) results in multiple instances of arguments that are mostly redundant or result in execution errors. We introduce grammar-constrained synthesis to generate syntactically valid arguments with high input irreducibility, which we estimate through self-ablative contrasts of execution artifacts. We prepare 1.6M samples using grammar-constrained and unconstrained synthesis and collect their execution artifacts for 86 Linux/Bash utilities. Using this dataset, we train small language models (CodeT5) and evaluate their accuracy in predicting the actual execution behavior of attacker-sourced Bash commands. These models outperform commonly used rule-based simulation (Cowrie) and prompted large language models by up to 14.2 pp in output edit similarity (O-ES). We enhance the NL2CMD dataset by constructing 500K samples executable in a grounded system context along with their complete execution artifacts. We empirically demonstrate that models trained on grammar-constrained samples outperform those trained on NL2CMD samples by 22.5 pp in O-ES. ShIOEnv, command grammars, and 2.1M samples used in our primary evaluations are released for use by other researchers.

♻ ☆ Measuring, Localizing, and Reducing the AI-Like Writing Style of Post-Trained LLMs
Aniket Anand, Janvijay Singh, Zhewei Sun, Dilek Hakkani-Tür, Nick Feamster
Post-trained language models often exhibit a recognizable AI-like writing style, yet its connection to post-training and internal representations remains poorly understood. In this work, we study whether post-training introduces or amplifies AI-like stylistic regularities and whether these regularities have a localized internal signature. To this end, we compare human text, base-model generations, and aligned-model generations under matched human-source prefixes. Aligned generations show lower human-corpus affinity and higher AI-detection rates than base generations, suggesting that post-training shifts generated text away from human-corpus style and toward detector-visible AI-like text. We then introduce PASTA (Post-training Alignment Signature Targeted Ablation), a training-free method that estimates a post-training alignment signature from aligned-base residual contrasts and ablates the corresponding direction during decoding. Across 11 aligned models and 6 AI-detectors, PASTA lowers the detection rate for most aligned models; this effect transfers well across detectors and is not reproduced by random directions. Qualitative analysis suggests that PASTA generations remain relevant and coherent while exhibiting greater stylistic variation. Together, these results show that AI-like stylistic effects of post-training can be measured, localized, and causally tested through activation ablation.
♻ ☆ CARTE: A Benchmark for Mapping Language Model Knowledge Across France
Sarah Almeida Carneiro, Christos Xypolopoulos, Xiao Fei, Yang Zhang, Michalis Vazirgiannis
We introduce CARTE (Culturally Anchored Regional-Territorial Evaluation), a multiple-choice benchmark for evaluating the ability of large language models (LLMs) to discriminate fine grained, geographically grounded and regionally differentiated knowledge within France. While prior benchmarks focus on national-level cultural understanding, they largely overlook intra-country variation and the need to distinguish between closely related regional contexts. CARTE addresses this gap by introducing 3,132 questions spanning the 13 metropolitan regions of France and covering 12 thematic domains, including culture, language, demographics, economy, environment, and mobility. Questions underwent four-stage validation: human review, LLM-as-a-judge, model-based difficulty calibration, and a second human review. We further introduce CARTE-LV, a subset targeting Linguistic Variation across French regions, enabling focused evaluation of language-related differences. We evaluate 32 open-weight LLMs of 1B to 30B parameters and three frontier API models across zero-, one-, and three-shot settings. Our experiments reveal performance disparities across regions and model scales, suggesting systematic gaps in pretraining coverage and limited robustness to intra-national variation.
♻ ☆ TaxoBench: Can Deep Research Agents Retrieve and Organize? Evaluating the Synthesis Gap with Expert Taxonomies
Ming Zhang, Jiabao Zhuang, Wenqing Jing, Kexin Tan, Ziyu Kong, Jingyi Deng, Yujiong Shen, Yuhui Wang, Zhenghao Xiang, Qiyuan Peng, Mingqi Wu, Long Ma, Zhangyue Yin, Shihan Dou, Maxm Pan, Tao Gui, Qi Zhang, Xuanjing Huang
Deep Research Agents increasingly automate survey writing, yet existing benchmarks do not jointly test whether they retrieve the papers experts consider essential and organize those papers into paper-grounded taxonomies. We introduce TaxoBench, a benchmark built from 72 highly cited LLM surveys, 3,815 cited papers, and their expert-authored taxonomies. TaxoBench evaluates systems in two settings: Deep Research mode measures end-to-end retrieval and organization from a topic, while Bottom-Up mode provides the expert paper set and isolates organization. We evaluate leaf-level assignments with ARI and V-Measure and hierarchy-level structure with US-TED, US-NTED, and Sem-Path. Across 7 Deep Research Agents and 16 LLM configurations, the best agent retrieves only 20.92% of expert-cited papers, and none of 70 standard Bottom-Up runs reaches the experts' average depth of 4.86. A controlled probe shows that models which match this depth do so by fragmenting the taxonomy, reducing alignment with the expert reference. We further find that raw Sem-Path remains near a no-organization floor even when a newer model generation gains 3.68 pp ARI; after depth matching, humans lead on all 10 matched surveys by 13.27 pp. These results identify retrieval and hierarchical organization as separate bottlenecks and show why hierarchy metrics must be calibrated before they are used to compare models.

♻ ☆ Disclosure Begets Disclosure: A Self-Reinforcing Privacy Loop in LLM Agents with Persistent Memory
Masahiro Kaneko, Hiromi Arai, Timothy Baldwin
Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use persistent memory to personalize responses, and personal attributes in this memory pose a privacy risk if exposed. We ask how consulting memory during dialogue changes what users go on to say, and thus what the memory comes to reveal. In multi-session simulations, a simulated user with 24 personal attributes talks with a support agent that does or does not consult memory, and in both settings attributes are inferred from the full accumulated record. Across four support models, memory raises response quality but also empirical leakage, the share of attribute values correctly inferred from the record, by a margin that widens over sessions. The cause is a loop in ordinary, non-adversarial assistance. Memory-conditioned responses carry earlier values forward, often as presuppositions, and users affirm them or leave them uncorrected, so that cues and the agent's own guesses become recorded values. The loop reinforces itself, and removing the agent's mentions of an attribute lowers its later disclosure about twice as much as single-turn effects predict. Users cannot leave it on their own, since correcting a value discloses the attribute, declining to discuss it shifts later predictions toward sensitive values, and incorrect values left in place become entrenched. The loop is best weakened at the agent's response. Instructing the agent not to state or presuppose attributes brings leakage to about the no-memory level while keeping most of the quality gains, whereas filtering or anonymizing what users say or what is stored gives up much of them.
♻ ☆ Markovian Generation Chains in Large Language Models EMNLP 2026
Mingmeng Geng, Amr Mohamed, Guokan Shang, Michalis Vazirgiannis, Thierry Poibeau
The widespread use of large language models (LLMs) raises an important question: how do texts evolve when they are repeatedly processed by LLMs? In this paper, we define this iterative inference process as Markovian generation chains, where each step takes a specific prompt template and the previous output as input, without including any prior data. In iterative rephrasing and round-trip translation experiments, the output either converges to a small recurrent set or continues to produce novel sentences within a finite horizon. Through sentence-level Markov chain modeling and analysis of simulated data, we show that iterative process can either increase or reduce the diversity depending on factors such as the temperature parameter and the initial input sentence. These results offer valuable insights into the dynamics of iterative LLM inference and their implications for multi-agent LLM systems.

♻ ☆ Graph Memory Transformer (GMT)
Nicola Zanarini, Niccolò Ferrari, Evelina Lamma
We investigate whether an explicit learned memory graph can replace the Feed-Forward Network (FFN) sublayer in a decoder-only transformer while preserving the surrounding autoregressive architecture. In the proposed graph Memory Transformer (GMT), a memory cell routes token representations over a learned bank of centroids connected by a learned directed transition matrix, while causal self-attention remains unchanged. In the base v7 instantiation, each of 16 transformer blocks contains 128 centroids and a 128 x 128 edge matrix. Each cell uses gravitational source routing and token-conditioned target selection to return a gated displacement from the estimated source memory state toward the target memory state, rather than a retrieved value. The resulting decoder-only language model has 82.2M trainable parameters and no dense FFN sublayers; the evaluated dense GPT-style baseline has 103.0M. It trains stably and exposes centroid usage, transition structure, and source-to-target movement for direct inspection in the forward computation. It trails the larger baseline in validation loss and perplexity (3.5995/36.58 vs. 3.2903/26.85), while showing close zero-shot benchmark behavior under the evaluated setting. These results support the viability and structural interpretability of replacing dense within-token transformation with graph-mediated memory navigation, without claiming state-of-the-art performance. Broader scaling, optimized kernels, and more extensive benchmark evaluation remain for future work.

♻ ☆ Evolving with Open Worlds: Towards Recursive Self-Improvement for Future Forecasting
Zhipeng Chen, Yunsen Liang, Yanxi Chen, Hongkang Zhao, Lingyun Feng, Ruiyang Ren, Guoxin Chen, Jia Deng, Feng Chang, Chuan Hao, Ran Tao, Jian Yang, Ji-Rong Wen, Bryan Dai, Wayne Xin Zhao
Most contemporary artificial intelligence (AI) systems are released with frozen parameters and improve through external updates, whereas an ideal system would keep improving from its own experience, a capacity we call recursive self-improvement (RSI). Yet studying RSI requires an environment that is continuously changing, objectively verifiable, and naturally grounded, posing a fundamental challenge for agent evolution. Here we introduce EvoForesight, a sustainable evolution agent system grounded in future event prediction, in which agents continuously interact with a dynamically evolving environment and improve by internalizing and externalizing the experience gained through these interactions. A living environment synthesizes verifiable tasks, and a recursive-evolution engine turns the reasoning trajectory behind each prediction back into capability: decoupling search from prediction adapts parameter-level evolution to the contamination-prone live environment, while distilling reusable skills lets the agent evolve from the limited live trajectories such an environment affords. On the external FutureX leaderboard, which evaluates predictions against real-world outcomes and is updated weekly, our self-improved forecaster ranked first across all systems over three consecutive weeks, achieving up to a 195% improvement in rating. On our synthesized evaluation, it ranked among the top three across four base LLMs, outperforming the strongest baseline by up to 10.7 Bradley--Terry points. More broadly, these results suggest that a system built for self-improvement is best understood as an agent that co-evolves with a genuinely living, open-ended environment, pointing to a shift in AI from training static models on fixed data toward cultivating agents that grow stronger from their own experience, with such an open world as the indispensable basis of that growth.

♻ ☆ GFlowPO: Generative Flow Network as a Language Model Prompt Optimizer
Junmo Cho, Suhan Kim, Sangjune An, Minsu Kim, Dong Bok Lee, Heejun Lee, Sung Ju Hwang, Hae Beom Lee
Finding effective prompts for language models (LMs) is critical yet notoriously difficult: the prompt space is combinatorially large, rewards are sparse due to expensive target-LM evaluation. Yet, existing RL-based prompt optimizers often rely on on-policy updates and a meta-prompt sampled from a fixed distribution, leading to poor sample efficiency. We propose GFlowPO, a probabilistic prompt optimization framework that casts prompt search as a posterior inference problem over latent prompts regularized by a meta-prompted reference-LM prior. In the first step, we fine-tune a lightweight prompt-LM with an off-policy Generative Flow Network (GFlowNet) objective, using a replay-based training policy that reuses past prompt evaluations to enable sample-efficient exploration. In the second step, we introduce Dynamic Memory Update (DMU), a training-free mechanism that updates the meta-prompt by injecting both (i) diverse prompts from a replay buffer and (ii) top-performing prompts from a small priority queue, thereby progressively concentrating the search process on high-reward regions. Across few-shot text classification, instruction induction benchmarks, and question answering tasks, GFlowPO consistently outperforms recent discrete prompt optimization baselines.
♻ ☆ On the Behavioral Traits of LLM Agents
Haokai Zhao, Jie Gao, Yunze Xiao, Xintao Wang, Weihao Xuan, Aditya Joshi, Mark Dredze, Jen-tse Huang
Users increasingly describe different AI agents as distinct colleagues to work with. AI personality research aims to quantify such impressions by attributing human- like "traits" to agents. However, existing measures fall short: models' self-reports (S-data) diverge from their actual behavior, while informant ratings from LLM judges (I-data) are costly to scale and cover few everyday scenarios. In this paper, we propose A-B-D to infer traits bottom-up from behavioral data (B-data), namely how agents act on their environment and communicate with users, as recorded in existing trajectories. From 345,667 real-world trajectories spanning 80 models, 12 tasks, and 50 harnesses, we extract 318 candidate features that capture both the actions an agent takes at each step (functional) and the language accompanying them (linguistic). We retain only features that show instance-level stability, cross-task consistency, and model discriminability. Factor analysis of the remaining 79 features uncovers six stable, model-attributable factors, two functional and four linguistic. For example, Claude-Sonnet-4.6 and Kimi-K3 exhibit the most planfulness, whereas GPT-5.5 and GPT-5.6 are the least energetic. Moreover, we quantify the "knowledge-action gap" in the wild: these factors correlate only weakly with self-reported Big Five scores, even for conceptually matched pairs such as extraversion and energetic (r = 0.07, p = 0.58). Our work offers a new lens for understanding AI personality, with implications for users, developers, and researchers from both computer science and social science.

♻ ☆ Is the Judge Its Twin? Post-training makes a model's writing more predictable but barely moves its taste, as a judge, toward predictable writing
Arman Nik Khah, Arvin Bahreini
Language models now routinely grade other language models. If post-training makes a model's writing more predictable, it may also teach the same model, as a judge, to reward predictable writing. We test this directly. We follow two open 7-billion-parameter model families, OLMo-2 and Zephyr, from their base models through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and direct preference optimization (DPO), and measure every stage as a writer of short stories and as a judge of story pairs. As writers, the models drift as feared. Each family's trained model finds its stages' stories more familiar in the order base, SFT, DPO, in all ten prompts, and a model from the other family finds the trained stories 7.0 to 9.2 percent (OLMo-2) and 24 to 25 percent (Zephyr) less surprising per token. As judges, they barely move toward predictable writing. Asked which story is better, a question every judge passes a competence check on, no trained judge's preference for the more predictable story grows by as much as one point of pick probability at its estimate. A one-sided 95% upper bound on that growth is about 5 points on the widest pairs. Training instead makes judges more swayed by story length and by display order, and it breaks "more creative" as a question. Trained judges no longer reliably prefer a story to its own words in scrambled order. For these two families, the practical hazard in a small trained judge of creative writing lies in these measurable habits.

♻ ☆ Context-Aware Subtitle Translation on CPU: Deployment-Driven Training, Evaluation, and Latency Design
Tsz-To Wong, Shyang-En Weng
We study English-to-Traditional-Chinese (Taiwan) subtitle translation for a free browser extension, where the model must run on the viewer's CPU in about 500 ms per subtitle. We train two models built on the LMT-60 base models, Sotto-1.7B and the deployed Sotto-0.6B, with a task-specific vocabulary, continued pretraining, supervised fine-tuning, weight interpolation, logit distillation and on-policy preference optimization. Because reference translations of subtitles are unreliable, we evaluate by pairwise comparison against one fixed baseline, Google Translate, with each pair judged in both orders by a panel of two LLM judges. On 1,820 held-out subtitles, Sotto-1.7B has a win rate of 0.576 against the baseline and wins head-to-head against four open translation models, and is slightly below a 27B model. We split latency into prefill and decoding: idle-time prefill, which prefills the known part of the prompt while waiting for the next subtitle, cuts mean end-to-end latency by 31% on a CPU, and the task-specific vocabulary encodes the same Chinese text in 28% fewer tokens. The deployed model stays within 500 ms for every subtitle in our latency test. The code is available at https://github.com/aiden1020/localsubs .
♻ ☆ Initial Evaluation of Potential Bias in Coverage of Humans in Wikidata
Clair Kronk
Introduction. Our findings reveal significant missingness across the evaluated axes. Ethnicity and sexual orientation were the most critically under-documented (missing statements) while rural birthplaces and non-WENA citizenship were the most underrepresented.

♻ ☆ Artificial intelligence is creating a new global linguistic hierarchy
Giulia Occhini, Kumiko Tanaka-Ishii, Anna Barford, Refael Tikochinski, Songbo Hu, Roi Reichart, Yijie Zhou, ester James V. Miranda, Hannah Claus, Ulla Petti, Ivan Vulić, Ramit Debnath, Anna Korhonen
Artificial intelligence (AI) has the potential to transform healthcare, education, governance and socioeconomic equity, but its benefits remain concentrated in a small number of languages (Bender, 2019; Blasi et al., 2022; Joshi et al., 2020; Ranathunga and de Silva, 2022; Young, 2015). Language AI - the technologies that underpin widely-used conversational systems such as ChatGPT - could provide major benefits if available in people's native languages, yet most of the world's 7,000+ linguistic communities currently lack access and face persistent digital marginalization. Here we present a global longitudinal analysis of social, economic and infrastructural conditions across languages to assess systemic inequalities in language AI. We first analyze the existence of AI resources for 6003 languages. We find that despite efforts of the community to broaden the reach of language technologies (Bapna et al., 2022; Costa-Jussà et al., 2022), the dominance of a handful of languages is exacerbating disparities on an unprecedented scale, with divides widening exponentially rather than narrowing. Further, we contrast the longitudinal diffusion of AI with that of earlier IT technologies, revealing a distinctive hype-driven pattern of spread. To translate our findings into practical insights and guide prioritization efforts, we introduce the Language AI Readiness Index (EQUATE), which maps the state of technological, socio-economic, and infrastructural prerequisites for AI deployment across languages. The index highlights communities where capacity exists but remains underutilized, and provides a framework for accelerating more equitable diffusion of language AI. Our work contributes to setting the baseline for a transition towards more sustainable and equitable language technologies.
♻ ☆ Confidence and Calibration of Activation Oracles for Reliable Interpretation of Language Model Internals
Federico Torrielli, Peter Schneider-Kamp, Lukas Galke Poech
An activation oracle is a language model trained to convert the activations of another model into natural language. However, it is so far unclear to what extent these verbalizations are calibrated. Crucially, the oracle's verbalization come without any measure of confidence. Here, we compare five methods for estimating the confidence of an activation oracle. Our experiments cover four oracles from two model families (Qwen and Gemma, from 8B to 27B parameters), tested on the secret word task, whereby the oracle is supposed to recover a secret word, that has been fine-tuned into a model but does not appear in the model outputs. Our results with $6{,}000$ samples per method/oracle combination show that the best calibration depends on the availability of a hypothesis space or a calibration set: When an assumed auditor can list the possible answers in advance, the oracle should score each candidate answer directly. Scoring the candidates gives a higher accuracy on every oracle and a confidence that separates correct from incorrect answers better than any other method. When the oracle has to write its answer as free text, the agreement over twenty sampled answers gives the best calibrated confidence, which needs only five labeled secret words to be tuned. When a calibration set is available, a rescaled probability of the answer is as good as re-sampling, at the cost of one generation instead of twenty. Asking the oracle to write a number for its confidence, on the other side, gives no useful signal on any oracle. Code and the patched trainer are available at https://github.com/federicotorrielli/probabilistic_activation_oracles.
♻ ☆ A Sociolinguistic Analysis of Automatic Speech Recognition Bias in Newcastle English
Dana Serditova, Kevin Tang
Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) systems are widely used in everyday communication, education, healthcare, and industry, yet their performance remains uneven across speakers, particularly when dialectal variation diverges from the mainstream accents represented in training data. This study investigates ASR bias through a sociolinguistic analysis of Newcastle English, a regional variety of North-East England that has been shown to challenge current speech recognition technologies. Using spontaneous speech from the Diachronic Electronic Corpus of Tyneside English (DECTE), we evaluate the output of a state-of-the-art commercial ASR system and conduct a fine-grained analysis of more than 3,000 transcription errors. Errors are classified by linguistic domain and examined in relation to social variables including gender, age, and socioeconomic status. In addition, an acoustic case study of selected vowel features demonstrates how gradient phonetic variation contributes directly to misrecognition. The results show that phonological variation accounts for the majority of errors, with recurrent failures linked to dialect-specific features like vowel quality and glottalisation, as well as local vocabulary and non-standard grammatical forms. Error rates also vary across social groups, with higher error frequencies observed for men and for speakers at the extremes of the age spectrum. These findings indicate that ASR errors are not random but socially patterned and can be explained from a sociolinguistic perspective. Thus, the study demonstrates the importance of incorporating sociolinguistic expertise into the evaluation and development of speech technologies and argues that more equitable ASR systems require explicit attention to dialectal variation and community-based speech data.

♻ ☆ Pathways of Visual Information Flow in Vision-Language Models
Israfel Salazar, Stella Frank, Dan Oneata, Desmond Elliott, Constanza Fierro
We study how visual information is routed in vision-language models (VLMs). Using causal patching on controlled synthetic and natural datasets, we find that models rely on two distinct pathways to solve visual tasks: A direct pathway, where visual information is retained in image token representations and read out by the final token at later layers, and a text-mediated pathway, where visual information is first transferred to the query tokens and then read out by the final token. Across three visual tasks, we show that pathway selection is task-dependent, and that data distribution and prompt design can also modulate which pathway is used to solve the image-based query. Moreover, using attention knockouts and corrupted-input patching, we find that these pathways are flexible, under certain interventions, models can rely on the text-mediated pathway as a fallback when the usual pathway is ablated. This behavior unifies findings in prior work and shows that ablation-based interventions can reveal what models could do rather than what they normally do. Together, our results provide a mechanistic characterization of visual information flow in VLMs and highlight the flexibility of their internal mechanisms under intervention.
♻ ☆ Beyond Prompt and Parser Engineering: Soft-Prompt Tuning for Automated LLM Evaluation
Selen Erkan, Bastian Boll, Kristian Kersting, Björn Deiseroth, Letitia Parcalabescu
Benchmark scores often misrepresent a large language model's (LLM's) knowledge, because they rely, e.g., on the model's ability to follow specific formatting requirements. This especially penalizes base models that may know the correct answers but lack the ability -- typically introduced in post-training -- to structure them as instructed. To overcome this, we propose an efficient, automated, and architecture-agnostic method for model evaluation based on soft-prompt tuning. By optimizing only 10 soft-prompt vectors (~0.0006% parameters for a 7B model) over a short tuning period, we adapt models to specific benchmark formats, closing gaps in format-following and ensuring that underlying knowledge is accurately reflected in benchmark scores. We evaluate soft-prompt tuning across 7 models and 11 datasets. Our results show that (a) soft-prompt tuning saturates format-following within 150 steps (~1200 samples) while preserving knowledge accuracy, (b) it closely matches strong evaluation-harness baselines on well-studied benchmarks while requiring no manual benchmark-specific prompt or parser design, (c) it improves evaluation signal in settings where baselines are harder to transfer, including long-context datasets and instruction-tuned variants with different output styles, and (d) it produces cleaner outputs that improve agreement between exact match scoring and independent LLM judges. Overall, we propose a lightweight, automated, and flexible protocol based on limited-budget soft-prompt tuning for evaluating LLM knowledge.

♻ ☆ Mimir: A Sub-2B Hierarchical Reasoning Model Trained on Permissible Data Punches Above Its Weight Class
Peter Schneider-Kamp, Jacob Nielsen, Gianluca Barmina, Danial Namazifard, Annemette Brok Pirchert, Kenneth Enevoldsen, Lukas Galke Poech
Training capable language models typically relies on web-scale pretraining followed by multi-stage post-training, creating substantial computational and data-governance barriers for settings in which training data must satisfy explicit permissibility constraints. We introduce Mimir, a 1.78B-parameter language model based on the Hierarchical Reasoning Model Text (HRM-Text) architecture, a recurrent-depth transformer whose layers are split into a low-level (L) and a high-level (H) stack that are applied repeatedly in an interleaved schedule. Mimir is trained from random initialization directly on a curated mixture of instruction, reasoning, tool-use, multilingual, and synthetic data: 161 datasets and 70.5B tokens per epoch for Mimir v1, extended to 103B tokens per epoch for its continued-training successor Mimir v1.5, with synthetic replacements for sources that do not satisfy permissibility criteria. Across 20 benchmarks in English, Danish (a low-resource language with ~6M speakers), and Math & Code, Mimir v1.5 attains the highest average score of all sub-2B models in every domain, outperforming both HRM-Text 1B and the looped transformers Ouro 1.4B and Ouro 2.6B, and Mimir matches or exceeds the performance of models of up to 9B parameters, such as Qwen 3.5 9B and Gemma 4 E4B, on the English and Danish averages. Two independent memorisation audits indicate low memorisation risk despite multi-epoch training. Inference-time ablations show that the number of L-stack applications can be changed with graceful degradation, whereas changing the number of H-stack applications breaks the model. Our results provide evidence that hierarchical recurrent language models can learn effectively from post-training-oriented, permissible data mixtures without conventional web-scale pretraining.

♻ ☆ Poisoning-Based Backdoors Are Not Cryptographic NeurIPS 2026
Masahiro Kaneko
Defending against a backdoor requires recovering the verifier that decides whether an input is a trigger, since blocking individual triggers fails against an attacker who can generate new ones. If a verifier could be installed with the asymmetry of cryptographic authentication, where only the attacker can produce triggers and the defender cannot reconstruct the verifier, defense that learns the verifier from observations would be impossible in principle. We show that no such asymmetry can arise when the verifier enters training only through the labels of poisoned data, which covers the dirty-label backdoors that dominate the literature on language models. A training procedure that installs a verifier from poisoned data is itself a learner for that verifier. Hence any verifier that is hard to learn cannot be installed through poisoning, and any verifier that poisoning can install is reconstructible by a white-box defender holding as many labeled trigger observations as the attacker used. Hiding a secret key in the input or the weights does not change this. Experiments on instruction-tuned large language models confirm the dichotomy, with no configuration yielding a backdoor that is both effective for the attacker and irrecoverable for the defender.
comment: NeurIPS 2026
♻ ☆ Investigating the Impact of Learner Errors on Social Bias in Large Language Models
Federico Marcuzzi, Martin Christoph, Iryna Gurevych, Alla Rozovskaya
Existing social bias benchmarks evaluate large language models mostly on fluent, well-formed English, reflecting native speaker usage and leaving non-native writers unrepresented. To address this gap, we investigate how grammatical errors by non-native writers affect social bias. We develop a controlled framework that injects common learner errors into prompts and apply it to three bias benchmarks covering stereotype alignment and demographic preference across gender, race, and religion. We find that grammatical errors increase bias, and the effect grows with the error rate. Among the four common error types we consider, preposition errors produce the largest and most consistent increase in stereotype alignment. Our analysis suggests that errors increase bias when they are more likely to interfere with comprehension. Finally, fine-tuning on erroneous texts reduces the bias increase caused by grammatical errors. Our findings show that bias evaluations based exclusively on well-formed prompts may underestimate the bias experienced by users whose English contains grammatical errors.
♻ ☆ Interrupting the Loop: Periodic Subject Changes Raise Judged Surprise and Connection in Base Language Models
Roberto I. Ono Filho
Where does the novelty a base language model produces with no task come from, and what can an LLM judge of a long stream actually see? We dismantle a cognitively inspired generation loop over 24 conditions on three base models. Most of its effect lives in one operation: a new subject injected every few hundred tokens (an interruption) into a stream whose literal repetition is damped (habituation). We judge windows of generated text only, with the premise as the unit (n=10) and a judge measured for repeatability, against a second judge family and human readers. Under that protocol the interruption raises judged surprise by 1.2 to 1.4 points and connection by 0.7 to 0.9 over habituation alone. A connective that asks for continuity hurts; a bare paragraph break adds nothing detectable; a reset context does at least as well as a kept one; and a pre-registered replication on new premises confirms the primary contrast. Three things the window judge could not see changed the first version of this study. The judge scores the experimenter's injected sentence as the model's own. A fixed rotation of injected sentences makes the model replay its own earlier segments (65-80% of post-interruption windows at periods 150-300 are mostly verbatim replay), and the judge scores the replay as surprise and, above all, as connection; at period 300 the source of 62% of windows lies beyond the judge's 600-token horizon. And the local gains do not compose: no arm produces an integrated document. The salience monitor, the in-loop judge, memory across interruptions and a gated Review add nothing. On a problem with a verifier (online bin packing), the interruption multiplies valid, distinct candidate heuristics three- to fourfold without raising the quality of the best. We report an evaluation protocol for long generation and a characterization of a simple intervention, not a mechanism of creativity.

♻ ☆ Moving the Mean Toward the Known Good, Not Beyond It: What Inference-Time Interventions and Weight Consolidation Buy in Open-Ended Generation
Roberto I. Ono Filho
What does a generation loop gain from learning on its own verified successes? In cycles of generate, verify, select and LoRA-consolidate on online bin packing, training on value-filtered candidates shifts what the model writes on held-out variants toward value (-1.7 points of excess, p=0.008; -3.1 against a random-consolidation control, p=0.004) while the best observed candidate converges to the classic heuristic's level and no further. A confirmation battery replicates the whole procedure three times, with fresh seeds and a never-consulted held-out set read exactly once: the mean was nearly identical in all three lineages (-2.0, -1.8, -1.9), and after aggregating within held-out variant all seven evaluable variants favored consolidation (p=0.008). The best observed candidate moved to the classic heuristic's level, exactly (0.021028, the lowest variant's classic level, in all three lineages and both arms); attract never went beyond its variant's classic level, while the random control did in 7 of 24 lineage-variant cells. A matched SFT-only control shows the supervised anchor, not repulsion from bad candidates, does the concentrating (96% of candidates land exactly at the classic heuristic's level). On far families the tails cut both ways: consolidation lowers the per-candidate rate of better-than-classic candidates (10% to 3.9%) while its larger production yields more such candidates absolutely (5 against 1, on few events). As motivation we report the inference-time ledger that led here: a model-written schematic recap buys judged document integration and nothing buys development; a verifier written into the stream is imitated, 16.4 fabricated verdict lines per notebook. In this arrangement (an 8-bit model in MLX, five cycles), mean quality among valid candidates can be bought and replicated; under attraction the observed best goes to the classic and, so far, never beyond it.

♻ ☆ AtManRL: Towards Faithful Reasoning via Differentiable Attention Saliency
Max Henning Höth, Kristian Kersting, Björn Deiseroth, Letitia Parcalabescu
Large language models (LLMs) increasingly rely on chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning to solve complex tasks, yet a reasoning trace may merely accompany the final answer rather than causally contribute to it or faithfully reflect how it was reached. We introduce differentiable attention manipulation to train salient reasoning with reinforcement learning -- method which we call AtManRL. By optimizing an additive attention mask that identifies the CoT tokens needed to recover the model's answer, we derive a saliency reward and combine it with an outcome reward in GRPO with decoupled advantages. Because both saliency and faithfulness depend strongly on trace length, we evaluate against a sweep of length-penalty baselines that traces the trade-off a pure length penalty can reach, rather than against a single baseline. Across three models (Qwen2.5-3B, Granite-3.3-2B, and Phi-4-mini) and four benchmarks (GSM8K, MMLU, ProofWriter, and QuestBench), AtManRL sits above this trade-off curve by 3.9-4.0pp in saliency, measured as answer changes under mistake injection, and by 5.7-7.7pp in faithfulness, measured as hint verbalization under hint injection, while matching the accuracy of baselines of similar length. These results suggest that training explicitly for saliency is a promising route towards chain-of-thought reasoning that is more faithful to the model's actual decision process.

♻ ☆ Operator Packages, Proposer Strength, and Construction-Family Plateaus in Office-Scale Verified Search
Roberto I. Ono Filho
Verified search, in which a language model proposes programs, a hard evaluator scores them, and selection keeps the best, has recently moved mathematical records; controlled ablations of the proposer-side components remain rare. We instrument a minimal FunSearch-style loop at office scale (a 30B local model on a laptop, 120-600 verified samples per run) with three operator packages: a schematic notebook the model writes and carries instead of verbatim elites, a named obstacle, and behavioural repulsion from constructions already found. On nine construction problems from a public repository, the complete 2^3 factorial with two replicates favours the primary contrast in a nominal two-stage analysis: the composition closes more of the seed-to-record gap (+0.196; nominal pooled p=0.023, stage-combination p~0.08; median per-problem effect +0.045). Repulsion raises construction-hash diversity in eight of nine problems (one-sided p=0.0039; partly a manipulation check). The factorial finds no positive memory-by-repulsion interaction (bounded to about +/-0.04); the gain decomposes additively, and memory+repulsion is the only arm that never collapses (0 of 18 runs), within 0.025 of the full composition. A frontier proposer under the identical loop reaches in tens of samples what the local model does not in hundreds; in single scoping runs its gains arrive without the operators. The searches stall after closing 85-92% of the gap on the flagship problem, and a later analysis reads that plateau as a resolution limit: the reference value is a continuum limit and the unaided long-run finals already carry its family's structure. In the registered family-hint test the family named in words is adopted and loses; handed as code, it is optimized, but our best finite-grid encoding stays below the plateau reached unaided. We release the harness, every candidate, and the dated pre-registrations.

♻ ☆ Hacking Generative Perplexity: Why Unconditional Text Evaluation Needs New Metrics ICML 2026
Antonio Franca, Alexander Tong
Diffusion language models (DLMs) have emerged as the leading non-autoregressive alternative to language modeling. Because their likelihoods are intractable, generative perplexity (gen-PPL) has become the de facto standard for reporting unconditional generation quality. We analyze why gen-PPL is unreliable as a measure of generation quality. Across samples, it is minimized by always emitting the scorer's most likely sequence. Per-sample unigram entropy does not measure this loss of diversity. Within a sample, gen-PPL rewards predictability under the scorer, which need not correspond to text quality. To make this failure explicit, we construct four training-free samplers that reach or beat trained-model gen-PPL on LM1B and OpenWebText at non-degenerate unigram entropy, while producing text that is incoherent by construction. We then rebenchmark recent DLMs using distributional discrepancies and diversity diagnostics, and compare their rankings with LLM judgments. As part of this evaluation, we introduce a compression-based discrepancy that measures local coverage and fidelity, the two directions of the KL divergence between real and generated text. We argue that DLM progress should be evaluated with a set of metrics that expose different properties of generated text.

♻ ☆ SpatialScout: Can VLM Agents Actively Explore and Map 3D Scenes?
Zhaoxi Zhang, Hao Liang, Linxuan Wu, Yiming Xu, Yifu Yuan, Kun Liang, Chenming Tang, Meiyi Qiang, Wentao Zhang, Weikang Li
Spatial intelligence requires agents to actively acquire visual evidence and integrate observations into a coherent representation of the three-dimensional world. Yet spatial question answering offers limited evidence of whether models can construct accurate metric maps through autonomous exploration. We introduce SpatialScout, a benchmark for evaluating vision-language model (VLM) agents on active object-level 3D mapping. SpatialScout comprises 80 diverse indoor scenes containing 3,140 target objects. Given an object inventory, RGB observations, camera calibration, and camera poses, agents control a camera to explore each scene and estimate object centers in a shared coordinate frame. The task requires coordinating viewpoint selection, cross-view association, geometric estimation, and map revision under bounded interaction budgets. Evaluation jointly measures localization accuracy and target coverage through object-scale overlap scores that penalize missing predictions. Experiments with frontier VLMs reveal substantial limitations: the strongest evaluated model, Claude Opus 5.5, achieves a mean overlap score of 41.71%, with 47.23% of targets exceeding an intersection-over-union (IoU) threshold of 0.5 and only 3.25% exceeding 0.9. These results reveal a substantial gap between recovering coarse object layouts and constructing complete, precise metric maps. SpatialScout provides a quantitative testbed for developing agents that build reliable 3D spatial representations through active perception. Code is available at https://github.com/JesseZZZZZ/SpatialScout.
♻ ☆ PsyCLIENT: Client Simulation via Conversational Trajectory Modeling for Trainee Practice and Model Evaluation in Mental Health Counseling EMNLP 2026

♻ ☆ Beyond Attack Success Rate: Cost-Aware Metric for Evaluating Iterative LLM Jailbreaks
Rohan Menon, Chih-Hong Cheng
Attack success rate (ASR) evaluates jailbreaks against large language models without accounting for repeated calls to the victim model. Yet each additional call gives the deployed system another opportunity to detect the attack and intervene. We introduce $\mathrm{ASR}_{\mathrm{STEP}}$, which penalizes additional victim-model calls while retaining binary success judgments. Our evaluation considers GCG, PAIR with a step budget, and a single-step PAP baseline on JailBreakBench across three model families. GCG's scores decrease by 7.0-8.7 percentage points after discounting, while PAP's remain unchanged. While for PAIR attack, we see a wider discounting range between 3.4-12 percentage points, showing that the "step" quantity differs per iterative strategy. Reporting both metrics makes the discount due to repeated victim-model interaction explicit. Code is available here: https://github.com/SuELS-Group/beyond_asr.

☆ LongZero: Enhancing Long-Context Reasoning in LLMs from Zero Data
Zhennan Wan, Jianfei Chen
Reinforcement learning (RL) is an effective way to strengthen the long-context reasoning of large language models (LLMs), but it needs training data that are long, diverse, challenging, and verifiable. Human annotation of such data is expensive, naturally occurring data are too scarce to sustain RL, and existing synthesis methods depend on external corpora or stronger teacher models, which become harder to obtain as the trained models get stronger. Recently, RL from zero data, in which a model learns from tasks it proposes itself, has achieved excellent performance, but existing methods target short-context tasks. Therefore, we propose $\textbf{LongZero}$, a method that lets an LLM improve its own long-context reasoning without any external data. Instead of writing questions and answers over existing documents, LongZero first has the model synthesize the question and answer with its supporting evidence in a compact structured form, and only then separately expands the evidence into a long multi-document context. LongZero combines these self-written multi-hop reasoning tasks with self-written multi-round retrieval tasks and trains the model with GRPO. We evaluate LongZero on two LLMs across five long-context reasoning benchmarks and compare it with RL on data synthesized from external corpora. Experimental results show that LongZero can generalize to real tasks, and outperforms both baselines, suggesting a viable and promising path to improving long-context reasoning of LLMs.

☆ Think Harder Where It Matters: Item-Level Adaptive Recursion for Sequential Recommendation
Zhanshuo Cao, Jiakai Tang, Yuqi Zhou, Sunhao Dai, Jun Xu
Existing sequential recommenders usually allocate a fixed computational budget to every item in a user's interaction history, regardless of the item's contribution to the final prediction. We challenge this de facto design with two diagnostic findings. (1) Non-uniform computational need: items within the same sequence vary drastically in how much computation they require. Most converge after shallow processing while a critical minority only stabilizes at full depth. (2) Suboptimality of fixed allocation: given oracle access to the target item, a non-uniform depth assignment consistently outranks the fixed one, indicating that existing uniform computation both wastes capacity on already-converged items and sacrifices overall recommendation quality. These observations motivate a shift from fixed-depth computation to item-level adaptive reasoning. To this end, we propose LoopRec. LoopRec recursively applies a single weight-shared block and introduces a lightweight gate at each recursion boundary that makes a binary exit-or-continue decision for every item independently. Items whose representations have sufficiently matured exit early, while those requiring deeper reasoning continue to subsequent recursions. This design converts the fixed global depth into a learned, per-item property without auxiliary depth labels, and is readily adaptable to diverse backbone architectures. Extensive experiments on public benchmarks with four backbone families demonstrate that LoopRec consistently outperforms matched fixed-depth counterparts. Further analysis reveals that LoopRec's learned allocation exhibits interpretable patterns aligned with our post-hoc oracle analysis: lower-frequency items and more recent interactions naturally receive deeper computation. Our code is available at https://github.com/Elpmis117/LoopRec.
☆ Environment Duels: A Microcosm of LLM Co-Evolution
Shibo Jin, Zhiqiu Xu, Yiling Zou, Shangyu Gong, Mayur Naik
Through a tangle of real-world interactions, from synthetic training pipelines to data shared on the open web, frontier LLMs absorb what their predecessors and competitors create. How well does each model play its role in this ecosystem, as a creator and as a solver? We introduce Environment Duels, a dynamic agentic benchmark that evaluates both capabilities on interactive environments. Each model writes environments, each with a privileged hint, aiming for tasks that capable solvers fail without the hint and pass with it. Across nine frontier LLMs, solving and authoring prove to be distinct skills: the strongest solvers are not the strongest authors, while smaller models learn more from how others played and write the hardest environments and the most useful hints. Beyond the scores, the pairwise interactions form a microcosm of how models affect one another. Head-to-head outcomes are transitive and so yield a stable ranking; independent authors converge on the same designs; and in a reinforcement-learning study with Qwen3.8-27B, the learner's hint lift on an author's environments tracks how much training on those environments improves it on held-out reasoning and agentic benchmarks, whether the environments are its own or another model's. Each of these patterns has a counterpart in how models shape one another outside the duel. By staging these interactions under controlled conditions, Environment Duels offers a window onto how LLMs teach, challenge and learn from one another. Code is available at https://github.com/LLM-Duels/EnvDuels.
☆ Quant-Arena: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Quantitative Trading Research
Houde Qian, Zheng Jiang, Wenyi Li, Chenxi Xu, Xinjie Lin, Yuzhao Peng, Ruoyu Han, Youjie Zheng, Kaisen Yang, Qinhuai Na, You Fu, Calvin Xiao
Quantitative research is an iterative loop: a researcher proposes a market hypothesis, implements it as an executable signal, tests it on historical data, and decides what to keep. Existing benchmarks score formulas, code, or individual trading decisions, leaving open whether large language models (LLMs) can complete this loop and produce strategies that work beyond the research period. We introduce Quant-Arena, a benchmark and public leaderboard for end-to-end quantitative research on real A-share market data. A model constructs features and stock-scoring code, receives feedback only on 2022--2023, and freezes one strategy for assessment on the sealed year 2024. A cash-account evaluator applies trading constraints and costs, including market impact estimated from a simulated order book. Scores are dominated by realized excess return and complemented by signal quality and the diversity of features used by the strategy. We evaluate nine frontier models, among which GPT-6 Astra scores highest; yet no model beats the equal-weight market in 2024. Quant-Arena is open to submissions from any model or agent harness.

☆ Did the Problem Actually Get Harder? Testing LLMs on Paired P and NP-Complete Problems Hardened by Adversarial Multi-Agent Rewriting
Nurul Labib Sayeedi, Md. Faiyaz Abdullah Sayeedi, Zabir Al Nazi, Mahbub E Sobhani, Shubhashis Roy Dipta
Many NP-complete (NPC) problems have a polynomial-time (P) twin that differs in a single structural detail, such as 3- versus 2-colouring a graph. A model that reasons rather than recalls should notice which side of this boundary a problem falls on, yet existing benchmarks either fix the wording of each task or ignore its complexity class, so they cannot separate the two. We introduce P2NPCBench, a benchmark of 12 problem families in which every NPC problem is paired with a P twin posed over the same surface. To remove the textbook cues that enable recall, we build it with AgentGAN, an adversarial multi-agent framework in which a generator rewrites each statement until a panel of frozen code models fails, while an executable validity gate keeps every rewrite answer- and class-preserving. The result is 2,114 hardened statements with hand-verified reference human solvers. Evaluating 12 proprietary and open-source models, we find that every model scores lower on NPC statements than on their P twins (35.4% vs. 63.8% pass@1), and that 95.1% of model-family pairs fail more often on the NPC side. A tenfold time limit raises NPC accuracy only from 28.5% to 31.9%, so failures stem from wrong or crashing programs rather than slow ones. Hardening also narrows the class gap from 24.8 to 14.6 points, showing that part of the apparent gap reflects familiarity with textbook phrasing rather than complexity class.

☆ VX-One: Typed, Calibrated Decision Heads for Real-Time Perimeter Triage - An empirical study of unknown-attack honesty, cascade gray zones, and calibration under drift
Juan S. Santillana
Perimeter triage faces a latency/semantics trade-off: signature engines are fast and format-safe but blind to unseen attacks, while generative models are semantic but slow and produce unparseable output. In late 2026 a class of commercial System One decision models (TypeSafe Jev; Microsoft-Decision-1) began selling typed decision primitives - binary gates, closed-set choices, ordinal scores - as product APIs, with no academic treatment. We present VX-One: a small multi-head network (100K-10M parameters) whose outputs are these primitives - a calibrated gate, a choice over a closed candidate set that always includes an explicit unknown option, and an ordinal score, trained multi-task, temperature-calibrated per head (~0.7-1.7 ms p50 on CPU). We evaluate it on UNSW-NB15, CIC-IDS2017, EMBER2024 against calibrated XGBoost, Random-Forest, MLP, Kitsune, and measure a two-phase cascade (Suricata with 52,674 Emerging-Threats rules -> VX-One) over five days of CIC-IDS2017. Findings: (i) VX-One wins detection (AUROC/FPR@95%TPR) on UNSW-NB15 and under temporal drift on CIC-IDS2017 (0.711 vs 0.637), but XGBoost wins multi-class attribution on all three benchmarks; (ii) leave-one-family-out gate detects never-seen families on 9/9 UNSW folds yet drops below chance on CIC recon family, and abstention threshold transfers poorly across benchmarks; (iii) on payload-less reconstructions signature engines resolve ~0.1% of flows - gray zone ~99.9% of traffic, recovered by Phase 2 (combined recall >=0.978); (iv) calibration collapses under temporal drift (ECE 0.04->0.40) for every method while ranking partially survives. We provide a measured account of where typed calibrated decisions help perimeter triage, and where they fail.
