♻ ☆ CanLegalRAGBench: Evaluating Retrieval-Augmented Generation on Canadian Case Law
Ethan Zhao, Maksym Taranukhin, Wei Cui, Moira Aikenhead, Vered Shwartz
RAG-based legal assistants have been growing in popularity, but LLM hallucinations remain a key issue and potentially undermines justice. While benchmarks have been developed to evaluate progress, many rely on synthetic queries rather than realistic legal scenarios. Moreover, Canadian law remains underrepresented in existing evaluations. To address this gap, we introduce CanLegalRAGBench, a Canadian legal QA benchmark based on realistic queries and expert-annotated answers grounded in case law. Our evaluation shows that retrieval performance is sensitive to design choices and that open-source embedding models are competitive with closed source models. However, it also reveals the limitation of automatic evaluations that penalize systems for retrieving alternative relevant documents. ♻ ☆ The Best Programming Language for Tokenmaxxing: An Investigation of Coding Agent Behavior Across Programming Languages
Zixuan Wu, Carolyn Jane Anderson, Arjun Guha
Although coding agents are now very effective in a variety of programming languages, this paper first shows that the cost (in tokens) can very significantly by programming language. We evaluate five recent models on programming problems in Python, Java, Rust, and OCaml. We carefully control for problem difficulty, and show that there can be stark variation in token consumption that is consistent across models. To understand why, we analyze both the structure and content of agent trajectories. First, we re-execute every intermediate solution and abstract each trajectory as a sequence of test-outcome vectors, then label the work between successive solutions. This reveals agents repeatedly producing noncompiling solutions in unfamiliar languages and revising solutions that already pass. Second, we analyze trajectory text, finding that agents plan solutions in code comments, distrust the provided tests in favor of inputs they invent, and sidestep unfamiliar target languages by prototyping in Python. Our results show that by-language token efficiency is a metric that should be considered when benchmarking and developing multilingual agents, and, for the tokenmaxxer, a guide to the most expensive language to work in.
♻ ☆ GENIE: A Fine-Grained Measure for Novelty EMNLP
Ramya Namuduri, Manya Wadhwa, Anshun Asher Zheng, Greg Durrett, Junyi Jessy Li
Large Language Models have consistently demonstrated a lack of creativity and diversity across tasks. Prior work has focused on addressing whether models are capable of generating creative outputs. Here, we aim to consider novelty and investigate what makes model-generated content novel or not novel in a task-specific manner. We propose a fine-grained evaluation metric GENIE to measure the novelty of responses along task-specific features with respect to a population of responses. We show that unlike GENIE, holistic metrics struggle to capture the high-dimensionality of novelty and do not provide insight on which properties they target. Finally, we use GENIE to measure the effectiveness of mitigation methods that address creativity to better understand where these methods can improve novelty.

♻ ☆ From Text to Voice: A Reproducible and Verifiable Framework for Evaluating Tool Calling LLM Agents
Md Tahmid Rahman Laskar, Xue-Yong Fu, Seyyed Saeed Sarfjoo, Quinten McNamara, Jonas Robertson, Shashi Bhushan TN
Voice agents increasingly require reliable tool use from speech, whereas prominent tool-calling benchmarks remain text-based. We study whether verified text benchmarks can be converted into controlled audio-based tool calling evaluations without re-annotating the tool schema and gold labels. Our dataset-agnostic framework uses text-to-speech, speaker variation, and environmental noise to create paired text-audio instances while preserving the original dataset annotations. Based on extensive evaluation of 7 omni-modal models on audio-converted versions of Confetti and When2Call, our framework demonstrates that the performance is strongly model- and task-dependent: Gemini-3.1-Flash-Live obtains the highest Confetti score (70.4), whereas GPT-Realtime-1.5 performs best on When2Call (71.9). On Confetti, the text-to-voice gap ranges from 1.8 points for Qwen3-Omni to 4.8 points for GPT-Realtime-1.5. A targeted analysis of failure cases demonstrates that degradations most often reflect misunderstandings of argument values in the speech. Considering real-world deployment scenarios, we further report text-only results, an ambiguity-based reformulation stress test, and a reference-free LLM-as-judge protocol validated against human preferences. Notably, we find that open-source Qwen3 judges with at least 8B parameters exceed 80% agreement with proprietary judges, supporting privacy-preserving evaluation. Overall, our framework provides a verifiable and reproducible first-stage diagnostic that complements purpose-built audio corpora.

♻ ☆ DialFork: Going Beyond Factuality to Evaluate Procedural Hallucination in Conversational Agents
Sergio Burdisso, Esaú Villatoro-Tello, Dairazalia Sánchez-Cortés, Yanis Labrak, Jordi Luque, Milos Cernak, Petr Motlicek
Large language models (LLMs) deployed as conversational agents are increasingly expected to follow procedural logic. While factual hallucination has received extensive attention, a different failure mode has been overlooked: procedural hallucination, in which an agent produces individually fluent and factually plausible utterances while deviating from the intended conversational process. We introduce DialFork, the first benchmark to measure this failure mode on raw dialogue text. DialFork is built by forking conformant dialogues at controlled turn positions and injecting one of four hallucination types, yielding 36,278 dialogues across 24 tasks in 12 domains; 30 human annotators validate the construction. We define three evaluation tasks that characterize procedural deviation at three granularities: how far a set of dialogues deviates from conformant ones (monitoring deployed agents), how far a single dialogue does (procedural retrieval), and at which turn a dialogue first goes off-track (real-time guardrails). For the first two, we propose dialog-flow-aware metrics, FlowKL and FlowScore, that compare dialogues by how they unfold rather than their content. At the dialogue level, FlowScore significantly outperforms all baselines across seven encoders, showing that semantic similarity is no substitute for procedural similarity. We release DialFork under open licenses at https://github.com/idiap/dialfork, hoping the community will measure conversational process alongside content.
♻ ☆ Trees from Marginals: Autoregressive drafting with factorized priors
Yuma Oda, Ryan Mathieu, Roman Knyazhitskiy, Artur Chakhvadze
Speculative decoding greatly increases the interactivity of autoregressive language models by trading off computation for extra tokens generated in a single forward pass. Factorized draft models are especially efficient because they predict future-token marginals in parallel, but their independence assumption causes acceptance rates to degrade sharply as the speculative budget grows. We analyze this limitation and introduce Weaver, a lightweight autoregressive adapter that constructs proposal trees from the top-K marginals of a factorized drafter. Weaver restores conditional dependencies between proposed tokens while avoiding a full-vocabulary projection. To support fast verification for models with Gated Delta Net layers, we derive a rollback-free tree-verification algorithm and implement optimized CUDA kernels in SGLang. By combining these model and systems contributions we achieve a 4.08-fold speedup over autoregressive decoding, and outperform a highly optimized DFlash baseline by 16.3%.
♻ ☆ EHRNote-ChatQA: A Benchmark for Evidence-Grounded Multi-Turn Clinical Question Answering over Longitudinal Discharge Summaries
Jiyoun Kim, Muhan Yeo, Eunhye Jang, Jeewon Yang, Hangyul Yoon, Su Ji Lee, Hee Jo Han, Hee-Jae Jung, Doyun Kwon, Jun young Lee, Jaehun Lee, Jung-Oh Lee, Sunjun Kweon, Jong Hak Moon, Daseul Kim, Minjae Cho, Edward Choi
Discharge summaries are crucial clinical documents containing the context of a patient's overall hospital stay, and are routinely reviewed by medical experts for patient readmission, ongoing care, and diagnostic decision-making. When reviewing them, medical experts often must iteratively synthesize information across multiple discharge summaries while verifying the evidence supporting each answer. Although large language models (LLMs) are increasingly explored for clinical question answering, existing benchmarks do not sufficiently reflect this setting: they often evaluate exam-style medical knowledge or focus on single-turn question answering with limited evidence-grounding evaluation. We introduce EHRNote-ChatQA, the first benchmark for evidence-grounded multi-turn clinical question answering over patients' multiple discharge summaries. Built from de-identified MIMIC-IV discharge summaries, EHRNote-ChatQA contains 967 patient-level multi-turn samples spanning one to five notes and 16,072 medical-expert-verified QA pairs (8,036 content questions, each paired with an evidence-grounding question) across eight clinical categories. The benchmark is constructed through an expert-informed pipeline combining discharge-summary structuring schema, expert-curated multi-turn QA templates, and LLM-based generation, followed by review and revision of every single QA sample by 12 medical experts (11 physicians and 1 nurse). Benchmarking 24 proprietary and open-weight LLMs reveals several challenges, including that LLMs struggle more with evidence grounding than content answering, multi-turn errors compound across turns, and single-turn clinical QA performance does not reliably transfer to this setting. These findings establish EHRNote-ChatQA as a rigorous and practical benchmark for evaluating clinical QA systems.

Shantipriya Parida, Debasish Dhal, Sonal Khosla, Sambit Sekhar, Muhammed Abdur Rahmaan Kamaldeen

♻ ☆ When Does Tool Use Increase the Expressive Power of Finite-Precision Recurrent Models?
Nikola Zubić, Qian Li, Yuyi Wang, Davide Scaramuzza
Modern sequence models are increasingly deployed as agents that interleave token generation with calls to external tools. We study when such tool access increases computational expressivity. We model any fixed finite-precision recurrent sequence model, including finite-precision state-space models (SSMs) with $B$ bits of internal state, as a deterministic finite-state controller interacting with an oracle through a finite command/observation interface, and establish a separation between the two regimes analyzed. First, every finite-state bounded-interface tool with memory set $M$ can be internalized at a cost of at most $\lceil\log_2|M|\rceil+O(1)$ recurrent bits, so the combined system remains finite-state. Second, a single unbounded tape with only local $\mathtt{read}$/$\mathtt{write}$/$\mathtt{move}$ commands suffices for Turing completeness: every single-tape Turing machine with state set $Q$ and tape alphabet $Γ$ is simulated by a controller with $O(\log|Q|+\log|Γ|)$ recurrent bits, and $\mathrm{EQ}_n$ requires $2^n$ states without tools but one constant-size controller with the tape. Third, this construction is realized exactly by a one-layer finite-precision \emph{selective} affine SSM with binary one-hot states, $\{0,1\}$ transition matrices, and zero biases. Under explicitly stated resource conventions, $O(\log B)$ recurrent bits suffice for tape-assisted simulation of any $B$-state Turing machine, whereas directly realizing an arbitrary $B$-state transition map in one exact affine update without external memory requires total recurrent dimension exactly $B-1$. Fixed description size, occupied tape cells, and interaction cost are reported separately from recurrent memory. Our results concern exact expressivity under deterministic semantics, not learnability or empirical performance.

♻ ☆ Multi-Label Sentiment Analysis on 100 Languages with Dynamic Weighting for Label Imbalance
Selim F. Yilmaz, E. Batuhan Kaynak, Aykut Koç, Hamdi Dibeklioğlu, Suleyman S. Kozat
We investigate cross-lingual sentiment analysis, which has attracted significant attention due to its applications in various areas including market research, politics and social sciences. In particular, we introduce a sentiment analysis framework in multi-label setting as it obeys Plutchik's wheel of emotions. We introduce a novel dynamic weighting method that balances the contribution from each class during training, unlike previous static weighting methods that assign non-changing weights based on their class frequency. Moreover, we adapt the focal loss that favors harder instances from single-label object recognition literature to our multi-label setting. ♻ ☆ Protecting De-identified Documents from Search-based Linkage Attacks
Pierre Lison, Mark Anderson
While de-identification models can help conceal the identity of the individuals mentioned in a document, they fail to address linkage risks, defined as the potential to map the de-identified text back to its source. One straightforward way to perform such linkages is to extract phrases from the de-identified document and check their presence in the original dataset. This paper presents a method to counter search-based linkage attacks while preserving the semantic integrity of the text. The method proceeds in two steps. We first construct an inverted index of the N-grams occurring in the text collection, making it possible to efficiently determine which N-grams appear in fewer than $k$ documents, either alone or in combination with other N-grams. An LLM-based rewriter is then iteratively queried to reformulate those spans until linkage is no longer possible. Experimental results on two datasets (court cases and Wikipedia biographies) show that the rewriting method can effectively prevent search-based linkages while remaining faithful to the original content. However, we also highlight that linkages remain feasible with the help of more advanced, semantics-oriented approaches.
♻ ☆ RubRIX: Rubric-Driven Risk Mitigation in Caregiver-AI Interactions
Drishti Goel, Jeongah Lee, Qiuyue Joy Zhong, Violeta J. Rodriguez, Daniel S. Brown, Ravi Karkar, Dong Whi Yoo, Koustuv Saha
Caregivers seeking AI-mediated support express complex needs---information-seeking, emotional validation, and distress cues---that warrant careful evaluation of response safety and appropriateness. Existing AI evaluation frameworks, primarily focused on general risks (toxicity, hallucinations, policy violations, etc), may not adequately capture the nuanced risks of LLM-responses in caregiving-contexts. We introduce RubRIX (Rubric-based Risk Index), a theory-driven, clinician-validated framework for evaluating risks in LLM caregiving responses. Grounded in the Elements of an Ethic of Care, RubRIX operationalizes five empirically-derived risk dimensions: Inattention, Bias & Stigma, Information Inaccuracy, Uncritical Affirmation, and Epistemic Arrogance. We evaluate six state-of-the-art LLMs on over 20,000 caregiver queries from Reddit and ALZConnected. Rubric-guided refinement consistently reduced risk-components by 45-98% after one iteration across models. This work contributes a methodological approach for developing domain-sensitive, user-centered evaluation frameworks for high-burden contexts. Our findings highlight the importance of domain-sensitive, interactional risk evaluation for the responsible deployment of LLMs in caregiving support contexts. We release benchmark datasets to enable future research on contextual risk evaluation in AI-mediated support.
♻ ☆ AnnoCaseLaw: Legal Annotation Benchmark of Civil Negligence
Rilton Franzone, Magnus Sesodia, Alina Petrova, John Armour, Thomas Lukasiewicz, Oana-Maria Camburu, Puneet K. Dokania, Philip Torr, Christian Schroeder de Witt, Fabio J. Fehr
Language models offer a way to scale costly, labour-intensive expert annotation, but their ability to reproduce expert judgements remains uncertain. This is particularly challenging in law, where annotators must distinguish the reasoning supporting judicial decisions from other legally relevant discussion. We present AnnoCaseLaw as a densely annotated benchmark comprising $471$ U.S. civil-negligence court opinions with more than $12,000$ expert highlights across five annotation categories. We evaluate proprietary frontier, open-weight, legal-specialised, and decision models on their ability to reproduce these annotations. Models perform better on Facts and Outcome than on Application of Law to Facts, Procedural History, and Relevant Precedents. On Application of Law to Facts, proprietary frontier models achieve the strongest balance of reasoning coverage and precision, while the open-weight model Gemma 4 achieves comparable Macro-F1 to proprietary frontier models at a fraction of the estimated inference cost. Our findings highlight the importance of evaluating annotation coverage and cost alongside aggregate scores when assessing the feasibility of automated legal annotation.

♻ ☆ Latent Bridges for Multi-Table Question Answering
Simone Varriale, Tamara Cucumides, Floris Geerts, Paolo Papotti
We introduce GRAB, a constructor-encoder-bridge pipeline for table question answering. Our method lifts relational data into an heterogeneous graph, encodes it via message passing, and transfers the signals to an LLM through a small set of query-conditioned latent tokens. This provides the LLM with a compact, task-relevant structural representation together with the flattened text. Crucially, the LLM remains strictly frozen to preserve its general reasoning capabilities; we train only the lightweight graph encoder and latent bridge (91M parameters), allowing the entire pipeline to be trained efficiently. Our pipeline significantly improves performance on relational Question Answering, with the largest gains in demanding multi-table settings, offering an efficient, principled way to connect relational deep learning with LLMs.
♻ ☆ Exploring Post-Training Alignment of Small Language Models for Biomedical Data-to-Text Generation: A Case Study of Medication Leaflet
Xi Yang, Guodong Liu, Chuqin Li, Fan Wu, Ergin Soysal, Min Jiang, Xing He, Jiang Bian, Yi Guo, Shams Zaman, Thomas Fuchs, Yonghui Wu
Translating structured biomedical information into accurate, patient-friendly narratives remains a challenging biomedical data-to-text (D2T) task. While small language models (SLMs) are attractive for healthcare applications because of their lower computational cost, easier deployment, and improved data governance, the impact of post-training alignment on biomedical D2T generation has not been systematically studied. In this work, we compare four alignment methods: supervised fine-tuning (SFT), direct preference optimization (DPO), odds ratio preference optimization (ORPO), and group relative policy optimization (GRPO), using Qwen-2.5 models for medication leaflet generation. We further introduce FDALeaflets, a dataset derived from OpenFDA drug labels, to evaluate cross-regulatory generalization in a zero-shot setting. Selected aligned SLMs outperform GPT-5 on several reference-based metrics, whereas GPT-5 achieves better hallucination and adequacy scores on FDALeaflets under LLM-as-judge evaluation. Among RL-free alignment methods, ORPO achieves the best in-domain semantic performance, reaching a semantic textual similarity (STS) score of 0.9752. GRPO achieves the highest FDALeaflets STS (0.9407), exceeding GPT-5 (0.9295). These findings show that post-training alignment is a key factor in improving both semantic fidelity and generalization for biomedical D2T generation, while clinical safety and patient readability require human verification.

♻ ☆ Language Models Give Group-Linked Fields the Wrong Weight
Zengqing Wu, Chuan Xiao
Large language models are increasingly used to judge people, and their answers can depend on inputs correlated with a protected attribute such as race, even when that attribute is never shown. Interpreting this reliance requires knowing how much predictive information those inputs add, but audits often report only whether a decision changes. We introduce an evidence-referenced audit that compares proxy reliance with two references, the known outcome rule and a Bayesian linear regression fitted to the same labeled examples, while measuring ranking accuracy separately. We apply it to four models in a simulated patient-ranking task with known field weights. We find four results. First, at zero predictive value, the models still relied on fields that carried no information, while the same-example learner did not. Second, as proxies became predictive, reliance grew far less than the reference. Third, display format mattered. Ordered labels drew more reliance than arbitrary labels, a direction that held in all four models. Fourth, ranking accuracy did not consistently track these changes in reliance. Based on these findings, we argue that decision changes and accuracy cannot show whether proxy reliance is warranted by the evidence. Audits should report reliance against an explicit predictive reference, alongside accuracy. Ordered fields whose values move with group membership, such as scores or income bands, are a useful starting point. Together, these results make it possible to measure how far reliance departs from the evidence, rather than infer it from observed choices alone.
♻ ☆ Neither Stochastic Parroting nor AGI: LLMs Solve Tasks through Context-Directed Extrapolation from Training Data Priors
Harish Tayyar Madabushi, Melissa Torgbi, Claire Bonial
This position paper argues that many behaviours framed as either "stochastic parroting" or "advanced reasoning" are better explained as context-directed extrapolation from training-data priors, and that ML and NLP research communities would benefit from evaluations that distinguish remembering and basic extrapolation from genuine application-level generalisation. Our central contribution is an actionable evaluation checklist and design patterns that make these distinctions testable in practice. Our framework posits that LLMs extrapolate from training data priors while using context to guide the model to the appropriate priors; we call this "context-directed extrapolation." Specifically, this context direction is achieved through examples in base models, leading to in-context learning, while instruction tuning allows analogous task specification via prompts rather than explicit examples. We argue that adopting this more realistic framework and controls for improving evaluation would better calibrate claims about LLM "reasoning," refocus research on how LLM performance interacts with training data and generalisability to out-of-domain tasks, and reduce miscalibration in deployment and policy decisions.

♻ ☆ Replayable Financial Agents: A Determinism-Faithfulness Assurance Harness for Tool-Using LLM Agents ICLR
Raffi Khatchadourian
Replaying a financial agent requires specifying the conditions of a rerun, preserving its execution record, and deciding which parts of its behavior to compare. We introduce the Determinism-Faithfulness Assurance Harness (DFAH), a framework and practical protocol for organizing these choices. The framework distinguishes comparisons of final decisions, tool-name sets, and ordered tool names, while treating support from available evidence as a separate evaluation target. We illustrate the framework using 2,957 recorded executions in synthetic compliance-triage and data-operations tasks. One data-operations configuration repeated its recorded decision across all trials on 84% of cases, while its ordered tool-name sequence repeated on 26%. Conversely, some compliance cases retained the same tool sequence while recorded decisions changed. A case-level example shows how repeated labels accompanied different lookups and escalation calls. These observations demonstrate how the comparison level changes the description of repeated behavior. The resulting protocol connects configuration control, preservation of each attempt, comparison of records, and inspection of changed cases. DFAH provides a practical foundation for designing replay experiments and locating execution differences during the development and evaluation of financial agents.

♻ ☆ Linguistic Productivity in Large Language Models: Models Coerce, but do not Preempt
Claire Bonial, Claire Benet Post, Laura Michaelis, Harish Tayyar Madabushi
Usage-based theories of grammar explain language users' ability to interpret and produce novel combinations of words through two complementary inferential strategies. In cases of coercion, a language user, when encountering a word that is mismatched to its grammatical context, reinterprets that word in a way that resolves the conflict. In cases of preemption, the user infers that an otherwise plausible structure is unavailable or dispreferred because it fails to occur in contexts where it would be expected. Large Language Models are also usage-based, in the sense that the structures of language are learned through exposure to vast amounts of text. Here, we test whether or not the opposing forces of coercion and preemption also encourage and constrain linguistic productivity in LLMs. We demonstrate across models of various sizes and architectures that larger models recognize constructional productivity in cases of coercion, even when these involve novel words. However, we also show that even the largest models do not replicate the conservative language strategy of preemption for novel words: in particular, they do not appear to draw on negative evidence when making choices among competing grammatical options. We conclude that coercion is detectable because it is both surprising and semantically motivated: when a word is mismatched to its syntactic context, the word's meaning can be adjusted to fit the context. Preemption has no such signature. There is no record of the lexeme-construction pairings that were avoided--only of those that were produced. This asymmetry helps to explain why models coerce (create mismatched sign combinations) but do not preempt (avoid unconventional yet semantically defensible ones).
comment: 18 pages, 9 tables (including Appendix)
♻ ☆ A rubric landscape for evaluating clinical reasoning in large language models: what exists, what is missing, and what needs to be combined
Zhangshu Joshua Jiang, Zina Ibrahim, James T. Teo
Exam-style accuracy does not establish whether large language models (LLMs) reason well over clinical records. We define clinical reasoning as integrating and updating evidence across time and sources to form, revise and justify a patient's problem representation and a defensible plan. This structured narrative review maps three literatures: medical education assessment instruments, clinical LLM benchmarks published from 2023 onwards, and general-domain methods for evaluating long-form generation. We examine six dimensions: problem representation, temporal synthesis, differential and management reasoning, counterfactual reasoning, calibrated uncertainty, and reasoning faithfulness. Preprints are included and flagged. No single instrument covers all six dimensions. Problem representation and differential or management reasoning are reasonably covered, although reliability varies by instrument and setting. TIMER-Eval targets temporal synthesis, and ER-Reason assesses sequential diagnostic belief updating. Dedicated uncertainty and counterfactual evaluations are emerging, but their applicability to longitudinal free-text reasoning remains limited. Factual completeness is well theorised in general-domain evaluation, with early clinical evidence of important omissions. Faithfulness remains the weakest dimension, with one identified clinical causal-ablation study on multiple-choice questions. Existing tools should be combined through binary rubric items, separate completeness and correctness scores, case-specific importance weighting with non-compensable safety caps, temporal order-consistency checks, and chance-corrected reliability reporting. Further design work is needed for calibrated uncertainty, counterfactual reasoning and faithfulness over longitudinal free-text records. This review provides a design rationale, not a validated instrument.

♻ ☆ Analyzing and Improving Chain-of-Thought Monitorability Through Information Theory
Usman Anwar, Tim Bakker, Dana Kianfar, Cristina Pinneri, Christos Louizos
Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitors are LLM-based systems that analyze reasoning traces to detect when outputs may exhibit attributes of interest, such as test-hacking behavior during code generation. In this paper, we use information-theoretic analysis to show that non-zero mutual information between CoT and output is a necessary but not sufficient condition for CoT monitorability. We identify two sources of approximation error that may undermine the performance of CoT monitors in practice: information gap, which measures the extent to which the monitor can extract the information available in CoT, and elicitation error, which measures the extent to which the monitor approximates the optimal monitoring function. Empirically, we show that CoT obfuscation can arise even in the absence of any optimization pressure against CoT. We further demonstrate that the fragility of CoT monitorability can be systematically mitigated through targeted training objectives. To this end, we propose a practical, label-free objective based on mutual information between outputs and CoTs, and benchmark it against an oracle-based method that directly rewards the monitored model for CoTs that maximize monitor accuracy. Across multiple environments, we show that our objective is effective at preventing CoT obfuscation even when training against a monitor, and helps mitigate reward hacking when the task reward is imperfectly specified.

♻ ☆ The Stepwise Informativeness Assumption: When Do Entropy Dynamics Track Reasoning Correctness in LLMs?
Mar Gonzàlez I Català, Haitz Sáez de Ocáriz Borde, George D. Montañez, Pietro Liò
Recent work uses entropy-based signals at multiple representation levels to study reasoning in large language models, but the field remains largely empirical. From a theoretical perspective, reductions in model uncertainty do not necessarily reflect progress toward correctness. In this work, we characterize when such alignment can be expected through the Stepwise Informativeness Assumption (SIA), which requires entropy-reducing reasoning-prefix extensions increase the probability assigned to valid answers in expectation. We show that next-token prediction can transfer answer-relevant information from training data to the model, but does not, by itself, guarantee alignment with correctness. Instead, we propose that this alignment depends on how the training regime couples reasoning traces to correct answers. Furthermore, we hypothesize observable signatures of this phenomenon and characterize its expected failure regimes. We empirically investigate how entropy dynamics relate to correctness across four reasoning benchmarks (GSM8K, ARC, SVAMP, MATH) and diverse open-weight language models spanning base, instruction-tuned, and RL-trained regimes. Our results show that entropy-correctness alignment strengthens with post-training but can weaken when task difficulty exceeds model capabilities.

♻ ☆ Output Vector Editing for Memorization Mitigation in Large Language Models
Ahmad Dawar Hakimi, Kaiwei Lei, Isabelle Augenstein, Hinrich Schütze
Large language models can reproduce training text, creating privacy, copyright, and security risks. We propose Output Vector Editing (OVE), a post-hoc method for suppressing text continuations that a model reproduces from a given prefix. Under superposition, a neuron can contribute to multiple features. Yet some neuron-level mitigation methods suppress localized neurons by zeroing their activations, removing each neuron's entire contribution. OVE instead edits their output vectors, combining (i) two-stage localization of MLP layers and neurons with (ii) full-vocabulary least-squares updates. Its multi-anchor variant, OVE-MA, adaptively selects continuation positions expected to provide stronger suppression relative to the required weight change. We evaluate recovery and locality across four models (360M--7B parameters), using autoregressively validated continuations and recovery tests with greedy decoding, beam search, and sampling. On 634 OLMo-7B pairs, OVE-MA with at most five output-vector updates per request leaves 24.45\% recoverable in these tests, compared with 32.02\% for the closest prior post-hoc editing method, REVS, while using approximately 75 times fewer update operations. These results show that adaptive position selection can concentrate effective suppression into a small number of weight updates. OVE edits weights without gradients and requires no additional intervention during inference.
♻ ☆ PersonalAI 2.0: Enhancing knowledge graph traversal/retrieval with planning mechanism for Personalized LLM Agents
Mikhail Menschikov, Matvey Iskornev, Alexander Kharitonov, Alina Bogdanova, Mikhail Belkin, Ekaterina Lisitsyna, Artyom Sosedka, Victoria Dochkina, Ruslan Kostoev, Ilia Perepechkin, Evgeny Burnaev
We introduce PersonalAI 2.0 (PAI-2), a novel framework designed to enhance LLM-based systems through integration of external knowledge graphs (KGs). The proposed approach addresses key limitations of existing Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) methods by incorporating a dynamic, multistage pipeline. The central point of the PAI-2 design is its ability to perform adaptive, iterative information search, guided by extracted entities, matched graph vertices and generated clue-queries. Conducted evaluation on five benchmarks (Natural Questions, TriviaQA, HotpotQA, 2WikiMultihopQA and MuSiQue) demonstrates an improvement in the factual correctness of generated answers compared to baseline methods (LightRAG, RAPTOR, HippoRAG 2 and PAI-1). PAI-2 achieves a 9 percentage points (pp) average gain by LLM-as-a-Judge on 2WikiMultihopQA and MuSiQue benchmarks, and attains comparable accuracy to HippoRAG 2 on the TriviaQA and HotpotQA benchmarks with Qwen2.5 7B and multilingual-e5-large reflecting its effectiveness in reducing hallucination rates and increasing recall. We show that enabled search plan enhancement mechanism gain in average a 18 pp boost compared to disabled one by LLM-as-a-Judge across five benchmarks. In addition, an ablation study reveals that PAI-2 achieves superior result on the MINE-1 benchmark, obtaining an 89\% information-retention score with Qwen2.5 7B and multilingual-e5-large. Collectively, these findings underscore the potential of PAI-2 to serve as a reusable component for personalized AI applications, which require context-aware knowledge-representation and reasoning capabilities. The source code of PAI-2 is available at the following link: https://github.com/Dzigen/PersonalAI.
♻ ☆ Effects of Theory of Mind and Prosocial Beliefs on Steering Human-Aligned Behaviors of LLMs in Ultimatum Games AACL
Neemesh Yadav, Yihuai Lan, Shan Dong, Mai Hieu Hien, Palakorn Achananuparp, Jing Jiang, Ee-Peng Lim
Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown potential in simulating human behaviors and performing theory-of-mind (ToM) reasoning, crucial for complex social interactions. We investigate ToM reasoning's role in aligning agentic behaviors with human norms in negotiation tasks, using the ultimatum game as our referenced task. We initialized LLM agents with different prosocial beliefs (Greedy, Fair, Selfless) and reasoning methods (chain of thought and ToM reasoning of varying levels), examining their decision-making processes and outcomes across multiple LLMs, including reasoning models like o3-mini and DeepSeek-R1 Distilled Qwen 32B. We perform 2,700 simulations to show that ToM reasoning enhances behavioral alignment with human norms, decision-making consistency, and negotiation outcomes. Consistent with prior findings, reasoning LLMs exhibit limited capability compared to ToM-enhanced LLMs, with different game roles benefiting from different ToM orders. Agents almost always chose strategies consistent with their assigned beliefs, and doing so aligned better with human norms in nearly every role, yet Greedy and Selfless agents still fell short of their norms in outcomes. Human verification further revealed that Llama 3.3 70B produces reasoning most consistent with its actions and beliefs. Our findings advance understanding of ToM's role in human-AI interaction and cooperative decision-making. The code used for our experiments can be found at https://github.com/Stealth-py/UltimatumToM.

♻ ☆ Can LLMs Normalize Databases? A Benchmark and Multi-Agent Framework for Schema Normalization
Dong-Jae Koh, Huisu Kim, SeongHwan Yoon, Lasse M. Jantsch, Chun-Hee Lee, Seonghyeon Lee, Young-Kyoon Suh
Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate structured outputs, but their reliability remains unclear when those outputs must satisfy database-level constraints. We study this issue through database normalization, involving reasoning about functional dependencies, lossless join decompositions, and inter-table constraints. We introduce a Database Normalization Benchmark (DNBENCH), comprising 3,275 samples for evaluating LLM-driven database normalization from 1NF to BCNF. DNBENCH uses a three-axis protocol to measure semantic equivalence, structural accuracy, and logical validity. Across Single, Complex, and Real World levels, DNBENCH uncovers recurring failures in dependency inference, schema decomposition, and inter-table constraint reconstruction. We further propose Multi-Agent Reasoning for Schemas (MARS), which separates evidence extraction, violation diagnosis, and decomposition planning from schema generation and verification. MARS improves the DNB-SCORE by 82.0% over the single-prompt baseline. All artifacts will be released upon acceptance.
♻ ☆ TabAgent: Distilling LLM Agent Components into Cheap Classifiers
Ido Levy, Segev Shlomov, Avi Yaeli, Nir Mashkif, Asaf Adi, Yinon Goldshtein, Eilam Shapira, Leshem Choshen
Agentic systems, AI architectures that autonomously execute multi-step workflows to achieve complex goals, are often built using repeated large language model (LLM) calls for closed-set decision tasks such as routing, shortlisting, gating, and verification. While convenient, this design makes deployments slow and expensive due to cumulative latency and token usage. We propose TabAgent, a framework for replacing generative decision components in closed-set selection tasks with a compact textual-tabular classifier trained on execution traces. TabAgent (i) extracts structured schema, state, and dependency features from trajectories (TabSchema), (ii) augments coverage with schema-aligned synthetic supervision (TabSynth), and (iii) scores candidates with a lightweight classifier (TabHead). On the long-horizon AppWorld benchmark, TabAgent maintains task-level success while eliminating shortlist-time LLM calls, reducing latency by approximately 95% and inference cost by 85-91%. Beyond tool shortlisting, TabAgent generalizes to other agentic decision heads, establishing a paradigm for learned discriminative replacements of generative bottlenecks in production agent architectures.

♻ ☆ AfrIFact: Cultural Information Retrieval, Evidence Extraction and Fact Checking for African Languages EMNLP 2026
Israel Abebe Azime, Jesujoba Oluwadara Alabi, Crystina Zhang, Iffat Maab, Atnafu Lambebo Tonja, Tadesse Destaw Belay, Folasade Peace Alabi, Salomey Osei, Saminu Mohammad Aliyu, Nkechinyere Faith Aguobi, Bontu Fufa Balcha, Blessing Kudzaishe Sibanda, Davis David, Mouhamadane Mboup, Daud Abolade, Neo Putini, Philipp Slusallek, David Ifeoluwa Adelani, Dietrich Klakow
Assessing the veracity of a claim made online is a complex and important task with real-world implications. When these claims are directed at communities with limited access to information and the content concerns issues such as healthcare and culture, the consequences intensify, especially in low-resource languages. In this work, we introduce AfrIFact, a dataset that covers the necessary steps for automatic fact-checking (i.e., information retrieval, evidence extraction, and fact checking), in ten African languages and English. Our evaluation results show that even the best embedding models lack cross-lingual retrieval capabilities, and that cultural and news documents are easier to retrieve than healthcare-domain documents, both in large corpora and in single documents. We show that LLMs lack robust multilingual fact-verification capabilities in African languages, while few-shot prompting improves performance by up to 43% in AfriqueQwen-14B, and task-specific fine-tuning further improves fact-checking accuracy by up to 26%. These findings, along with our release of the AfrIFact dataset, encourage work on low-resource information retrieval, evidence retrieval, and fact checking.

♻ ☆ A Robust Evaluation of Probe Robustness: Lessons for Reliable OOD Uncertainty Quantification EMNLP 2026
Joe Stacey, Hadas Orgad, Kentaro Inui, Benjamin Heinzerling, Nafise Sadat Moosavi
Recent work has shown that the hidden states of large language models contain signals useful for uncertainty estimation, motivating a growing interest in efficient probe-based approaches. Yet it remains unclear how robust existing methods are, with prior work reporting conflicting conclusions under substantially different evaluation settings. We address this by introducing ProbeDrift, a systematic evaluation framework for supervised uncertainty probes covering a wide range of OOD settings across models, tasks, and distributional shifts. Using ProbeDrift, we train over 2,000 probes to disentangle the effect of key design choices, showing poor robustness of current methods beyond near-OOD settings. We find that robustness is driven by design decisions that have a largely invisible effect in-distribution, including the choice of feature type, aggregation strategy, and training signal. We argue that robust uncertainty estimation requires robust evaluation. To support this, we release ProbeDrift as a lightweight Python library that contains the train and test splits underpinning our extensive evaluation. We also show how insights from our evaluation can directly lead to more robust methods through a simple Hybrid Back-Off (HBO) strategy.

♻ ☆ What Transfers from Text to Vision? Capability Scaling Laws and Transfer Dynamics for VLMs
Ziran Li, Qiang Wang, Zhengyu Chen, Shanglin Lei, Borun Chen, Jingang Wang, Xunliang Cai
Choosing the right large language model (LLM) backbone is the most consequential decision when building a vision-language model (VLM), yet it remains fundamentally unprincipled: compute-based scaling laws fail to generalize across model families, and no framework exists for directly predicting VLM performance before training begins. We propose the Capability-Driven Multimodal Scaling Law, the first cross-family framework that predicts VLM benchmark accuracy from directly observable textual capability. Given a low-dimensional capability score $S$ extracted from LLM textual benchmarks via PCA, we model VLM performance as a function of $S$, with a per-backbone transfer rate and an absorption rate that quantifies data-scaling efficiency. To fit and validate the framework, we train over 150 VLMs on 34 LLMs spanning 7 model families under a strictly controlled recipe. Evaluations on more than 200 textual and 50 multimodal benchmarks show that the law accurately extrapolates transfer rate from models up to 8B parameters to 72B-scale backbones, predicts full VLM training trajectories with high fidelity, and generalizes to entirely held-out model families. Beyond the scaling law, our analysis surfaces actionable insights: certain textual benchmarks negatively correlate with multimodal performance, exposing latent benchmark-gaming behavior; base LLMs outperform instruction-tuned counterparts as VLM backbones due to higher absorption rates and lower data-scaling decay; and different model families occupy distinct positions in the transfer--absorption space. The framework turns backbone selection from costly empirical sweeps into a principled, quantitative decision. Code and data are available at https://github.com/wangq-dev/CDMScaling.
♻ ☆ From Value Conditioning to Behavioral Shift: Lightweight Value Alignment of LLMs
Shangrui Nie, Florian Mai, David Kaczér, Charles Welch, Zhixue Zhao, Lucie Flek
Large language models exhibit value-related preferences in their outputs, yet steering such behavior often requires large training datasets or paired preference annotations. In this work, we investigate a lightweight alternative: can fine-tuning on value survey responses induce measurable shifts in value-conditioned model outputs and downstream behavior? We first estimate baseline value profiles for several open-source LLMs by asking them to rate value-related descriptions organized around the ten Schwartz values. We then fine-tune models on survey-style supervision that selectively changes the target value ratings while keeping non-target values close to their baseline responses. We evaluate the resulting shifts in two settings. First, we measure whether the intervention changes responses on held-out survey questions. Second, we test whether these survey-induced shifts transfer to out-of-domain behavioral proxies, including a contextual moral-judgment benchmark constructed from Reddit AITA posts and a text-based interactive decision-making benchmark. Our results show that survey-based supervision can change held-out survey responses and produce downstream shifts in model response distributions on proxy behavioral evaluations. However, this transfer is strongly model- and value-dependent, and is shaped by how value descriptions are framed in the supervision data. These findings suggest that value surveys can serve not only as diagnostic tools, but also as lightweight supervision for studying behavioral value conditioning in LLMs.

♻ ☆ Hyperdimensional Probe: Decoding LLM Representations via Vector Symbolic Architectures
Marco Bronzini, Carlo Nicolini, Bruno Lepri, Jacopo Staiano, Andrea Passerini
Despite their capabilities, Large Language Models (LLMs) remain opaque with limited understanding of their internal representations. Current interpretability methods either focus on input-oriented feature extraction, such as supervised probes and Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs), or on output distribution inspection, such as logit-oriented approaches. A full understanding of LLM vector spaces, however, requires integrating both perspectives, something existing approaches struggle with due to constraints on latent feature definitions. We introduce the Hyperdimensional Probe, a hybrid supervised probe that combines symbolic representations with neural probing. Leveraging Vector Symbolic Architectures (VSAs) and hypervector algebra, it unifies prior methods: the top-down interpretability of supervised probes, SAE's sparsity-driven proxy space, and output-oriented logit investigation. By combining the supervised learning paradigm of traditional probes with the dictionary-based representation principle of SAEs, our approach enables deeper input-focused feature extraction while supporting output-oriented analysis. Our experiments demonstrate that our approach consistently extracts meaningful semantic information across different LLMs, embedding sizes, and configurations, uncovering concept-oriented insights into LLM inference across two distinct scenarios: input-completion tasks and QA-focused text generation. VSA-based probing overcomes the limitations of logit-based analyses, which are constrained by the model's token vocabulary, while also mitigating the noisier interpretability outcomes often produced by SAEs in settings with a bounded conceptual feature space. By supporting a joint investigation of input-output features, this work advances the semantic understanding of neural representations while unifying the complementary perspectives of prior methods.

♻ ☆ GLUScope: A Tool for Analyzing GLU Neurons in Transformer Language Models
Sebastian Gerstner, Leonor Veloso, Hinrich Schütze
We present **GLUScope**, an open-source resource for analyzing neurons in Transformer-based language models (LMs), intended for interpretability researchers. We focus on the now-standard *GLU-based activation functions* such as SwiGLU, used in virtually all recent open-weights LMs. This introduces a new challenge: understanding positive activations is not enough. Instead, both the *gate* and the *in* activation of a neuron can be positive or negative, leading to four different possible sign combinations (*activation quadrants*) that in some cases have quite different functionalities (e.g. promoting vs. suppressing a specific set of tokens). Accordingly, for any neuron, our tool shows text examples for each of the four sign combinations, and indicates how often each combination occurs. Our resource can lead to novel insights: in particular, thanks to analyzing all four activation quadrants, it can surface interpretable behavior that other tools would miss. We also release the code used to create the resource, thus enabling researchers to build on it by adding more models, text corpora, or neuron pages.

♻ ☆ Word-Class and Construction-Like Structure Emerges in Neural Successor Representations Trained on Natural Language
Mathis Immertreu, Achim Schilling, Thomas Kinfe, Patrick Krauss
Neural language models are typically trained on next-token prediction, although linguistic structure spans multiple temporal scales. Successor representations (SRs) make this horizon explicit by encoding discounted distributions over future states. Here, we ask whether such predictive representations can recover not only word classes, but also finer functional and construction-like structure from natural language. A residual network trained on WikiText-103 predicts SR distributions at three horizons without part-of-speech supervision. At the shortest horizon, unsupervised clustering robustly recovers nouns, verbs, and adjectives, while directed inter-cluster transitions reproduce familiar syntactic asymmetries. At finer resolutions and across 13 part-of-speech categories, the same geometry reveals semantic-functional groupings that cross category boundaries and directed relations tracing candidate date, measurement, and title-name constructions. Part-of-speech agreement declines as the predictive horizon lengthens. These results suggest that word classes are coarse regions within a richer predictive geometry in which categorical and construction-like linguistic structure emerge from future-word distributions.
♻ ☆ Personalization Without Stereotyping: Do Speech LLMs Know When to Turn a Deaf Ear?
Kshitij Ambilduke, Caroline Brun, Thibaut Thonet, Vassilina Nikoulina, Marcely Zanon Boito
Recent speech LLMs can leverage rich paralinguistic cues, enabling more complex and personalized interactions. However, little work investigates how this sensitivity can lead to stereotyping, and existing evaluations largely measure demographic disparities without distinguishing beneficial personalization from stereotypical behavior. We introduce BASIS-Bench, a benchmark designed to disentangle these two aspects through three axes: (1) demographic robustness, measuring disparities in content-based tasks across speakers and demographic classification capabilities; (2) stereotypical bias, measuring whether perceived demographic cues trigger unprompted stereotypical behavior; and (3) personalization, measuring whether models can tailor responses to demographic characteristics when explicitly requested. We evaluate eight recent speech LLMs and find that, despite often recognizing demographic cues, most fail to translate them into appropriate personalization. Explicit demographic labels improve personalization but also amplify stereotypical associations already present in the textual backbones. These findings highlight the need to train speech LLMs not only to perceive demographic cues, but to learn when they should shape their responses.
♻ ☆ PRISM: Prompt-Refined In-Context System Modelling for Financial Retrieval EMNLP 2026
Chun Chet Ng, Jia Yu Lim, Zhen Hao Chu, Yixi Zhou, Wei Zeng Low
Deploying LLMs for financial information retrieval demands accuracy, cost efficiency, and reproducibility, yet most approaches require expensive fine-tuning. PRISM is a training-free framework that composes three deployable modules (prompt engineering, in-context learning, and a candidate refinement module) with a boundary-condition study of multi-agent coordination for financial document retrieval. On FiQA-2018, stacked reranking and $k$-reciprocal encoding raise Recall@100 from 0.7783 to 0.8326. On FinAgentBench, a Cohere-refined top-50 pool yields a higher observed end-to-end NDCG@5, below this evaluation's minimum detectable effect, while cutting per-query cost by about 35%. Added complexity does not always pay off: $k$-reciprocal encoding stops helping once the upstream stage is already high-recall, and multi-agent coordination is not a default for fine-grained chunk ranking. Matched pairs that hold prompt, model, and exemplars fixed lose 0.0702 and 0.0681 combined NDCG@5 to their single-path controls, and the whole gap sits at the chunk stage. We release an expert-annotated FinAgentBench evaluation set, together with a label-robustness sweep over 16 alternative ground-truth constructions, restoring a reproducible evaluation path after the original evaluator went offline. Without fine-tuning, PRISM placed third on the official FinAgentBench private leaderboard (leaderboard basis, NDCG@5 0.71181), reaches NDCG@10 0.6197 on FiQA-2018, and 98% accuracy on FinanceBench. We report per-query latency, token, and dollar accounting across roughly 40 configurations, present the quality-cost-latency Pareto frontier, and introduce FACET, a transparent SLA-specific scalarization for choosing among non-dominated configurations. Code and dataset are released at https://bit.ly/prism-ailens.
comment: Accepted to the EMNLP 2026 Industry Track
♻ ☆ MARS: Multi-hop Adaptive Retrieval and SPARQL Generation for KGQA
Nikit Srivastava, Daniel Vollmers, René Speck, Nikolaos Karalis, Hamada M. Zahera, Axel-Cyrille Ngonga Ngomo
Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong reasoning performance, but their tendency to hallucinate limits their reliability in knowledge-intensive tasks requiring up-to-date and grounded information. Combining knowledge graphs (KGs) with LLMs facilitates the use of explicit symbolic knowledge that can be continuously updated without costly fine-tuning, while benefiting from rapidly advancing LLM reasoning. We propose MARS, a scalable knowledge graph question answering (KGQA) approach that requires no model fine-tuning. Rather than relying on open-ended agentic exploration, MARS performs a structured retrieval procedure that links question entities to the KG and iteratively retrieves relevant next-hop information. At each step, MARS decides whether to continue graph traversal or to generate the final SPARQL query, allowing the model to adapt the retrieval depth to the question while keeping the overall pipeline more predictable than fully agentic approaches. We evaluate MARS on three established KGQA benchmarks across several LLMs and settings, including multilingual evaluation, and provide insights through ablation studies and error analysis. Our approach achieves competitive performance relative to state-of-the-art methods while remaining efficient and scalable. The evaluation results, code and resources are publicly available: https://github.com/dice-group/mars-kgqa.

♻ ☆ Scaling Multilingual Mixture-of-Experts: How Language Coverage Changes Capacity and Compute Allocation
Joel Dag, Nikit Srivastava, Hamada M. Zahera, Axel-Cyrille Ngonga Ngomo
Multilingual models share capacity across languages, so adding languages can hurt performance on existing ones. Mixture-of-experts models expand this capacity with little additional per-token computation. Given this extra capacity, does adding experts reduce what each additional language costs? To answer this question, we propose a repetition-aware joint scaling law relating number of active parameters, training tokens per language, number of experts, and language coverage. Fitted on 26,300 per-language loss observations from 64 pretrained configurations, our law reduces prediction error on held-out configurations by 28% relative to the strongest evaluated baseline. At matched number of active parameters and training tokens per language, 32 experts reduce the loss penalty of expanding from 12 to 200 languages by 83% compared with a dense model. Our law further shows that broader language coverage favors more experts across a wider range of compute budgets, guiding capacity allocation as multilingual models scale.
♻ ☆ A Theoretical Analysis of Why Masked Diffusion Models Mitigate the Reversal Curse NeurIPS 2026
Moongyu Jeon, Sangwoo Shin, BumJun Kim, Kyelim Lee, Albert No
Autoregressive language models (ARMs) suffer from the reversal curse: after learning ''$A$ is $B$,'' they often fail on the reverse query ''$B$ is $A$.'' Masked diffusion language models (MDMs) exhibit this failure in a much weaker form, but the underlying reason has remained unclear. A common explanation attributes this mitigation to their any-order masked training objective. However, observing ''$[\mathbf{M}]$ is $B$'' during training teaches recovery of $A$ from $B$ in one positional configuration, and does not by itself explain why the learned evidence should transfer to the reverse prompt ''$B$ is $[\mathbf{M}]$.'' We provide a theoretical analysis showing that this transfer arises from a parameter-level coupling between forward and reverse positional conditionals: shared Transformer parameters store token-pair evidence, while relative positional encodings route attention through queries and keys without changing the value-side evidence being retrieved. In a one-layer MDM, we prove that forward masked training strengthens evidence that is reusable in reverse queries, induces correlated forward--reverse attention routes, and yields a positively aligned shared-storage gradient component that decreases the reverse loss to first order. Controlled one-layer experiments and large-scale LLaDA/Dream experiments verify these signatures and show that they translate into improved reverse prediction.

☆ NarraSpace: Multimodal Scene-Grounded Evidence for Picture-Description Dementia Classification
Qingming Li, Ruiyan Sun, Satoshi Nakamura
Picture-description dementia classification uses a shared visual scene, but a transcript-level prediction alone does not identify the scene evidence underlying its inputs. NarraSpace links words and speech times to named picture elements and retrieves utterances for predefined scene events. Narrative-spatial descriptors summarize coverage and temporal organization. A frozen language model contrasts each event's text under examples from Alzheimer's disease and healthy-control training participants, alongside transcript surprisal and coverage measurements. Retained source records link event scores to their source utterances, speech intervals, and lexical matches. On the official 48-participant test set of the Alzheimer's Dementia Recognition through Spontaneous Speech benchmark, a fixed support vector classifier achieves $80.88\%\pm2.56\%$ mean accuracy over fifty seeds; averaged probabilities yield $81.25\%$ accuracy and $91.32\%$ area under the receiver operating characteristic curve. NarraSpace makes the construction of scene-linked classification inputs explicit and inspectable by connecting event-indexed language-model measurements to their source utterances, speech timing, and extraction records. Such source-linked measurements could support clinician review of picture descriptions by presenting quantitative summaries alongside the original speech evidence.

☆ Benchmarking Arabic Dialects for Math Problems
Noor Abo Mokh, Teresa Lynn, Nizar Habash, Ahmed Elshabrawy, Sara Alhajeri, Alaa Elsetohy, Preslav Nakov, Alham Fikri Aji
Mathematical reasoning remains underexplored for low-resource languages, including Dialectal Arabic. To bridge this gap, we introduce the DAR-MGSM (Dialectal Arabic Multilingual Grade School Math) Benchmark, the first manually curated benchmark for evaluating mathematical reasoning in Dialectal Arabic. DAR-MGSM consists of math word problems (MWPs) requiring multi-step reasoning and covers five major Arabic dialects alongside Modern Standard Arabic (MSA). The benchmark comprises two parallel versions: translated-only and translated & culturally localized. The latter version adapts entities and references to regional varieties. We evaluate eleven Arabic-centric and multilingual large language models on DAR-MGSM. The results show that the performance generally declines on the localized version, highlighting the challenges introduced by culturally grounded adaptations. However, the magnitude of this effect varies across models, with Arabic-centric models exhibiting different levels of sensitivity to localization. A mechanistic interpretability analysis shows that translated and localized variants of the same MWPs often induce distinct internal reasoning trajectories, even when aggregate accuracy remains similar. Overall, our results show that mathematical reasoning performance depends on both linguistic variety and cultural context.
☆ Capable Teacher, Myopic Scorer: Reward-Free Teacher Repair for Agentic On-Policy Distillation
Shu Zhao, Tan Yu
Agentic on-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student agent on its own trajectories while a teacher scores every token. A natural explanation of where it goes wrong is compounding error: the student's mistakes carry its trajectories beyond the region where the teacher is reliable, so recent methods keep training inside that region, through episode-length curricula, teacher-guided turns, prefix replay, or teacher intervention where the two disagree. We show this explanation is incomplete. From the states where the student departs most from the teacher, the teacher's own takeovers still finish the task, yet the student trained on its token scores keeps running out of turns. We attribute this to the myopia of next-token scoring. The teacher rates each token without seeing where the episode ends, so its scores let through the safe, delaying moves that outcome-based RL would punish with zero reward, a penalty agentic OPD lacks. We call this difference between what the teacher does and what it scores the rollout-scorer gap. We propose Rollout-to-Scorer Distillation (R2S), which repairs the scorer before distillation by mining high-disagreement states from the untrained student, running teacher takeovers from them, keeping the states where enough takeovers pass a reward-free check such as consensus or an LLM judge, and fine-tuning the teacher to give its takeover-informed scores on every assistant token from the divergence turn onward, without the takeover in context. The repaired teacher is frozen as the sole scorer for standard OPD, requiring no task reward during repair or training. Across three environments, two teachers and four students, R2S outperforms three state-of-the-art agentic-OPD methods in all twelve teacher-student-environment cells, by 12.8 success points on average over the best of them in each cell. Project page: https://tree-shu-zhao.github.io/r2s/.
comment: 17 pages, 5 figures, 13 tables. Project page: https://tree-shu-zhao.github.io/r2s/
☆ Cache Engineering for Agents
Tianyu Fu, Ruoxi Wang, Zihan Min, Zhongqi Ren, Zhihao Li, Tengxuan Liu, Shengxu Mu, Hanling Zhang, Yu Wang
Large Language Model (LLM) agents can handle increasingly complex tasks, navigating long trajectories of observations and actions. As these trajectories grow, key-value (KV) cache I/O and storage become the dominant cost. Trajectories can also exceed the LLM's maximum context length, requiring control over active cache growth. Prior work uses context engineering to reduce cache size by trimming or summarizing history. However, modifying the trajectory prefix invalidates subsequent cached states and triggers costly re-prefilling of retained context. Task accuracy can also suffer when too little context is retained to limit re-prefill costs. We propose cache engineering as a new scheme. It allows agents to directly control their active KV cache without modifying the trajectory or re-prefilling retained context. Specifically, agents can proactively use drop to exclude selected cache from future attention and repos to adjust retained keys' position encodings. We formalize these cache operations and identify history dependence as a serving challenge: identical token sequences can yield different cache states under different cache operation sequences, making prefix matching insufficient for correct cache reuse. Thus, we co-design the AKV serving engine. It provides a stateless interface compatible with existing serving APIs, while internally managing stateful cache reuse, eviction, and restoration. Experiments show that a tool-response rolling-drop cache policy improves average benchmark scores by 1.7 points and reduces average cost by 27.7%, compared with conventional threshold-triggered summarization. With AKV, it achieves up to 1.2x the output throughput and a 1.5x speedup in P95 request latency. Recursive self-improvement of cache policies further reduces cost by 7.1% over expert design. Our code is at https://github.com/thu-nics/AKV.
☆ Large Flow Language Models
Arnav Chavan, Chanhyuk Lee, Udbhav Bamba, Jaehoon Yoo, Seunghoon Hong, Aditi Raghunathan, Jinwoo Kim
Continuous flow language models offer a path to parallel generation with tools for control and acceleration unavailable to discrete diffusion, yet effective techniques for building them at the billion-parameter scale remain less established. We present Lafa (Large flow language model), a fast, high-performing flow language model scaled to 8B parameters in a data-efficient manner. Lafa is a blockwise flow model with causal attention on the prefix and bidirectional, asynchronous flow within each block. This design enables efficient training by adapting pretrained autoregressive (AR) models and fast inference via prefix caching and blockwise parallel decoding. We train Lafa at 0.5B, 7B, and 8B scales with fewer than 30B tokens, more efficient than previous diffusion models, and evaluate it on instruction following, reasoning, and coding, where the learned flow matches or surpasses the quality of AR and can be distilled into flow maps for one- and few-step generation. Notably, flow training preserves or even improves pretrained AR capabilities via a multi-token prediction effect, which we leverage in a self-speculative decoding scheme that verifies flow-based drafts autoregressively within a single model. This yields 2.54x the throughput of an AR model at comparable quality, outperforming multi-token prediction and discrete diffusion methods. Our findings show that continuous flows can serve as a scalable foundation for language modeling.

☆ Binary Discrete Diffusion: Candidate-Set Diffusion for Logic and Planning Tasks
Rodrigo Casado Noguerales, Dimitri von Rütte, Bernhard Schölkopf, Thomas Hofmann, Aran Raoufi
Discrete diffusion models iteratively denoise a noisy sequence of categorical data such as text, enabling any-order generation with self-correction and in-filling capabilities. This makes them promising candidates for reasoning, planning, and constrained-generation tasks like coding and math. Yet masked and uniform diffusion erase a token's identity in a single corruption event, so their noisy states cannot explicitly represent partially resolved uncertainty in which several alternatives remain plausible. We close this gap with Binary Discrete Diffusion, a family of continuous-time discrete-diffusion processes whose noisy state is a set of candidate tokens per position, represented as a binary membership vector. Binary diffusion changes candidate sets through additions and removals, allowing information to be lost and recovered gradually while the diffusion state itself represents partial uncertainty. On Sudoku-Extreme, Boxoban, and graph coloring, the symmetric formulation of binary diffusion consistently outperforms strong, well-tuned masked and uniform baselines under matched architectures and controlled training, sampling, and tuning budgets, with its largest gains on the hardest instances, while remaining competitive on language modeling (Text8, TinyStories, OpenWebText). These results support candidate sets as an effective intermediate representation for iterative reasoning and show that the corruption kernel can materially change the computation learned by the reverse model.

☆ BitMTP: When Multi-Token Prediction Meets Low-Bit Large Language Models NeurIPS 2026
Ning Zhang, Shihao Wang, Jinrui Zhang, Chaodong Xiao, Lei Zhang
For efficient deployment of large language models (LLMs), methods of ultra-low-bit backbones reduce weight storage, memory traffic, and linear-layer execution cost, while multi-token prediction (MTP) methods reduce sequential decoding steps by predicting multiple future tokens in parallel. Although complementary, the direct combination of these methods cannot accelerate decoding at comparable quality: masked drafting lacks intermediate token input supplied in next-token training, while wider computation and causal key-value (KV) updates can offset the savings from fewer steps. To address these challenges, we propose BitMTP, an algorithm-system co-design framework for efficient MTP on ultra-low-bit LLM backbones. A joint next-token prediction and MTP objective equips a single model with both autoregressive (AR) generation and block-causal drafting. Confidence-only decoding, speculative-only decoding, and hybrid decoding provide different quality-throughput operating points without using separate draft and verifier models. Accepted-prefix alignment keeps the committed prefix, materialized causal KV boundary, and CUDA Graph replay state consistent for continued generation. BitMTP uses this contract to select width-specialized low-bit execution and reuse captured graphs over shared KV storage. On NVIDIA H20, BitMTP with hybrid decoding achieves five-task mean speedups of 2.2x on BitNet b1.58-2B and 2.1-2.4x on 1.7B-4B Bonsai models at comparable task quality. On RTX 3090 and Jetson Orin, it further achieves up to 2.0x in five-task mean throughput and 3.3x on MBPP+, respectively. These results demonstrate that BitMTP accelerates ultra-low-bit LLM generation across backbones, model sizes, and GPU types. The source code is released at https://github.com/PolyU-VCLab/BitMTP

☆ Present but Hard to Read: Probing Latent Reasoning with Minimal Pairs
Aur Shalev Merin
Probing studies of latent reasoning often read a model's state where the answer begins and take a failed probe as evidence that a value is absent. We test this with a digit copied from the question, in the looped model Ouro-1.4B and the standard transformer Qwen2.5-1.5B. Comparing prompts that differ only in that digit shows it is present at the last prompt token, in a code shared across questions (readout about 0.9). A probe that sees one prompt at a time, the usual setting, barely reads it: 0.36 in Ouro and 0.19 in Qwen even after 5,249 training questions. The digit's change is small rather than crowded: random noise lands on equally busy directions, while a random word in its place moves the state far more and is readable. Since every varied token leaves a trace, a successful probe is ambiguous too. We give a protocol, including a synthetic null for generic mixing, for testing computed values against copies and mixtures of the inputs. In pre-registered studies of two-step problems it finds a small signal for the unwritten intermediate a+b just before Ouro writes the answer's last digit, and its sign follows the operation, though mixing alone produces much of that sign-following part. Holding that value at another problem's from the first loop on does not move the written digit toward the other problem's, while the same patch of the answer does: the value is readable but not used.

☆ OptionWorld: A Benchmark for Diagnosing Failures of LLMs as Long-Term Personal Assistants
Ruoyu Zhang, Zhaocen Liu, Weiyi Tian, Melissa Feng, Shilong Liu
Long-term assistants must follow unfinished tasks across days as information and circumstances change. Finding information or starting an action, however, does not ensure that the task gets completed. We introduce OptionWorld, a benchmark that traces information acquisition, judgments, actions, and outcomes in simulated daily-life tasks to identify where current LLMs fall short as long-term personal assistants. In runs with archived decision inputs, the key facts remain in the model's last input before the deadline in 35.5% of failed instances. Failure analysis identifies the three most frequent problems: needed information is not obtained or combined, available opportunities to act go unused, and agents impose restrictions that the task does not require. Three follow-up experiments show that supplying missing facts restores task success in some cases; action reminders can restart work while subsequent steps still miss the deadline; and correcting a supplied note that wrongly forbids contact restores permitted inquiry. These findings locate shortcomings in maintaining task state: connecting facts to unfinished work, following through before deadlines, and separating actual restrictions from the agent's assumptions.

☆ Asymmetric Looped Transformers: Fixed Global Representations for an Iterative Decoder
Cheng Liang, Tengfei Zhang, Wenjie DU, Jianyu Wu, Changlian Ma, Shuchen Pu, Ya Zhang, Yanfeng Wang, Zhouhan Lin
Looped Transformers increase effective depth by repeatedly applying shared layers, offering a way to scale computation without increasing parameter count. However, full-stack looping places context construction and iterative state refinement within the same recurrent computation, without explicitly distinguishing their roles. We introduce the Asymmetric Looped Transformer (AsymLoop), which separates single-pass context encoding from recurrent state refinement. A single-pass causal encoder produces layer-specific Global key-value (KV) caches that remain fixed across iterations, while a weight-tied recurrent decoder generates Local KV from its evolving state. Joint attention over both sources under a single softmax allows each update to integrate persistent contextual information with the current intermediate state. For training, we apply a heavy-tail-guided layerwise learning-rate method to the encoder and decoder. Experiments on FineWeb-Edu across four model scales, from 60M to 1B, show consistently lower validation perplexity and higher average downstream accuracy than Full Loop under matched effective depth and training token budgets. At 1B, AsymLoop improves average zero-shot accuracy across seven commonsense reasoning benchmarks from 49.58% to 50.85%. Further experiments extend these benefits to longer training budgets, an OLMo-style backbone, and sparse mixture-of-experts models. These results support asymmetric context encoding and state refinement as an effective design for recurrent language models.

Ziyong Wang, Yupei Lin, Runxuan Huang, Liang Lin, Dongyu Zhang, Guangrun Wang

We investigate this question using contact-rich manipulation as a representative testbed, examining tactile understanding as a concrete instance of this broader capability. Contact-rich manipulation requires interpreting tactile feedback and adjusting actions after obstruction. Existing learned tactile policies acquire these capabilities through specialized pretraining and per-task optimization, with substantial data and training costs. We present GPT-as-Tactile-Policy, which summarizes expert demonstrations into task guidance and tactile references for contact assessment and corrective action. ☆ Toolflation: Seller Agents Distort Tool Markets Through Spontaneous Generative Engine Optimization
Zhenwei Tang, Di Bai, Jintao Liu, Xidong Wu, Qianfeng Wen, Xinwu Cheng
Agents are starting to sell to other agents. Seller agents write the listings of tools, services and products, and buyer agents read these listings when they choose what to use. We show that seller agents told only to be selected more often practice generative engine optimization on their own, and that this distorts the market for sellers and for buyers. We build tool markets from real data tools on Amazon and Instagram and run every tool on every question, so we know which tools can answer each question. Under all five production models we test, sellers rewrite their descriptions to claim more and more fields, including fields that their tools return for fewer than half of the targets. We call this process toolflation. Nothing in the market limits it, and how far it goes depends on the model, as the number of claims that contradict the sellers' own evidence differs by an order of magnitude between models. Toolflation shifts the buyers' choices between sellers, and the most capable tool loses share in seven of the ten combinations of seller model and platform. Under four models, the choices stay with capable tools and buyer agents keep their task success. Under the fifth, sellers copy one another until the descriptions no longer tell the tools apart. Buyer agents then pick tools by their position in the list, tools that answer almost nothing win a quarter to two fifths of the choices, and task success falls from 89% to about 30% on Amazon, close to random choice, and from 77% to 40% on Instagram. The sellers did only what their vendors asked, and the cost fell on buyers they never dealt with. We therefore argue that honesty toward third parties under competition is part of safety alignment, and that models should be tested as sellers before they are deployed as sellers.

☆ Every Model Leaves a Trace: Multi-Resolution LLM Fingerprinting
Mohammad Reza Modarres, Armin Tourajmehr, Artin Tavasoli, Yadollah Yaghoobzadeh, Mohammad Taher Pilehvar
Large language models leave model-specific traces in their generated text, but it remains unclear how finely these fingerprints can distinguish related models and which signals remain informative as obvious cues are reduced. We study this through fingerprint resolution, considering both model resolution, from different families to distilled and adapted variants, and signal resolution across behavioral, stylometric, lexical, and semantic evidence. We progressively suppress surface cues, paraphrase outputs, vary the amount of available text, and test generalization across prompt distributions and languages. Our results show that fingerprints persist beyond broad family differences, remaining detectable in distilled models and even LoRA adaptations of the same base model.
