♻ ☆ Abstract Compression of Conversational Audio Context for LLM-based ASR
Shashi Kumar, Esaú Villatoro-Tello, Sergio Burdisso, Kadri Hacioglu, Thibault Bañeras-Roux, Hasindri Watawana, Dairazalia Sanchez-Cortes, Srikanth Madikeri, Petr Motlicek, Andreas Stolcke
Standard LLM-based speech recognition systems process utterances in isolation, limiting their ability to leverage conversational context. Here we study whether multimodal context from prior turns improves LLM-based ASR and how to represent that context efficiently. We find that, after supervised multi-turn training, conversational context mainly helps with the recognition of contextual entities. However, conditioning on raw context is expensive because the prior-turn audio embedding sequence grows rapidly with conversation length. To address this, we propose Abstract Compression, a learned latent abstraction of prior-turn audio that replaces each prior-turn audio sequence with a fixed number of latent tokens while retaining corresponding transcripts explicitly. On both in-domain and out-of-domain test sets, the compression-enabled model recovers part of the gains of raw-context conditioning with a smaller prior-turn audio footprint. We also provide targeted analyses of the compression setup and its tradeoffs.

♻ ☆ The Trilemma of Truth in Large Language Models
Germans Savcisens, Tina Eliassi-Rad
The public often attributes human-like qualities to large language models (LLMs), assuming that they "know" certain things. In reality, LLMs encode information retained during training as internal probabilistic knowledge. This study examines existing methods for probing the veracity of that knowledge and identifies three flawed underlying assumptions. To address these flaws, we introduce sAwMIL (Sparse-Aware Multiple-Instance Learning), a multiclass probing framework that combines multiple-instance learning with conformal prediction. sAwMIL leverages LLMs' internal representations to classify statements as true, false, or neither. We evaluate sAwMIL across 16 open-source LLMs, including default and chat-based variants, using three new curated datasets. Our results show that (1) common probing methods fail to provide a reliable and transferable veracity direction and, in some settings, perform worse than zero-shot prompting; (2) truth and falsehood are not encoded symmetrically; and (3) LLMs encode a third type of signal that is distinct from both true and false.

♻ ☆ Compute-Optimal Scaling Laws for Spiking Language Models NeurIPS 2026
Dan Saunders, Benjamin Ren, Catherine He
Spiking neural networks target energy-efficient language modeling through sparse, binary activations, but the efficiency-accuracy tradeoff of spiking language models is uncharacterized at scale. We present the first Chinchilla-style IsoFLOP scaling study of a spiking language model, sweeping SpikeGPT-family models (RWKV backbone) from width 384 to 1536 across compute budgets from $3\times10^{17}$ to $10^{19}$ FLOPs on FineWeb-EDU. We report two findings. First, the compute-optimal frontier is $N_{opt} \propto C^{0.56}$, close to the 0.50 that Hoffmann et al. report for dense Transformers, so spiking does not change how parameters and data should be traded off under a compute budget. Second, against non-spiking RWKV and Transformer baselines trained the same way, the loss gap splits into a backbone cost of about 0.16 nats that does not change with compute and a spiking cost that falls steadily with compute, from 0.34 to 0.23 nats over our range. These results give the first compute-optimal recipe for spiking language models and quantify the accuracy cost of their sparsity.

♻ ☆ SoK: Grading Open-Text Legal Answers, from Law School Exams to Trustworthy Rubric Judges
Sebastian Nagl
Law students are examined through open-text answers: case solutions, essays and memos that apply the law to a set of facts. Such answers are expensive to grade, human graders disagree widely on them, and large language models (LLMs) now offer to grade them at scale, both in teaching and in the benchmarks that test legal AI. This paper systematizes how open-text legal answers can be evaluated. We derive eight desiderata from educational measurement and from the law that governs exams, among them reasons, credit for defensible alternative solutions, robustness to gaming and human oversight. We then compare eight evaluation paradigms against them, from unstructured human grading to LLM judges with instance-specific rubrics and pairwise comparison. Three findings stand out. A rubric written for the task is the strongest lever for agreement between an LLM judge and human graders, but studies measure that agreement against different references and rarely report how far the human graders agree with each other. The central validation gap lies where examination law is most demanding: defensible alternative solutions, follow-on errors, and the examiner's correction of model judgments. Rubric-centred grading is the most auditable family, because it makes the standard, its application and the final decision checkable. From this we derive the trustworthy rubric judge: an instance rubric that credits arguments and alternative paths, evidence checked in code, probes, repeated runs, calibration against independent human grades, logging, and an examiner who reads every script. Today the evidence supports such a judge for formative feedback and for benchmarks, and as an assistant to the examiner, not a replacement, where credit is at stake.

♻ ☆ Robust LLM Watermarking with Minimal Semantic Distortion for IP Protection
Kieu Dang, Phung Lai, NhatHai Phan, Yelong Shen, Ruoming Jin
Proprietary large language models (LLMs) face risks of intellectual property (IP) violation, as adversaries can replicate an LLM by collecting input-output pairs to train a surrogate model, causing financial setbacks. Watermarks offer a promising defense to verify ownership, but existing methods often struggle with semantic distortion, factual inconsistency, and adversarial attacks. In addition, key-conditioned watermarks for provider-specific detection, especially in cross-provider and multi-user scenarios, remain largely underexplored. To address these challenges, we propose SAFESEAL, a novel key-conditioned watermarking framework that achieves strong detectability with minimal impact on model utility, effectively balancing detectability, utility, and robustness. SAFESEAL preserves named entities while substituting linguistic terms with context-aware synonyms through a key-conditioned Tournament sampling mechanism, maintaining semantic fidelity and factual consistency. For detection, we introduce a key-conditioned contrastive detector that jointly encodes the text and key, enabling provider-specific and robust watermark verification. We derive theoretical bounds on the utility-detectability trade-off and significantly reduce latency through lightweight models, batching, and parallelism. Extensive experiments show that SAFESEAL outperforms baselines in utility, detectability, and robustness, achieving a BERTScore of 0.983, entity similarity of 0.963, a 98.2% detection rate, and the highest human ratings for text quality and content preservation, with latency comparable to the fastest baseline. To promote transparency and community-driven progress, we release the first public watermark leaderboard and an interactive demo.
♻ ☆ MemT-1.0: Towards Memory-Native Evolution
Zhiyu Li, Zeyu Zhang, Linyun Liu, Yiwei Bu, Chenyang Xi, Xichong Zhang, Yi Yu, Yu Yu, Zhichen Liu, Yuxiao Yi, Chunyu Li, Haoran Liu, Zhenkai Ding, Le Zhou, Juncheng Zhang, Boyu Chen, Zehao Lin, Guorui Li, Renyu Fu, Ji Li, Youyang Xia, Jiajun Shen, Harvey Li, Jingsong Zhou, Helan Hu, Tong Shen, Tianxiao Xu, Chenye Han, Weijie Li, Zhongquan Zhou, Hongming Xu, Yuekeng Li, Erenkai Zhu, Peng Yu, Wenhao Li, Jingyi Kang, Xixuan Hao, Xuzhou Zhu, Can Chen, Jiawei Liu, Chen Jiang, Tao Jiang, Fangwei Xiang, Zepeng Yao, Kai Chen, Guang Yang, Mingfei Lu, Chunhui Li, Zhengxuan Jiang, Bo Tang, Shiyu Zhan, Yihang Sun, Jie Chu, Wenjun Xiong, Zhenxiang Hua, Chen Lin, Detong Su, Dongyu Deng, Qiuping Ding, Guofeng Ding, Si Chen, Guohua Wen, Xu Chen, Zhi-Qin John Xu, Ying-Cong Chen, Ningyu Zhang, Haofen Wang, Muning Wen, Haotian Chen, Wentao Zhang, Xuanhe Zhou, Rita Zhang, Junchi Yan, Feiyu Xiong
Memory-native evolution requires making memory an intrinsic capability of foundation models, enabling them to continuously accumulate and utilize information. To this end, we introduce MemT-1.0, a new architecture of memory foundation models that internalize memory as part of model computation. Building on Metis, MemT-1.0 employs Gated DeltaNet-2 with a stable token-level memory update mechanism within each turn. We further introduce Mixture-of-Storage to scale native memory capacity. Following a deliberate backbone preparation, we develop a two-stage training pipeline consisting of continued pre-training and post-training, supported by 34B tokens of training data and efficient training and inference infrastructure. We release a model family comprising MemT-1.0-Nano and MemT-1.0-Flash, and conduct a comprehensive evaluation of memory and general capabilities. The results show that they outperform the parametric memory baselines on average and remain competitive with retrieval-augmented methods, while largely preserving general capabilities. At a history length of 128K tokens, MemT-1.0-Nano achieves approximately $1.86\times$ end-to-end inference speedup and a $66\times$ reduction in persistent storage relative to full context KV cache. By internalizing retrieved procedural experience, MemT-1.0 improves task success on agentic tasks, providing a practical step toward memory-native evolution.

♻ ☆ SINT-Flow: Schema Integration using Large Language Model Workflows
Keti Korini, Christian Bizer
The goal of schema integration is, given a set of input schemata or tables, to derive a global, unified schema that is able to represent the concepts, attributes, and relationships of all input tables in a coherent fashion. This paper presents SINT-Flow, a schema integration framework composed of five LLM-based operators that can be combined into workflows to perform fully automated, end-to-end schema integration. In contrast to existing approaches, SINT-Flow can process denormalized source tables that contain attributes describing multiple entity types. During the schema integration process, these tables are decomposed into separate entity-specific relations. To evaluate SINT-Flow, we introduce SINT-Bench, a schema integration benchmark comprising 10 schema integration tasks consisting of altogether 93 relational tables, including tables that describe multiple types of entities. We evaluate SINT-Flow using GPT-5.2 as well as the open-weight model Qwen-3.6-27B as alternative backbone models. Using these models, SINT-Flow achieves a Completeness rate of at least 97% in entity type detection, 81% for attribute detection, and an F1-score of 84% for schema mapping. Furthermore, we perform an ablation study to prove the utility of the applied self-consistency strategy as well as the inclusion of a review loop into the Schema Matching Operator.
♻ ☆ Pair Difficulty Matters: Rethinking Pairwise LLM-as-a-Judge Evaluation and Consistency EMNLP 2026
Bruno Brocai, Maria Becker
Large Language Model judges are widely used to rank texts and text-generating systems through pairwise comparison, and their reliability is typically assessed via three proxies: position bias, transitivity, and pairwise agreement (self- or human-labeled). Because these proxies drive judge selection and benchmarking, a substantial literature reporting that judges perform poorly on them risks steering practitioners away from otherwise capable evaluators. We argue this assessment is misleading. Under the Bradley--Terry geometry underlying pairwise aggregation, each proxy is dominated by close-rank-gap pairs, where inconsistency is information-theoretically expected and individual verdicts contribute little to the aggregate ranking; far-gap pairs carry the ranking signal but barely move the proxies. We formalize this argument and validate it in a controlled simulation and on two human-rated corpora: the proxies correlate only weakly with ranking accuracy against gold, and their predictive component concentrates in the far-gap regime. Judges should therefore be assessed on rank-gap-conditional metrics, ideally against human rankings. Code at https://github.com/brunobrocai/PairDifficulty.

♻ ☆ Serial Depth of Adaptive Parallel Sampling: Lower Bounds and Schedules for Masked Diffusion Models
Chuling Wen, Weijie Liang, Jian Lu, Zhouchen Lin
Masked diffusion models generate sequences in rounds, predicting several masked positions at once. Fewer rounds can accelerate decoding, but sampling positions independently can lose their dependence and bias the output. How few rounds suffice for a prescribed error budget? Beyond error bounds for individual decoding rules, we study sharp lower bounds on the number of rounds required for a given target distribution. We introduce serial depth: the minimum number of rounds, averaged over target sequences, needed to meet a forward Kullback-Leibler error budget. With exact conditional probabilities, we prove these bounds for all deterministic rules that may adapt to generated values and construct matching schedules for several distribution families, with depths ranging from $\log\log n$ to linear in sequence length $n$. To narrow the gap to trained models, we show that these lower bounds remain valid for learned predictors and give sufficient accuracy conditions on visited contexts for schedules to retain their error guarantees. On two pretrained models across five text domains, a schedule suggested by the theory achieves higher average log-likelihood on held-out text in five rounds than random selection in eight. A preregistered confirmation study certifies a relative improvement on independent dialogue data.

♻ ☆ From Outcomes to Components: A Counterfactual Evaluation Framework for LLM-Proposed Repairs
Hengyu Ye, Songhua Hu
A repair action proposed by a large language model (LLM) specifies which records to change and how to change their values. A failed action may still contain a useful choice of records or a useful update, depending on how the two are paired. We introduce a counterfactual evaluation framework that separates selected records (targets) from the applied change (an update). Four executions combine proposed and reference targets and updates to measure the effects of correcting or retaining each component and their interaction. We evaluate the framework on two tasks: traffic assignment, where affected demand must be identified from road counts, and database repair, where affected rows must be identified from aggregate reports. An injected fault supplies a reference action that reverses it, and the recovery rate measures an action's loss reduction relative to that reference. For sparse-target actions in the traffic experiments, retaining the proposed update on reference targets achieves 79.4% of the reference loss reduction, compared with 43.7% from tuning update strength on the proposed targets. In the database experiments, update replacement worsens 17 of 108 actions, all selecting unaffected records, with sharply different frequencies across models. The value of a proposed update depends on the targets it is paired with. Missed targets hide useful updates, while excess targets can make a milder update better than the reference correction. In a component-reuse study, testing new target-update pairings from two proposals raises mean held-out recovery rate to 0.616, compared with 0.580 when selecting between the original proposals.

♻ ☆ DECODEM: Data Extraction from Corporate Organizational Documents via Enhanced Methods
Jens Frankenreiter
Much empirical legal research depends on translating unstructured text into structured variables. In corporate governance research as elsewhere, this translation has traditionally been performed by humans. Manual coding is time-consuming and costly. In corporate governance research, standardized commercial datasets offer an alternative, but they cover a limited set of firms and governance variables. This paper introduces the DECODEM Benchmarks and uses them to evaluate whether large language models can extract corporate governance variables from charters and bylaws. The benchmarks combine a sample of corporate charters and bylaws of publicly traded U.S. corporations obtained from the SEC's EDGAR database with hand-coded labels that capture whether a document includes a specific governance arrangement. The paper uses these benchmarks to evaluate a range of extraction approaches and models. The approaches vary along several dimensions, including the amount of guidance included in the prompts and the extent to which the model is provided with the full document or with relevant excerpts only. The results show that current models can recover many governance variables with high accuracy across extraction approaches. At the same time, performance varies substantially across documents and variables. In particular, extraction is less accurate for charters than for bylaws, including when the comparison is limited to variables that appear in both benchmarks. The results also show that frontier models perform comparably on the tasks evaluated here and that more elaborate extraction pipelines do not consistently improve their performance. More elaborate extraction strategies matter more for efficiency-oriented models, where they can substantially narrow the performance gap relative to frontier models.
♻ ☆ Label Unification for Cross-Dataset Generalization in Cybersecurity NER
Maciej Jalocha, Johan Hausted Schmidt, William Michelsen
The field of cybersecurity NER lacks standard-ized labels, making it challenging to combine datasets. We investigate label unification across four cybersecurity datasets to increase data resource usability. We perform a coarse-grained label unification and conduct pairwise cross-dataset evaluations using RoBERTa models. Qualitative analysis of predictions reveals errors, limitations, and dataset differences. To address unification limitations, we propose alternative architectures including a multi-head model and a graph-based transfer model. Under our selected mapping and training setup, unified training underperformed the corresponding single-dataset baselines on all four development sets. The multi-head model with weight sharing provides no consistent benefits over single-dataset baselines, and the graph-based transfer model shows no consistent benefits.

♻ ☆ Performance-Driven Policy Optimization for Speculative Decoding with Adaptive Windowing NeurIPS 2026
Jie Jiang, Xing Sun, Ruotian Chen, Jianan Su, Kaixin Shen
Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by having a lightweight draft model propose speculative windows of candidate tokens for parallel verification by a larger target model. In practice, speculative efficiency is often bottlenecked by hard-to-draft positions, where an early mismatch truncates the accepted prefix and invalidates the rest of the speculative window. Most learning-based drafters are still optimized with token-level supervised objectives, even though speculative utility is inherently window-level and prefix-sensitive. We propose PPOW (Performance-Driven Policy Optimization with Adaptive Windowing), a reinforcement learning framework that shifts drafter optimization from token-level imitation to window-level optimization. PPOW combines a Cost-Aware Speedup Reward, a Distribution-Based Proximity Reward, and Adaptive Divergence-Aware Windowing, which prioritizes informative windows with high confidence-weighted draft-target divergence. PPOW achieves average acceptance lengths of 6.29-6.52 and speedups of 3.39-4.36$\times$ across multiple model families and benchmarks under a unified decoding protocol. These results show that performance-driven window-level optimization is a practical approach to improving speculative decoding efficiency.

♻ ☆ VibeTraining-Bench: Data-Trainer-Harness Co-Design for Iterative System Improvement
Ruotian Peng, Yuanyi Ren, Shuaiqi Liu, Xin Guo, Fang Zhu, Yangyi Huang, Yue Wu, Zheng Wang, Yandong Wen, Zhihang Fu
Recursive self-improvement (RSI) requires agents to diagnose system bottlenecks and coordinate improvements across training and inference. We refer to the agents' capability to jointly optimize training data, the trainer, and the inference harness as cross-component co-design. We introduce VibeTraining-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating this capability across tasks spanning terminal operation, software engineering, multi-turn tool use, and mathematical reasoning. Within each task, research agents start from an identical initial agent system under a fixed resource budget. Guided by validation feedback, they iteratively refine data, trainers, and inference harnesses, then select and submit a final agent system for evaluation on the test set. To prevent reward hacking, we enforce three safeguards: test data isolation, separate research and grading containers, and network restrictions for research agents. Our evaluation results show that frontier research agents substantially improve target agent systems. For example, on SWE-bench Verified, GPT-6-Astra raises the target agent system's performance from 32.9% to 53.7%, comparable to the human-developed instruct model's 55.4%. However, there is still room to improve their ability to co-design and iteratively refine data, trainer, and harness to achieve cumulative performance gains. We hope that VibeTraining-Bench will serve as a unified platform for advancing RSI, deepening our understanding of cross-component optimization for agent systems. Code and data are available at https://github.com/D2I-ai/VibeTraining-Bench.

♻ ☆ Computational Typology
Gerhard Jäger
Typology is a subfield of linguistics that focuses on the study and classification of languages based on their structural features. Unlike genealogical classification, which examines the historical relationships between languages, typology seeks to understand the diversity of human languages by identifying common properties and patterns, known as universals. In recent years, computational methods have played an increasingly important role in typological research, enabling the analysis of large-scale linguistic data and the testing of hypotheses about language structure and evolution. This article provides an illustration of the benefits of computational statistical modeling in typology.

♻ ☆ StartLux-Decision: AutoThink Decision Models with Reinforcement Learning for Calibrated Deliberative Decisions
Xialie Zhuang, Zunhai Su, Jia Pan, Ngai Wong, Guanhua Chen, Chuan-Wei Kuo
We introduce StartLux-Decision, decision models from 0.8B to 27B parameters that combine efficient single-pass prediction with adaptive, confidence-guided reasoning. The Instant models use bidirectional attention in full-attention layers to improve decision accuracy while keeping single-pass inference. The AutoThink models invoke explicit reasoning for uncertain decisions and answer confident cases directly. A three-stage training pipeline establishes shared decision capabilities, specializes the two variants, and equips AutoThink with reasoning through reasoning supervision and reinforcement learning. We introduce Reinforcement Learning for Calibrated Deliberative Decisions (RLCD$^2$), which uses a frozen model and proper scoring rules to reward improvements in decision probability quality and applies a soft penalty to overlong reasoning traces. RLCD$^2$ raises the share of reasoning traces that terminate within the generation limit by up to 48 percentage points and cuts average reasoning length by up to 65%, while largely preserving decision accuracy. On the public subset of Decision Index 0.3, the Instant models outperform the strongest evaluated comparable-scale open-weight decision models by 7.4-16.9 points, and the 27B AutoThink model reaches 73.4. We also introduce DecisionPivot, a programmatically verifiable benchmark of computational decisions built from contrast pairs that differ in a single answer-changing detail. On DecisionPivot, AutoThink outperforms its Instant counterparts by 11.5-18.4 points in pair accuracy and leads among evaluated systems. The models can be run using frameworks such as vLLM and MLX, with quantized versions available to reduce memory usage.Inference code and links to model weights are available at https://github.com/StartLuxLabs/Startlux-Decision .

♻ ☆ No Single Tokenizer Feature Reliably Predicts Downstream Language Model Performance EMNLP 2026
Antonio Lopardo, Luis Frentzen Salim, Shikhar Srivastava, Kevin Jiang, Mridul Sharma, Catherine Arnett
Tokenization is a critical component of the language modeling pipeline, but it is poorly understood. Tokenizers can impact downstream performance, but there is currently mixed evidence about which tokenizer features predict a better language model without requiring a full training run. In this paper, we analyze approximately 200 language models trained on nine different languages. We evaluate both models and their tokenizers and test whether there is a relationship between any proposed intrinsic tokenizer evaluations and model performance, as measured by a wide variety of benchmarks. Despite the quantity and variety of proposed intrinsic tokenizer metrics, we find that none consistently predicts downstream performance across model families, tasks, and languages. This motivates the need for novel tokenizer evaluation metrics and approaches. We propose novel linguistically grounded metric introduced in this work shows that new metrics can capture aspects of tokenizer quality that existing metrics miss, though their predictive power is likely to vary across model families, languages, and tasks.

♻ ☆ KuaFu: Compressing Long User Behavior into Understanding at Billion Scale
Jiahao Hui, Lin Zhu, Ben Tan, Yishen Hu, Jingdong Shu, Zetai Jiang, Xining Ran, Yeshou Cai, Gong Chen, Haijie Gu
Learning user preferences from long behavioral sequences is fundamental to large-scale advertising, recommender systems, and conversational agents. In industrial systems, preference modeling is typically task-wise, with different downstream tasks independently processing the same user history to capture different aspects of preference. This repeated processing creates substantial redundancy at billion-user scale, especially as preferences evolve and representations require frequent updates. We present KuaFu, a unified user preference modeling system that replaces repeated user-level sequence encoding with reusable item-level compressed representations. Each behavioral item is encoded once and shared across users and downstream tasks, while a user's history is built chronologically and extended with new item representations as they arrive, letting KuaFu capture evolving preferences without re-encoding the entire history. To reduce inference and storage overhead, KuaFu introduces a two-axis projector: token-axis compression shortens behavior sequences by up to 15x and reduces each item from 150 KB to 10 KB, while width-axis compression reduces the cached representation to 0.5 KB. We further develop a four-stage training pipeline consisting of reconstruction pre-training, preference-understanding post-training, joint compression-generation training, and hallucination-aware reinforcement learning, progressively improving representation fidelity and downstream utility. Across six MRQA and five RecBench benchmarks, KuaFu consistently outperforms existing compression methods at comparable ratios, with gains of up to 17.7 EM and 1.90 AUC points respectively. KuaFu has been deployed and evaluated on the Tencent advertising and recommendation platform for ten months. Over this period, it improves per-GPU throughput by up to 350%, saves 190 GPUs, and increases overall GMV by 1.37%.

♻ ☆ Detection Is Cheap, Routing Is Learned: Why Refusal-Based Alignment Evaluation Fails
Gregory N. Frank
Current alignment evaluation mostly measures whether models encode dangerous concepts and whether they refuse harmful requests. Both miss the layer where alignment often operates: routing from concept detection to behavioral policy. We study political censorship in Chinese-origin language models as a natural experiment, using probes, surgical ablations, and behavioral tests across nine open-weight models from five labs. Three findings follow. First, probe accuracy alone is non-diagnostic: political probes, null controls, and permutation baselines can all reach 100%, so held-out category generalization is the informative test. Second, surgical ablation reveals lab-specific routing. Removing the political-sensitivity direction eliminates censorship and restores accurate factual output in most models tested, while one model confabulates because its architecture entangles factual knowledge with the censorship mechanism. Cross-model transfer fails, indicating that routing geometry is model- and lab-specific. Third, refusal is no longer the dominant censorship mechanism. Within one model family, hard refusal falls to zero while narrative steering rises to the maximum, making censorship invisible to refusal-only benchmarks. These results support a three-stage descriptive framework: detect, route, generate. Models often retain the relevant knowledge; alignment changes how that knowledge is expressed. Evaluations that audit only detection or refusal therefore miss the routing mechanism that most directly determines behavior.
comment: Code and data: https://github.com/gregfrank/routing-is-learned
♻ ☆ How Alignment Routes: Localizing, Scaling, and Controlling Policy Circuits in Language Models ICML
Gregory N. Frank
Models can represent policy-relevant content yet respond differently. We study the policy-routing circuit that turns detection into refusal. Using political censorship and harmful-request refusal as test cases, four-method circuit analyses in Qwen3-8B, Phi-4-mini, and Gemma-2-2B reveal sparse gate-amplifier circuits at architecture-specific locations. An intermediate-layer gate initiates routing, while later, distributed amplifier heads strengthen a refusal-directed signal. The gate is hard to find with direct logit attribution (DLA): in Qwen3-8B, it contributes less than 1% of output DLA. We therefore combine DLA screening, directional intervention, activation interchange, and gate-to-amplifier knockout. Activation interchange identifies upstream trigger candidates, and knockout suppresses a majority of tested later-head contributions in all three models. The gate is an upstream trigger, not a self-contained refusal mechanism; later amplifiers strengthen the signal toward the output. Candidate-head screens in nine additional models from six labs, spanning 2B-72B parameters, nominate similarly localized routing candidates without establishing head-level homology. Policy routing is a sparse, causally structured interface between content detection and aligned behavior that output attribution alone can miss.

♻ ☆ Dynamic Model Merging Made Slim NeurIPS 2026
Guodong Du, Wanyu Lin
Model merging consolidates independently fine-tuned checkpoints without joint training or access to task data. Dynamic methods retain task-specific capacity, but must divide a limited storage budget between shared and expert parameters. We propose DiDi-Merging, which jointly allocates low-rank capacity to these components through differentiable rank selection, followed by data-free parameter-space refinement. On the eight-task ViT-B/32 benchmark, the small configuration reaches 89.3% average accuracy (98.7% normalized retention) at 1.24x the storage of one backbone, including that backbone; the medium configuration reaches 89.8% at 1.4x. Removing refinement reduces the medium configuration by 4.3 percentage points, while static-merge plus uniform-recovery controls are 4.7-8.9 points below the small configuration. Additional evaluations cover language and multimodal models at their separately reported budgets. The main results assume known task identity; practical routing and accuracy under larger task counts at fixed capacity remain distinct evaluation questions. Code is available at https://github.com/WanyuGroup/NeurIPS2026-DiDi-Merging.

♻ ☆ Rethinking How to Remember: Beyond Atomic Facts in Lifelong LLM Agent Memory NeurIPS 2026
Jingwei Sun, Jianing Zhu, Jiangchao Yao, Tongliang Liu, Bo Han
To enable reliable long-term interaction, LLM agents require a memory system that can faithfully store, efficiently retrieve, and deeply reason over accumulated dialogue history. Most existing methods adopt an extracted fact based paradigm: handcrafted static prompts compress raw dialogues into atomic facts, which are then stored, matched, and injected into downstream reasoning. Nevertheless, such fact-centric designs inevitably discard fine-grained details in original dialogues and fail to support deep reasoning over scattered isolated facts. Moreover, static prompts cannot maintain consistent extraction granularity across diverse dialogue styles. To address these limitations, we propose TriMem, which maintains three coexisting representation granularities, including raw dialogue segments anchored by source identifiers for storage fidelity, extracted atomic facts for efficient memory retrieval, synthesized profiles that aggregate dispersed facts into holistic semantic understanding for deep reasoning. We further adopt TextGrad-based prompt optimization, which iteratively refines extraction and profiling prompts via response quality feedback, achieving lifelong evolution without any parameter updating. Extensive experiments on LoCoMo and PerLTQA across multiple LLM backbones demonstrate that TriMem consistently outperforms strong memory baselines. The code is available at https://TMLR-TriMem.github.io .
comment: Accepted by NeurIPS 2026
♻ ☆ An Empirical Study of Development-Tuned Representation Sensitivity for Medical QA Data Selection NeurIPS 2026
Yuexin Wu, Dayou Yu, Vasile Rus
We study medical question-answering (QA) data selection when a developer already has questions, labels, and auxiliary explanatory texts but can retain only $B$ examples for a final LoRA update. Representation-Sensitivity Prioritization (RSP) first restricts the candidate bank to its top-$4B$ predictive-entropy examples, then combines the frequency of answer flips with non-flipping contraction of the top-two answer margin under hidden-state perturbations. We evaluate this operational score on five medical QA datasets with google/medgemma-4b-it, $B=128$, and three seed branches. After removing four MedExpQA test questions that also occur in its acquisition pool, dataset-specific development-tuned RSP attains the highest observed five-dataset mean accuracy (53.44%) and Macro-F1 (48.21%) among the evaluated selectors. Relative to random selection within the same entropy-restricted pool, the descriptive differences are +1.49 and +2.10 percentage points, respectively; paired 95% bootstrap intervals include zero. The audit mixes benchmarks with different relationships to documented backbone post-training sources, and the same test cohorts had been used in earlier analyses before the expanded tuning grid was designed. Results are therefore exploratory, modest, and dataset-dependent rather than evidence of statistical superiority. RSP point estimates also exceed an archived no-update reference, but unpinned model revisions and incomplete historical input identifiers limit attribution of that difference to fine-tuning. Because every selected training example includes auxiliary text and no matched answer-only control is evaluated, the experiments also do not identify a causal benefit from auxiliary-text conditioning itself.

♻ ☆ Beyond Invariance: Factor-wise Selective Treatment of Shifts for Multimodal Learning
Guimin Hu, Qiwei Peng, Boyan Xu, Ruichu Cai
Distribution shifts pose a fundamental challenge to robust multimodal learning. Existing methods typically promote invariance by suppressing environment-dependent variations, implicitly applying a uniform treatment to shifted information. However, shifted information can have different predictive consequences: some contributes little to prediction, some remains reliably informative, and some exhibits environment-dependent predictive effects. This raises a fundamental question: which shifted information should be suppressed, preserved, or attenuated? To address this question, we propose FaShift, a factor-wise shift framework that diagnoses latent representation factors according to their distributional changes, intervention effects, and cross-environment stability. By factorizing multimodal representations and evaluating these complementary properties, FaShift distinguishes non-predictive factors, stably predictive factors, and predictively useful but unstable factors. It then selectively suppresses, preserves, or attenuates their contributions, rather than enforcing uniform invariance. Experiments on MOSI, MOSEI, and CH-SIMS show that FaShift achieves the best ID accuracy and F1 across all three datasets. Compared with the strongest competitors reporting OOD results, it improves OOD accuracy/F1 by 1.5/3.6 points on MOSI and 1.7/2.7 points on MOSEI, while achieving the best reported OOD accuracy and F1 on CH-SIMS.
♻ ☆ The Shape of Wisdom: How Language Models Converge on an Answer
Shailesh Rana
When a language model answers a factual question, where does its internal state go? We asked four open models (Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-7B, Gemma-2-9B and Llama-3.2-1B) 2,256 factual questions whose answers recur, such as the country a person is a citizen of. At every layer, we recorded the state from which the answer is produced. Questions that receive the same answer converge on a shared region of state space, which we summarise by its centre, the answer centroid. The answer becomes readable within a single layer. In every model, the share of questions whose state lies nearest its own answer's centroid jumps at one layer, between 47% and 71% of the way through the network. The centroids follow the answer the model gives rather than the question: the centroid of a country learned from questions about people's citizenship also identifies that country in questions about towns and films. The model also uses the centroids. After the jump, moving the state of a wrongly answered question to the right answer's centroid makes the model give the right answer in 83%-97% of cases late in the network, against 2%-4% for a random move of the same size. Before the jump, moves of the same size almost never work. Near misses, in which the model answers wrongly but picks the right option in multiple choice, can be seen from the onset layer, where the right answer's centroid is already among the nearest to the state. This signal is about as informative as the right answer's rank in the model's final output, and more informative than the model's confidence in its reply.

♻ ☆ Probe, Don't Prompt: A Hidden-State Probe for Metadata Filtering in Multi-Meta-RAG
Mykhailo Poliakov, Nadiya Shvai
Multi-Meta-RAG improves retrieval for multi-hop question answering by filtering a vector store on metadata, specifically the news source and the publication date, which it extracts from each query using GPT-3.5. This extractor requires a proprietary API call for every query, and its output is not restricted to the allowed sources: even though the prompt specifies 49 sources, GPT-3.5 generates 128 distinct source names, 79 of which do not appear on the list. We show that the source part of this extractor can be replaced by a local, deterministic probe trained on the hidden states of a small open-source language model. The probe is a multi-label logistic classifier on the mean-pooled hidden states of one layer, trained with a class-balanced loss on the gold evidence sources. Using 5-fold cross-validation to select the layer, we evaluate four models ranging from 135M to 1.5B parameters out-of-fold across all 2556 MultiHop-RAG queries. The probe reaches a set-exact accuracy of 90.9%, compared to 88.0% for a substring baseline and 80.9% for GPT-3.5. The difference comes from null queries, for which GPT-3.5 never predicts an empty source set. Across the remaining queries, all three methods achieve set-exact accuracies within 1.1 percentage points of each other. Unlike earlier probing work, we use the probe as a component of a retrieval pipeline rather than for interpretation. For all four models, the cross-validation selects one of the first layers, in contrast to the middle layers reported for retrieval tasks. We attribute this to the source name appearing verbatim in 95.4% of gold query-source pairs. The 135M model is 1.5 percentage points less accurate than the 1.5B model, so the filter requires only a partial forward pass through a small model and one linear layer. The code is available at https://github.com/mxpoliakov/Multi-Meta-RAG.

We introduce Muse Glimmer, an open-source 30B parameter causal language model with perception encoder, purpose-built for autonomous agentic tasks on consumer hardware. Muse Glimmer integrates multi-step reasoning, reliable tool use and failure recovery into a single model that runs locally on consumer devices without requiring cloud infrastructure. The model accepts text and image inputs and produces text outputs, was trained on data from over a hundred languages, and is quantized to approximately 4-bit precision for efficient single-GPU inference. Muse Glimmer achieves state-of-the-art performance for its size class on agentic benchmarks, setting a new standard compared to the models of its size: Gemma4-31B and Qwen3.6-27B on tool use, multi-step planning, and agentic task completion. ♻ ☆ Rethinking Generalization in Knowledge Graph Embedding: An Empirical Study and Sparse Peak Regularization
Linyu Li, Zhi Jin, Yuanpeng He, Dongming Jin, Haoran Duan, Zhengwei Tao, Xuan Zhang, Jiandong Li, Huanyao Zhang, Gadeng Luosang, Nyima Tashi
Knowledge graphs (KGs) provide structured representations of entities and relations, and underpin data management and Web applications such as search, recommendation, and question answering. Since Web-scale KGs such as Freebase and Wikidata are inherently incomplete, knowledge graph embedding (KGE) maps entities and relations into continuous vector spaces and is widely used to infer missing facts through link prediction. Despite extensive progress in KGE model design, the role of regularization has been less systematically examined, especially beyond tensor-decomposition models. The primary contribution of this paper is a comprehensive empirical study of regularization for KGE from the perspective of generalization. Across translation-based, tensor-decomposition-based, GNN-based, temporal, and multimodal KGE models, we show that overfitting is common across model families and that appropriate regularization can substantially improve both generalization and predictive performance. Motivated by these findings, we further propose Sparse Peak Regularization (SPR), a simple and model-agnostic regularizer that introduces rank-based selective sparsity into the regularization target. SPR concentrates the penalty on dominant high-energy embedding components, which contribute most to the embedding norm and overfitting risk, while leaving low-energy components unpenalized so that weak but useful signals are not unnecessarily suppressed. Experiments on eight benchmark datasets and diverse KGE models show that SPR achieves the best results in nearly all settings compared with existing regularizers such as N3, DURA, and ER. These results show that regularization deserves more systematic attention in KGE, and that selective regularization is an effective and broadly applicable strategy for improving the generalization of KGE models.
♻ ☆ LongHisDoc: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Chinese Long Historical Document Understanding
Zeyu Shan, Yongxin Shi, Shihang Wu, Peirong Zhang, Yuanyuan Fang, Zhaopeng Wang, Lianwen Jin
Chinese historical documents are invaluable cultural treasures, but their complexity poses significant comprehension challenges for non-specialists. Despite the success of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) in modern document tasks, their capabilities in Long (full-book) Historical Document understanding remain largely unexplored. To bridge this gap, we introduce LongHisDoc, the first benchmark tailored for long historical document understanding. LongHisDoc comprises 1,012 expert-annotated QA pairs derived from 101 PDF documents, with an average of 85.1 pages and 42,776.4 tokens per document. It spans 10 categories and features four QA types: single-page, cross-page, multi-hop, and unanswerable. Answering these questions requires integrating multimodal evidence from text, layout, and visual elements. Our evaluation reveals that the top-performing LVLM and LLM achieve only 62.9% and 69.9% accuracy, respectively, highlighting a substantial performance gap. Furthermore, we assess various modal retrievers and the end-to-end RAG performance to provide references for Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems. We believe that LongHisDoc serves as a comprehensive benchmark to catalyze future research in full-book historical document understanding. The benchmark and evaluation code are available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/qweq12433454/LongHisDoc and https://github.com/SCUT-DLVCLab/LongHisDoc.
♻ ☆ A Survey of Generative Categories and Techniques in Multimodal Generative Models
Longzhen Han, Awes Mubarak, Almas Baimagambetov, Nikolaos Polatidis, Thar Baker
Multimodal generative models (MGMs) have rapidly expanded beyond text generation to encompass images, music, video, human motion, and 3D objects. This survey categorises six primary text-conditioned generative modalities and examines how four techniques, namely self-supervised learning, mixture of experts, reinforcement learning from human feedback, and chain-of-thought prompting, are adapted across them, distinguishing shared mechanisms from experimentally supported benefits. We organise evaluation around faithfulness, compositionality, and robustness, and interpret evidence within each study's protocol. Selected within-study comparisons reported in the reviewed literature support four findings: preference gains need not improve text relevance, and reward optimisation can reduce within-prompt diversity; intermediate plans require tests of their realisation in final outputs; expert granularity changes the mechanism and its storage, computation, and runtime costs; and gains against an optimisation score need confirmation outside that scoring path. We further analyse trustworthiness risks, including bias, privacy leakage, and misuse of generated media, and examine the evidence for mitigation strategies.

♻ ☆ Same Evidence, Different Answers: Canonical-Context On-Policy Distillation for Multi-Turn Language Models
Zizhuo Lin, Quanling Liu, Jinsheng Quan, Chao Zhang, Yifan Zhu, Xing Shi, Jingtao Xu, Zhihui Li, Yawei Luo
Large language models (LLMs) often solve a task when all instructions are given in a single prompt, but fail when the same information is revealed gradually across turns. When a clean FULL prompt and a RAW-SHARDED conversation contain the same complete user evidence, the model should still arrive at the same answer. We argue that a key reason for this gap is self-anchored drift: responses produced under partial information introduce unsupported assumptions, and those assumptions later distort the final answer. To reduce this effect, we propose Canonical-Context On-Policy Distillation (CCOPD). During training, the same base model is used in two roles: a frozen teacher conditioned on the clean FULL prompt and a trainable student that receives the same evidence incrementally through a multi-turn conversation; CCOPD aligns the student's behavior on its own trajectories with the teacher's canonical full-context behavior. Trained only on math problem conversations, CCOPD yields a 32\% average relative improvement in RAW-SHARDED performance over the original base model across math and five zero-shot out-of-domain task families, while largely preserving full-context performance. Further analyses suggest that CCOPD strengthens grounding in user evidence and reduces sensitivity to contamination from earlier assistant turns.
♻ ☆ MultiAgent-OPD: End-to-End Training of Hierarchical Multi-Agent Systems with On-Policy Distillation
Yuhan Sun, Jiaxin Zhao, Yongdong Luo, Yibo Hu, Meiguang Jin, Junfeng Ma, Weixun Wang, Weihang Pan, Zhongan Bi
In a hierarchical multi-agent system, a Leader assigns tasks and subagents carry them out. Jointly training the Leader and the subagents is difficult because a final answer gives only indirect feedback to the subagents. An LLM judge can score the performance of the subagents, but these scores depend on the judge's own assessment of the quality of subagents' responses. We present MultiAgent-OPD, which combines on-policy distillation with reinforcement learning to jointly train a shared-parameter Leader-Searcher-Answerer team end to end. We first train a Teacher in a Searcher-Answerer workflow using answer correctness. During team training, the frozen Teacher scores student-generated search and summary tokens to provide local process supervision for the Searchers, while the Leader learns from team success and the Answerer from candidate correctness. Across seven datasets, the trained system reaches 38.68% macro avg@4, exceeding Prompted Critic by 4.06 percentage points and C-MAS + RL, the trained cascaded multiagent system, by 2.61 percentage points. The team also solves more questions within four attempts.
♻ ☆ TTCS: Test-Time Curriculum Synthesis for Self-Evolving NeurIPS 2026
Chengyi Yang, Zhishang Xiang, Yunbo Tang, Zongpei Teng, Chengsong Huang, Fei Long, Yuhan Liu, Jinsong Su
Test-Time Training offers a promising way to improve the reasoning ability of large language models (LLMs) by adapting the model using only the test questions. However, existing methods struggle with difficult reasoning problems for two reasons: raw test questions are often too difficult to yield high-quality pseudo-labels, and the limited size of test sets makes continuous online updates prone to instability. To address these limitations, we propose TTCS, a co-evolving test-time training framework. Specifically, TTCS initializes two policies from the same pretrained model: a question synthesizer and a reasoning solver. These policies evolve through iterative optimization: the synthesizer generates progressively challenging question variants conditioned on the test questions, creating a structured curriculum tailored to the solver's current capability, while the solver updates itself using self-consistency rewards computed from multiple sampled responses on both original test and synthetic questions. Crucially, the solver's feedback guides the synthesizer to generate questions aligned with the model's current capability, and the generated question variants in turn stabilize the solver's test-time training. Experiments show that TTCS consistently strengthens the reasoning ability on challenging mathematical benchmarks and transfers to general-domain tasks across different LLM backbones, highlighting a scalable path towards dynamically constructing test-time curricula for self-evolving. Our code and implementation details are available at https://github.com/XMUDeepLIT/TTCS.
comment: NeurIPS 2026 Poster
♻ ☆ When Is 0.1% Enough? Analyzing the Combined Effects of Dimensionality Reduction and Quantization on Text Embedding Compression
Riku Kisako, Hayato Tsukagoshi, Ryohei Sasano
Recent high-performing text embedding models often produce high-dimensional real-valued vectors, resulting in substantial storage and computational costs. To address this issue, embedding compression methods based on dimensionality reduction or quantization have been proposed; however, the effects of their combination have not been sufficiently investigated. In this paper, we systematically investigate how dimensionality reduction and quantization jointly affect embedding compression across four task families in the Massive Text Embedding Benchmark (MTEB), using five pretrained embedding models. Our results show that combining dimensionality reduction and quantization can preserve near-original performance with substantially smaller storage footprints than either method alone. In particular, for classification, several models retain at least 99% of their original classification score using less than 0.1% of the storage required by the original embeddings. The preferred balance between dimensionality and bit-width varies across tasks, with retrieval generally being more sensitive to compression.
